MODELLING OF CRACKED REINFORCED CONCRETE CORROSION IN SERVICE 
ENVIRONMENT

Graduate and Postdoctoral Studies 
In Partial Fulfillment of the Requirements

ii 
ABSTRACT 
Corrosion of reinforced concrete (RC) structures is a complex problem , and corrosion rate 
prediction is a challenging task. The impact of cracking on corrosion rates during the propagation 
phase is influenced by concrete properties and further complicated by moisture content fluctuation, 
chloride ion contamination and carbonation in the cracked reinforced concrete structures. 
Numerical simulation is a versatile tool that allow s an in-depth assessment of corrosion of RC 
structures. However, current numerical models face challenges in describing the corrosion cell of 
rebars in concrete in the presence of spatial variations of microenvironment due to cracking and 
service exposure conditions.  
The objective of this research was to develop a comprehensive mechani stic model for predicting 
the corrosion rate of cracked concrete in realistic service environments. In this model, the 
homogenized corrosion kinetics of rebar were coupled with the evolution of the non -uniform 
microenvironment in concrete caused by crackin g of concrete and varying exposure conditions. 
This objective was achieved by conducting a series of experimental programs and numerical 
simulations. 
The first-phase experimental program quantified the corrosion parameters of rebar such as 
corrosion potential, corrosion current density and Tafel slopes in simulated pore solutions and 
mortar with various chloride content, carbonation, and moisture conditions. A novel two -step 
potentiodynamic test routine was developed, and great care was taken to ensure a realistic rebar 
condition history such as preserving the mill scale, passivation of rebar prior to exposure to the 
corrosive environments, and extended exposure periods.  
The experimental results revealed the synergistic effect of chloride, moisture, and carbonation on 
the corrosion parameters of rebar. It was found that the role of water saturation on corrosion 
includes 1) an area effect, which affects the rebar-pore solution area, 2) an ohmic control effect by 
changing the bulk resistivity, and 3) an anode -to-cathode ratio effect by altering the anodic and 
cathodic site area ratio. These effects complicate the role of chloride and carbonation on rebar 
corrosion in mortar, rather than being solely dep endent on chloride concentration and pH in 
simulated pore solutions. 
\nPDF_PAGE 4\n 
iii 
The first-phase study provided a database, from which the corrosion kinetics model was developed, 
where the homogenized corrosion parameters were empirical functions of chloride content, water 
saturation, and carbonation. In addition, it provided the basis for modelling the corrosion kinetics 
in concrete from corrosion in solution.  
In the modelling phase, a circuit model was developed to simulate the polarization behaviour of a 
rebar experiencing non-uniform surface conditions. It started from localized corrosion kinetics for 
active and passive rebar-concrete units (mesoscopic–macroscopic level) and arrived at a simulated 
scan curve representing the overall performance of the rebar, from w hich the homogenized 
corrosion parameters were derived. The simulation results confirmed the hypothesis that the 
homogenized corrosion parameters are functions of concrete resistivity and the active-to-passive 
rebar-concrete interface area ratio.  
Building upon the circuit model, a simulation approach was developed to derive the homogenized 
corrosion parameters of the rebar -concrete interface (mesoscopic –macroscopic level) from the 
corrosion kinetics of the rebar-solution interface (microscopic pore level). It featured in describing 
the allocation of active and passive rebar-solution corrosion sites in the representative elementary 
volume based on the non-uniform water and chloride distribution in concrete pores. The simulated 
homogenized corrosion kinetics were functions of the total chloride content and water saturation 
of concrete, and they captured the patterns of the empirical functions from the first -phase 
experimental study.  
To simulate the microenvironment evolution at the rebar depth of a concrete s tructure, a 
comprehensive 3-D transport model (environment model) was developed. In the case study of an 
uncracked RC structure — the University Bridge subjected to service conditions with the source 
of chloride, carbonation, varying relative humidity, and  exposure to unevenly sheltered run -off 
water, this model predicted the corrosion rates of century -old rebar in concrete by accurately 
modelling the microenvironment at rebar depth with a realistic depiction of the microclimate as 
well as various speculate d climate scenarios, including elevated CO 2 levels and changes in 
precipitation. 
Finally, a comprehensive corrosion model was formulated by incorporating crack configuration 
into the environment model and coupling it with the corrosion kinetics model. This  model 
\nPDF_PAGE 5\n 
iv 
simulated the transport of species such as water, chloride ion, oxygen, and carbon dioxide in the 
concrete domain and the electrochemical reaction on the rebar -concrete interface and solved for 
the potential and current fields. A second -phase experimental program was designed to validate 
this model, using segmented rebars in cracked concrete specimens subjected to wetting and drying 
cycles with chloride salt solutions. 
The validated model enabled a numerical investigation of the mechanisms behind var ying field 
observations of rebar corrosion in cracked concrete. It explained the complex effects of cracks on 
corrosion, including corrosion reduction over time, crack size and healing effects. 
Under submerged conditions, cracks affect corrosion by affecti ng the diffusion rate of chloride 
ions through the crack. Thinner cracks lead to less chloride influx, resulting in a reduced peak 
corrosion current in the cracking zone. The corrosion rate decrease s over time and eventually 
reaches a similar level limited by oxygen diffusion that is independent of crack size. 
Under wetting and drying conditions, the corrosion kinetics of rebar in the cracking zone are 
affected by chloride and moisture, with the possibility of rebar acting as a cathode during drying 
cycles. The average corrosion rate in the cracking zone peaks during wetting cycles and decreases 
during subsequent drying cycles, with the peak values diminishing with repeated wetting and 
drying cycles. Micro-cell corrosion eventually becomes the primary contributor to rebar corrosion 
in the cracking zone and the rate depend s on the chloride content on the rebar and oxygen 
concentration. 
Crack healing result s in reduced transport properties, dilution of high chloride content near the 
crack, and limited oxygen diffusion. It accelerate s corrosion rate reduction during wetting and 
drying cycles. Less permeable concrete, such as high performance concrete, benefit s more from 
crack healing with greater chloride dilution. The critical crack width below which it is considered 
harmless should be determined based on the effects of the crack on corrosion with specific material 
properties and exposure conditions.

CO-AUTHORSHIP 
All the experimental and numerical modelling work that is presented in this thesis was conducted 
by Gang Li . Moh Boulfiza and Richard Evitts provided valuable input and contributions to the 
analytical work. All the manuscripts included in this thesis are the result of collaborative efforts 
between the authors. 
The author s would like to express their sincere appreciation to the Natural Sciences and 
Engineering Research Council of Canada (NSERC), the City of Saskatoon, Mitacs, and the 
Department of Civil, Geological, and Environmental Engineering at the University of 
Saskatchewan for their financial support of this project.  
The manuscript corresponding to Chapter 5 (The Impact of Non-uniformity and Resistivity on the 
Homogenized Corrosion Parameters of Rebars in Concrete - a Circuit Model Analysis) was 
published in Corrosion Engineering, Science and Technology. The manuscript was reformatted 
and included in the thesis with written permission. It is cited as 
“Li, G., Evitts, R., & Boulfiza, M. (2023). The impact of non-uniformity and resistivity on the 
homogenised corrosion parameters of rebars in concrete – a circuit model analysis. Corrosion 
Engineering, Science and Technology, 58(4), 399–409. 
https://doi.org/10.1080/1478422X.2023.2190442” 
The manuscript corresponding to Chapter 3 (On the Corrosion Parameters Acquired through 
Potentiodynamic Scans of Carbon Steel Rebar  in Simulated Pore Solution and Mortar ) and the 
manuscript corresponding to Chapter 4 (Carbon Steel Rebar Corrosion in Mortar: the Interactive 
Effect of Moisture, Chloride, and Carbonation on Rebar Corrosion ) were  submitted to 
Construction and Building Materials in August 2023. 
The manuscript corresponding to Chapter 7 (Effect of Accurate Microclimate Representation on 
the Corrosion Performance of a Bridge Subjected to Carbonation and Chloride Attack ) was 
submitted to Case Studies in Construction Materials in September 2023. 
Note: The authors declare no competing financial interest and no conflict of interest.

1 
1 Introduction 
1.1 Background 
Reinforced concrete (RC) corrosion damage is a multibillion-dollar problem in Canada and many 
other countries. In 2001, the Federal Highway Administration (2002) reported that the total direct 
cost of corrosion was U.S.$239 billion per year which was 3.2 percent of the U.S. gross domestic 
product (GDP). The corrosion of reinforced concrete structures has become a more urgent problem 
in the last few years with an increasing number of aging RC structures. According to a recent 
report published by Transportation for America (Davis et al., 2013), one in nine bridges in the U.S. 
have sustained a major defect requiring closure, significant maintenance, rehabilitation, or 
replacement of one of the major components. Prediction and early diagnosis of corrosion ha ve 
become very important tasks for construction engineers, repair practitioners, and owners of 
buildings and bridges. It allows preventive action to take place and avoids unnecessary major 
expensive repairs, and hence, a reduced life cycle cost. It would also help policymakers to regulate 
the evaluation criteria and cho ose optimum rehabilitation strategies for RC structures exceeding 
their design service life that do not necessarily need to be replaced.  
Corrosion of RC structures is a complex problem and accurate prediction of the corrosion rate is a 
challenging task. The alkalinity (pH>12.5) of concrete provides a suitable microenvironment to 
generate and maintain a passive layer on steel, which protects the rebar from corrosion. Therefore, 
in the absence of cracks, steel reinforcement in concrete structures usually exhibits a prolonged 
corrosion initiation period. However, the existence of cracks complicates the micro -environment 
in concrete near the rebar which makes the assessment of RC corrosion much mo re difficult. The 
cracks in aged RC structures can occur as a result of many phenomena such as restrained shrinkage, 
thermal shocks, freeze -thaw cycles, and alkali -aggregate reactions, and can also be induced by 
external loadings. In most cases, cracks do not immediately affect the load -carrying capacity of 
the concrete structure, but may adversely affect its durability by providing easier access to oxygen, 
carbon dioxide, water and other aggressive agents such as chloride ions in marine environments 
or from the use of de-icing salts, which allows the corrosion process to initiate earlier and possibly 
propagate much faster than in the case of uncracked concrete.  
\nPDF_PAGE 42\n 
 
2 
Many studies (Arya & Ofori-Darko, 1996; Lu et al., 2017; Pettersson et al., 1996) have found that 
the effects of cracking on corrosion rate in the propagation period are affected by the properties of 
cracks, such as crack width, crack frequency, and the properties of concrete, and cover depths. 
Moreover, these effects may be further complicated by the service environment. For example, 
under various exposure conditions, there exists a different critical crack width below which the 
effect of the crack is negligible. Therefore, the empirical corrosion model drawn from experiments 
is only limited to the  application of similar scenarios: similar concrete structure layout, similar 
concrete quality, and similar exposure conditions. Given the interactive multi-factor nature of the 
development of the problem, a numerical model that can simulate the transport phenomena and 
electrochemical processes of the cracked RC structures seems to be a promising way to capture 
the necessary complication. Such a versatile mechanistic model can then be used to assess the 
corrosion of cracked concrete in various service envir onments. However, the currently available 
numerical models still suffer from serious limitations to capture the spatial variations of corrosion 
in cracked concrete in their service environment.  
1.2 Limitations in current corrosion models 
Not being limited by the practical constraints of the field assessment, numerical simulation is a 
versatile tool that allows a more in-depth assessment of corrosion in RC structures.  Over the past 
couple of decades, numerous studies have been carried out to develop models for  assessing 
corrosion rates in RC structures.  
Empirical models (Alonso et al., 1988; DURACRETE, 1999; Liu & Weyers, 1998; Vu et al., 2005) 
or closed-form regression equations from numerical models (Pour-Ghaz et al., 2009a; Yu et al., 
2014) correlate the corrosion rate with corrosion affecting factors such as water/cement ratio, 
concrete resistivity, moisture content, carbonation degree, cover thickness, temperature etc. The 
empirical models reveal how the material properties and environmental factors affect the corrosion 
rate. However, these models are only close to their experimental setup conditions. The limitations 
could be found when applied to a different corrosive environment or different corrosion patterns 
such as non-uniform corrosion in cracked concrete under wetting and drying conditions: the fitted 
model parameters lack physical meaning, and thus cannot be properly selected.  
\nPDF_PAGE 43\n 
 
3 
Other mechanistic numerical models (Ghods et al., 2007; Isgor & Razaqpur, 2006; Kim & Kim, 
2008; Kranc et al., 1992; Muthulingam & Rao, 2015; Sohail, 2013; Yu, Liu, & Li, 2017; Zacaruk, 
2013)(Ghods et al., 2007; Isgor & Razaqpur, 2006; Kim & Kim, 2008; Kranc et al., 1992; Sohail, 
2013; Yu, Liu, & Li, 2017; Zacaruk, 2013)  are based on solving the electrochem ical electrode 
process on a rebar -concrete interface and the current distribution in a concrete domain. The 
electrochemical process simulation may be coupled with the solution of transport of oxygen, 
moisture, and chloride ions in concrete. The model param eters include the electrochemical 
properties of steel and the transport properties of concrete. Although these electrochemistry-based 
numerical models have rationally defined parameters, the guidance of parameter selection is 
lacking; usually, a large scatter of values is used among different researchers and both parameters 
from solution and concrete have been used. 
However, there are various drawbacks and limitations of the current models that prevent their 
application in a more realistic situation: non -uniform corrosion of cracked/uncracked RC 
structures under different environmental conditions. 
Firstly, the corrosion kinetics are usually taken as constant although it was observed that in 
simulated pore solution, it is a function of pH value, chloride cont ent and oxygen content. There 
are some attempts to empirically correlate the corrosion parameters with chloride concentration 
anodic Tafel slope and anodic equilibrium potential (Maekawa et al., 2008)  due to the anodic 
control assumption  (Glass et al., 1 991) or by making critical chloride content independent of 
anodic Tafel slope (Sagüés et al., 2008) or by relating equilibrium potential with the concentration 
of reacting ions such as OH−, Fe2+ , oxygen content by Nernst’s equation (Isgor & Razaqpur, 
2006). However, the dependence of these critical corrosion parameters (i.e., equilibrium potential, 
exchange current density, Tafel slope) on the chemical environment is usually not explicitly 
represented. 
Secondly, more often than not, the role of satur ation is not properly modelled. The experimental 
observation suggests that the corrosion rate increases with increasing water saturation until the 
cathodic current reaches a maximum value due to the limiting transport of dissolved oxygen. In 
most corrosion models, saturation does not contribute to the rebar-concrete corrosion kinetics but 
is usually only reflected as the change in bulk resistivity, which then translates to the resistance 
\nPDF_PAGE 44\n 
 
4 
between anodes and cathodes. However, in macro-cell corrosion models, an increase in resistivity 
will only affect the “macro-cell” current by increasing the resistance between anodes and cathodes. 
However, the reduction of corrosion rate due to water saturation could be explained in two ways: 
(1) resistance in the corrosion cell circuit (micro- and macro-) controls the corrosion rate; (2) the 
overall corrosion rate on the rebar-concrete interface is affected by the actual rebar -pore solution 
interface area, which is the effective corroding surface. There is little research on the modelling of 
the above-mentioned mechanism regarding the corrosion-saturation relationship. 
Thirdly, the corrosion kinetics are typically assigned to questionable pre -defined anodes and 
cathodes (macro-cell corrosion model) as input information, which limits the application of these 
models to problems where anodic site and cathodes are separated, such as chloride-induced pitting 
corrosion in cracked concrete with the assumed sized anode in the cracking areas and the rest areas 
as the cathode to couple with. Although in some cases, the allocation of anode and cathode is 
determined by certain threshold criteria such as pH, and chloride ion concentration, this treatment 
of the corrosion cell is not suitable in more uniformly chloride -contaminated concrete or 
carbonation-induced concrete where rebar is not strongly polarized to discrete anodic and cathodic 
sites. Such scenarios may exist after the carbonation or contamination of chloride propagates 
around the cracks for an extended period in aged structures.  More importantly, there is no clear 
cut-off between these conditions.  
Finally, the effect of realistic service exposure conditions as boundary conditions is rarely included. 
The micro-environment of concrete around the crack will be greatly affected by weather conditions 
such as ambient relative humidity, temperature, precipitation, and other factors such as the 
seasonal application of de -icing salts. Although the chloride diffusion, carbonation and moisture 
transport in concrete were extensively simulated in numerical models, few corrosion models have 
incorporated the evolving microenvironment around the rebar from these models. In current 
models, the microenvironment around the rebar is typically described by a chloride diffusion 
problem with a constant chloride concentration on the surface of RC  (Cao, 2014). The resultant 
chloride content is merely used to trigger the assignment of discrete anodes and cathodes. 
\nPDF_PAGE 45\n 
 
5 
1.3 Statement of the research question and significance 
A comprehensive mechanistic prediction m odel is developed to predict the corrosion rate of 
cracked concrete under different service environments. In this model, rational and measurable 
electrochemical properties of rebar in solution are to be used to develop homogenized corrosion 
kinetics (both anodic and cathodic) at every point on the rebar surface with surrounding concrete 
in different non-uniform micro-environment caused by cracking of concrete and varying exposure 
conditions. The electrochemical processes are coupled with the evolution of th e micro -
environment. 
This research improves our understanding of the corrosion kinetics of rebar in cracked concrete 
and the comprehensive model provides an objective and quantitative assessment of the real-world 
corrosion problem in RC structures with/wit hout cracking, under different weather and climate 
conditions. An accurate assessment of the corrosion performance of a given RC structure would 
help the engineers and property owners to make informed decisions on the frequency of inspection 
of existing st ructures, rehabilitation timelines and strategies. The inclusion of region -dependent 
weather and climate data will help policymakers to improve their local structural 
design/construction code, for example, maximum crack length/width allowed, minimum cover 
depth, concrete types etc. and municipal policies such as the amount of the application of de-icing 
salts. 
1.4 Objectives 
The main objective of this research is to develop a comprehensive mechanistic corrosion model 
for rebar corrosion in non-uniform corrosive microenvironments caused by cracking and exposure 
conditions. These sub-objectives include  
1) to investigate the synergistic effect of chloride, moisture, and carbonation on the corrosion 
parameters of rebar in concrete. 
 
2) to develop a model that describes the homogenized rebar corrosion parameters in concrete 
as a function of the variable microenvironment (moisture level, chloride concentration, 
carbonation state) 
 
3) to develop a model that simulates the microenvironment evolution at the rebar depth of 
concrete structure in its service conditions that involves sources of chloride , carbonation 
and moisture fluctuation. 
\nPDF_PAGE 46\n 
 
6 
 
4) to integrate the homogenized rebar corrosion kinetics model and the microenvironment 
evolution model into a comprehensive corrosion model. 
 
5) to validate the comprehensive corrosion model with the experimental corrosion test of 
cracked beam samples exposed to wetting and drying cycles with chloride salt solution.  
 
6) Utilize the validated comprehensive model to explore the intricate relationship b etween 
cracks, microenvironmental factors, and rebar corrosion, with particular emphasis on 
addressing some controversial aspects in the literature. This includes investigating the 
relative importance of microcell versus macrocell corrosion, examining corr osion 
reduction over time, and analyzing the effect of crack size (opening) on corrosion.  
 
1.5 Methodology and scope 
The main goal of this project is to model the rebar corrosion in cracked/uncracked RC structures 
under realistic service exposure conditions.  The core of the research question is to understand the 
realistic corrosion kinetics of the rebar -concrete interface under varying conditions of moisture, 
chloride, and carbonation and make it applicable in large -scale FEM models.  To break it down, 
one needs to know the effect of the pore solution composition on the corrosion kinetics of the 
rebar-solution interface, and how moisture level —one distinctive characteristic brought by 
concrete’s pore system, affects this behaviour at a representative elementar y surface area of the 
rebar-concrete interface.   
These objectives of this study were achieved by a combination of laboratory experiments and 
numerical modelling.  
The laboratory tests were used to study the synergistic effects of moisture, chloride, and 
carbonation on the rebar corrosion in both simulated pore solution and mortar. 
The laboratory experiments were divided into two phases. Phase one consists of the concrete 
property tests and electrochemical tests of rebar corrosion. The concrete properties i nclude 
porosity, chloride diffusivity, carbonation resistance and water retention property, and resistivity.  
Knowledge of these properties was used to impose a controlled corrosive environment in the 
reinforced concrete samples that involve varying moistu re levels, chloride concentration, and 
carbonation state. The electrochemical tests are then carried out to test the corrosion parameters of 
the embedded rebar in those formulated conditions. With the cause —controlled corrosion 
\nPDF_PAGE 47\n 
 
7 
microenvironment condition in concrete and the response—tested rebar corrosion kinetics, we may 
analyze the role of moisture, chloride, carbonation, and resistivity in contributing to the corrosion 
of the rebar -concrete interface. Phase two is a parallel validation test. It tests the  corrosion 
condition of the cracked beams under controlled conditions and validation the performance of the 
developed comprehensive corrosion model. 
The numerical modelling part aims at developing the comprehensive mechanistic corrosion model 
that simulates the evolution of moisture, chloride, and carbonation in the cracked concrete under 
the natural-like exposure condition and the electrochemical processes taking place on the rebar 
exposed to the non -uniform microenvironment. This comprehensive model can b e broken down 
into three parts: 
“Environment model”; a mass transfer model that simulates the evolution of micro -environment 
in uncracked concrete under service exposure conditions, which then can be extended to 
incorporate the effects of cracking.  In thi s part, data analysis of historical meteorological 
information, field monitoring and laboratory testing of moisture, chloride, and carbonation 
evolution in concrete structures exposed to natural and simulated environments were used to come 
up with realistic boundary conditions. 
“Corrosion kinetics model”; a model that describes the homogenized corrosion kinetics for rebar 
in concrete. This homogenized model is essentially dependent on the microenvironment near the 
rebar while treating the rebar -concrete interface as a continuum, and hence can be applied to the 
whole rebar surface without having to specify discrete anodic and cathodic areas. The 
homogenized corrosion kinetics provides a link between the rebar corrosion at the non -uniform 
rebar-pore solution i nterface and the overall corrosion behaviour at the rebar -concrete interface. 
The corrosion kinetics of the rebar -concrete is not just a quantity like the corrosion rate, it also 
contains the polarization behaviour or in other words the dependence of the c orrosion rate on the 
overpotential distribution in the representative elementary rebar -concrete interface.  Therefore, 
such kinetics, classically-described by the Butler-Volmer equation, cannot be acquired by simply 
volume-averaging or homogenization. In t his paper, this homogenization was done through 
numerical modelling of the corrosion kinetics of rebar embedded in concrete at a microscopic level 
starting from the corrosion kinetics in pore solution. This will provide a numerical method of 
\nPDF_PAGE 48\n 
 
8 
acquiring electrochemical parameters of rebar in the concrete of different saturation and different 
pore solution composition.  At the same time, the empirical homogenized corrosion kinetics can 
also be derived from the laboratory corrosion test of the reinforced concre te samples under 
controlled conditions.  
In the comprehensive corrosion model, the homogenized corrosion kinetics will be used to 
accommodate both micro- and macro-corrosion, so that both micro-cell and macro-cell corrosion 
kinetics are dependent on the varying concrete conditions. This will enable the corrosion modelling 
rebar exposed to microenvironments with spatial variations caused by the existence of cracks, 
which could not be properly done by using separated pure anodes and cathodes.  
The RC structures studied in this research do not include RC structures with surface treatments 
(such as coating, and fibre-reinforced polymer wrapping) and the rebar is not subject to cathodic 
protection (galvanic protection and/or impressed current prote ction) or stray current.  The steel 
reinforcement is regular carbon steel; stainless steel or galvanized rebar is not considered. 
1.6 Organization of chapters 
Chapter 1 is the introduction to the project. It presents the background of the corrosion of 
reinforced concrete structures and points out the limitations of current corrosion models. The 
research objectives and the methodologies are then proposed to target these limitations.  
Chapter 2 reviews the existing literature related to the non -uniform corrosion phenomenon in 
reinforced concrete structures in detail. It covers the experimental observation of the behaviour of 
rebar corrosion in cracked concrete and the corrosion modelling methods. The electrochemical 
basics of the rebar corrosion cell were revisite d. Different treatments of the corrosion cell in the 
numerical models were critically discussed. It also reviews the modelling of moisture transport, 
chloride penetration, and carbonation in concrete that affect the corrosive microenvironment on 
the rebar. 
Chapter 3 and Chapter 4 present the experimental studies on the corrosion parameters of the carbon 
steel rebar in mortar samples and simulated pore solutions. The synergistic effects of chloride, 
carbonation and water on rebar corrosion were investigated.  
\nPDF_PAGE 49\n 
 
9 
Chapter 3 presents an improved three-electrode potentiodynamic test performed on the as-received 
carbon steel rebar in four media: simulated pore solution, carbonated simulated pore solution, non-
carbonated mortar, and carbonated mortar. There are six levels of chloride contamination and two 
levels of carbonation state for both rebar in simulated pore solution and mortar. The carbon steel 
rebar corrosion parameters, including corrosion potential, corrosion current density, and Tafel 
slopes were measured. The effects of chloride and carbonation on corrosion were compared 
between mortar samples and simulated pore solution. 
Chapter 4 covers an in-depth analysis of the results of the corrosion parameters from mortars with 
various chloride content, carbonation state and water content alongside their water retention curves 
and bulk resistivity. It discusses the roles of water saturation on corrosion concluded as 1) an area 
effect by the influenced rebar-pore solution area, 2) an ohmic control effect by the influenced bulk 
resistivity, and 3) an anode-to-cathode ratio effect. The corrosion kinetics parameters are described 
as empirical functions of the microenvironmental conditions. 
Chapter 5 and Chapter 6 use the modelling method to explore how the overall corrosion parameters 
are related to the non -uniform corrosion parameters at mesoscopic –macroscopic level and 
microscopic level.  
Chapter 5 presents the concept of the homogenized corrosion parameters of rebar in concrete with 
non-uniform surface conditions with a  circuit model. This model starts from localized corrosion 
kinetics for active and passive rebar-concrete units (mesoscopic–macroscopic level) described by 
the Butler -Volmer equation and arrives at the simulated scan curve representing the overall 
performance of the rebar, from which, the homogenized corrosion parameters can be derived. It 
explored the validity of describing the overall scan curve with the Butler -Volmer equation, and 
how much deviation can be caused by the non -uniformity of rebar condition and concrete 
resistivity.  
Chapter 6 presents a modelling approach to simulate the activation -controlled corrosion kinetics 
of the rebar-concrete interface (mesoscopic–macroscopic level) from the corrosion kinetics of the 
rebar-solution interface (microscopic pore level). The proposed model describes the area of active 
and passive rebar -solution corrosion sites at the representative elementary volume based on the 
\nPDF_PAGE 50\n 
 
10 
non-uniform water and chloride distribution in the concrete. The simulated homogenized corrosion 
kinetics of rebar in concrete as derived from this model are functions of the total chloride content 
and water saturation of concrete, similar to the empirical functions outlined in Chapter 4. These 
corrosion kinetics can be applied to every point on the rebar surface with surrounding concrete of 
non-uniform microenvironment caused by cracking of concrete and varying exposure conditions.  
Chapter 7 presents a comprehensive 3 -D transport model that simulates the microenvironment 
evolution at the rebar depth for a reinforced concrete structure. This model was applied to an old 
concrete arch bridge subjected to service conditions with the sour ce of chloride, carbonation, 
varying air relative humidity, and exposure to unevenly sheltered run -off water. The model uses 
the bridge inspection data, weather data, moisture and oxygen content in concrete monitored by 
in-situ installed sensors to predict the microenvironment in the concrete arch. Results for the past 
100 years of service showed good agreement with the bridge's current condition. Simulations for 
the next 50 years under different climate scenarios, such as elevated CO2 level and change in 
precipitation, reveal implications of climate change for rebar section loss in the arch. 
Chapter 8 presents a comprehensive corrosion model that simulates the evolution of moisture and 
chloride content on embedded rebars in cracked concrete. The model utilizes corrosion kinetics as 
functions of the microenvironmental conditions on the embedded  rebar to solve the potential and 
current fields in the concrete domain and rebar surface. An experimental program was developed 
to quantify material properties, and a corrosion test of segmented rebars in cracked concrete under 
wetting and drying cycles was employed to validate the model.  
Chapter 9 presents  a numerical exploration of the potential mechanisms causing the marked 
differences in observations of rebar corrosion in cracked concrete. It explains the complex effect 
of cracks on observed corrosion , corrosion reduction over time, and the effect of crack size and 
crack healing. 
Finally, Chapter 10 concludes the main findings of this work and recommendations for future work. 
 
 
\nPDF_PAGE 51\n 
 
11 
2 Literature Review  
 
In this chapter, a critical review of the published literature on the corrosion of cracked reinforced 
concrete is provided. Firstly, the experimental and field observation of non -uniform corrosion in 
cracked concrete will be summarized. Secondly, various numerical treatments of the corrosion cell 
will be critically reviewed. Thirdly, special attention will be paid to the modelling of the corrosive 
micro-environment affected by the natural exposure conditions. 
2.1 Non-uniform corrosion in cracked concrete 
Steel reinforcement embedded in concrete is protected from corrosion by both physical and 
chemical barriers. Concrete contains a highly alkaline pore solution (pH 12 –13) within its 
hardened cement paste. The high alkalinity of the pore soluti on is due to the presence of sodium, 
potassium and calcium hydroxides produced by the hydration of cement particles with mixing 
water. In this alkaline environment, a protective iron oxide film forms and maintains the steel bars 
in a passive condition with  a very low corrosion rate. This protected state of steel in concrete 
against corrosion, known as passivity, requires a minimum pH value of approximately 11.5 to be 
maintained. The quality and the thickness of concrete cover have been shown to affect the stability 
of this passive layer around the reinforcement. The loss of passivity is often a result of the loss of 
alkalinity of the surrounding concrete due to carbonation or the presence of chloride ions. Cracks 
in concrete serve as fast pathways for the in gress of chloride onto the rebar. When the chloride 
content on the rebar reaches a critical value, and/or the pH value drops to a certain value, the 
protective film will be destroyed, which will cause the onset of active corrosion.   
Non-uniform corrosion will take place wherever there is a non -uniform microenvironment. Non-
uniform corrosion can be observed both around the circumference of the rebar and along the rebar. 
In aged RC structures, where adequate chloride penetration and/or carbonation front has just 
reached the rebar through an uncracked concrete cover, more corrosion products are found on the 
rebar surface closer to the cover surface (Ji et al., 2013; Muthulingam & Rao, 2014, 2015) . In 
cracked RC structures, higher corrosion rates should be expected in the cracking areas affected by 
chloride penetration and/or carbonation. However, experimental observations have shown that this 
is not necessarily the case (i.e., the cracks may not c ause a high corrosion rate), and hence 
\nPDF_PAGE 52\n 
 
12 
reinforcement corrosion in cracked concrete is more complex than corrosion in the absence of 
cracks. Although a certain number of previous studies (Alekseev et al., 1993; Lu et al., 2017; 
Pettersson et al., 1996; Schießl & Raupach, 1997; Subramaniam & Bi, 2010; Wilkins & Lawrence, 
1980) have focused on the effect of cracking on steel reinforcement corrosion in concrete, this area 
still remains relatively unexplored, despite its practical importance since it is virtually impossible 
to eliminate cracking in real structures. Currently, there is a lack of clear understanding of the 
corrosion process in such situations and how the properties of the surrounding concrete affect the 
rate of local metal loss at the crack location. A review of the current state of knowledge in this area 
is provided in the following sections. 
2.1.1 Effect of cracks on corrosion - experimental observation  
The most significant effect of concrete cracking on rebar corrosion is the formation of a macro -
cell that may significantly increase the corrosion rate. It has been observed that passive 
reinforcement in the non -cracked part of an RC structure and bare steel can act as effective 
cathodes if there is galvanic contact to the reinforcement at the cracks a nd when these cathodic 
areas have sufficient oxygen supply. This is typically the case, for cathodic areas located in the 
splash zone or exposed to air where the corrosion around cracks can be accelerated due to macro-
cell corrosion. However, in submerged conditions, the cathodic oxygen reduction reaction is 
limited by the transport of dissolved oxygen to the rebar surface (Hussain, 2011). It was found that 
the main effect of macro cracks in submerged concrete is a reduction of the initiation time. Once 
the active rebar corrosion was initiated, the corrosion rate started high, but rapidly decreased and 
approached the active corrosion rate typically found in uncracked concrete (Alekseev et al., 1993). 
According to Wilkins & Lawrence (1980), the cross-sectional static cracks, even with up to 1 mm 
crack width, would not lead to corrosion at a significant level in the case of submerged concrete.  
The effect of cracks on the corrosion rate in cracked concrete could also depend on the crack width 
and concrete type. For high performance concrete in partially submerged conditions (splash zone), 
it has been observed that although the chloride threshold level and the initiation time may be close 
to zero for crack widths up to 0.8 mm and a concrete cover of 30 mm, the active corrosion rate 
will become so small that it may be considered as practically harmless (Pettersson et al., 1996). In 
a recent study (Lu et al., 2017), the effects of transverse cracks on chloride-induced rebar corrosion 
\nPDF_PAGE 53\n 
 
13 
under wetting and dryi ng cycles were found to depend on both the cover depth and crack 
parameters such as width and frequency/spacing. The corrosion rate increases more rapidly with 
increasing crack width and frequency and decreasing cover depth. Under various cover depth and 
crack frequency scenarios, it has been observed that there exists a certain critical crack width below 
which the effect of the crack is negligible. However, the reasons for the existence of that critical 
width and its dependence on exposure conditions and the crack parameters are not clear. 
Two different mechanisms of corrosion, the micro-cell and the macro-cell have been identified in 
cracked concrete specimens (Subramaniam & Bi, 2010). The two mechanisms primarily differ in 
the length scales and the phys ical distance between the underlying electrochemical reactions. In 
the microcell mechanism, the anodic and cathodic reaction sites are microscopic and are located 
adjacent to each other. In macro-cell corrosion, the anode and cathode are spatially separated by a 
finite macroscopic distance resulting in a flow of electric charges over a physical distance. In the 
case of chloride-induced corrosion, the anodic reaction is localized, confined to the area of steel 
where the chloride threshold level for depassiva tion is exceeded. The cathodic reaction may 
significantly occur in all areas that are not permanently water -saturated and cathodic current 
density decreases as the distance from the crack increases (Raupach, 1996) . That explains the 
experimental observation that crack frequency could be identified as a governing factor in the rate 
of corrosion (Arya & Ofori-Darko, 1996), as it enables more passive areas near those cracks to be 
polarized to its cathodic direction and hence larger total cathodic current. Poursaee et al. (2010)  
studied corrosion of multi-rebar mortar samples subject to various types of de-icing salt solutions, 
where an active rebar near the surface was subject to the solution and the other three were at a 
deeper location. It was found the micro and macro corrosion rates were similar. However, another 
similar test (Hansson et al., 2006) suggested that macro-cell corrosion is the main mechanism of 
rebar corrosion in ordinary corrosion. Although these tests are not in cracked concrete, these 
findings could suggest that the contributions from micro-cell and macro-cell depend on the area of 
active rebar and passive rebar and their arrangement. The contribution of micro -cell corrosion 
could be the dominant mechanism when the active rebar area is comparable to the pass ive rebar 
and the distance between these two are large.  
In conclusion, most of the previous research on corrosion in cracked concrete has been limited to 
measuring either potentials or corrosion rates in either field structures (submerged or splashed 
\nPDF_PAGE 54\n 
 
14 
zone) or preconditioned samples (often subjected to wetting/drying cycles) in the lab but often 
without monitoring of the distribution around the crack of any of the environmental parameters 
that can affect the corrosion process, such as water saturation, oxyg en, and chloride. Under 
different exposure conditions, the properties of cracks such as crack width, crack frequency, the 
properties of concrete, and cover depths would have very different effects on the corrosion rate. A 
clear understanding of the relativ e magnitudes of the microcell and macro -cell mechanisms in a 
corroding system and what creates the conditions for any of the two to become the dominant 
mechanism is still currently not available. The role of each mechanism, its contribution to the total 
corrosion rate and its dependence on the local microenvironments (moisture, oxygen, and chloride) 
created by the very existence of cracks are not clear yet. 
2.1.2 Numerical modelling of corrosion 
In the last few decades, corrosion of rebar in concrete has been ext ensively studied. Numerous 
studies have been focusing on the factors that affect the rate of rebar in concrete, such as water -
cement ratio, cover thickness, moisture content, chloride content, pH value, oxygen availability 
etc. A variety of numerical models were proposed to predict the corrosion rate in the propagation 
phase and mechanical behaviour of reinforced concrete structures under different exposure 
conditions (Otieno et al., 2012) . Despite different levels of complexity, direct modelling of the 
electrochemical process of rebar embedded in concrete remains the most versatile way to calculate 
the corrosion current, and hence the rate of loss of metal.   
2.1.2.1 Basics of corrosion kinetics 
When modelling corrosion in reinforced concrete, concrete is usually  treated as a continuous 
homogeneous material, where the corrosion kinetics are generally modelled by the Butler-Volmer 
equation or by the Tafel equation for larger over -potentials, and their variations incorporating 
concentration polarization.  
Although c orrosion is not a reversible process, it is worth mentioning that in some literature, 
occasionally, terms such as equilibrium potentials (reversible potential of half-cell reactions: iron 
dissolution and oxygen reduction) and mixed potential (corrosion potential of coupled full reaction) 
are used interchangeably, and accordingly exchange current density and corrosion current density. 
\nPDF_PAGE 55\n 
 
15 
The confusing term usage could be due to the fact the Butler-Volmer equation when applied to the 
single electrode and mixed electrode, the mathematical expression takes the same form (Tan, 2012):

Despite the similarity of the Butler -Volmer equation when applied to a single electrode with a 
reversible half-cell reaction and when applied to an electrode with a coupled reaction,  there is a 
distinct difference in the meaning of the net current density calculated. Taking the corrosion of 
steel as an example: in a reversible half-cell reaction (Figure 2.1a), at the reversible potential, 𝐸0,𝐹𝑒, 
the exchange current, 𝑖0,𝐹𝑒 has no contribution to the change of iron (dissolution and deposition at 
the same rate).  When the potential is polarized to 𝐸, the net current, 𝑖𝑛𝑒𝑡 represents the to tal 
change of iron as dissolution as shown in Figure 2.1a. However, in a coupled full reaction (Figure 
2.1b), at the mixed potential, 𝐸𝑐𝑜𝑟𝑟, the exchange current, 𝑖𝑐𝑜𝑟𝑟 represents iron dissolution at the 
same rate as the oxygen reduction reaction. When polarized from its mixed potential,  the net 
current, 𝑖𝑛𝑒𝑡 (assume positive) does not include all the change of iron: the “internal current, 𝑖𝑂2” 
also contributes to a portion of the total change of iron – coexisting macro-cell (net current, 𝑖𝑛𝑒𝑡) 
and micro-cell corrosion (internal current, 𝑖𝑂2). 
\nPDF_PAGE 58\n 
 
18

(b)

In Butler -Volmer/Tafel equation -based numerical models, corrosion parameters depicted by 
Evan’s diagram ( Figure 2.2) i.e. anodic branch of steel dissolution ( 𝐸0𝑎, 𝑖0𝑎, 𝛽𝑎) and cathodic 
branch of oxygen reduction ( 𝐸0𝑐, 𝑖0𝑐, 𝛽𝑐) are ofte n used. It is mathematically equivalent to use 
both anodic and cathodic branches ( 𝐸𝑐𝑜𝑟𝑟, 𝑖𝑐𝑜𝑟𝑟, 𝛽𝑎, 𝛽𝑐) depicted in Stern-Geary diagram (Figure 
2.2), which, by linear extrapolation, gives a portion of the Evans diagram.

However, the latter set of parameters is more accessible in regular corrosion tests such as a three-
electrode potentiodynamic scan of a corroding rebar. Evan’s diagram is a simplified illustration of 

\nPDF_PAGE 59\n

the internal polarization over a corroding metal surface, and cannot be experimentally measured 
directly (Tan, 2012).  Therefore, it is practical to estimate 𝐸0𝑎, 𝑖0𝑎, 𝛽𝑎, 𝐸0𝑐, 𝑖0𝑐, 𝛽𝑐 from measured 
𝐸𝑐𝑜𝑟𝑟, 𝑖𝑐𝑜𝑟𝑟, 𝛽𝑎, 𝛽𝑐, or write the equivalent polarization equations in terms of 𝐸𝑐𝑜𝑟𝑟, 𝑖𝑐𝑜𝑟𝑟, 𝛽𝑎, 𝛽𝑐, 
as they render the same straight lines in a log-scale diagram. 
2.1.2.2 Treatment of corrosion cells 
Fundamentally, there are three types of corrosion cells: pure micro -cell, pure macro -cell, and a 
combination of the two, macro-and-micro-cell. Non-Uniform corrosion in RC has been modelled 
both in the longitudinal direction (Ghods et al., 2007; Isgor & Razaqpur, 2006; Kranc et al., 1992; 
Sohail, 2013)  and around its circumference (Kim & Kim, 2008; Muthulingam & Rao, 2015; 
Zacaruk, 2013) of rebar using one of these three types of cells. The following sections will discuss 
the pros and cons of each approach together with their implementation in practice. 
• Micro-cell model: no separation of anode and cathode 
The corrosion process is divided algebraically into separate anodic and cathodic reactions with no 
net accumulation of electrical charge. Uniform kineti cs of the anodic reaction and cathodic 
reaction are both prescribed on the surface of a rebar that may undergo corrosion. In this case, as 
the physical circuit is not explicitly modelled, the resistivity of the concrete will not play a role in 
determining the corrosion current although a conceptualized resistance between anodes and 
cathodes can be used to account for the ohmic polarization.  
The micro-cell model has two major limitations: Firstly, the effect of resistivity on corrosion rate 
cannot be captured. In aqueous solutions, the resistance between micro-anodes and micro-cathodes 
can be neglected because the resistivity is very low, and the distance between the anodic and 
cathodic sites is very short. In porous media, such as concrete, however, this short distance does 
not necessarily lead to negligible resistance, and the anode-cathode separation might be larger than 
in the case of an aqueous solution. This non -negligible resistance as a function of the degree of 
saturation plays a significant role in the case of uniform corrosion, which cannot be captured with 
a simple mixed-potential uniform corrosion model. The effect of resistance between anodes and 
cathodes can only be included from the original experiment test where the corrosion kinetics are 
determined.  
\nPDF_PAGE 60\n 
 
20 
Secondly, the intermediate active to passive transition kinetics is indeterminate and usually not 
available.  Rebar condition falls solely into either passive or active zones. For a given finite length 
of rebar (e.g. 5-10 cm), a binary characterization with either “active” or “passive” is not accurate. 
Appreciable variation has been found with repeated testing of strictly conditioned reinforced 
concrete samples (Sohail, 2013). It is believed that in aged concrete structures, due to varying 
exposure environments, different compositions, material non -uniformity (e.g. micro-cracks), etc. 
the rebar condition of a given finite segment may fall in between being active and passive. 
• Macro-cell model: separation of anode and cathode 
Macro-cell model is the most widely used approach with localized corrosion (often crack-induced) 
(Ghods et al., 2007; Isgor & Razaqpur, 2006; Kranc et al., 1992; Yu et al., 2014). In these models, 
the active rebar is considered a pure anode and the passive rebar is considered a pure cathode. 
These pure anodic and cathodic sites are prescribed by the user or determined based on other 
corrosion initiation criteria (i.e. critical chloride content, pH value). By separating the anode and 
cathode on a rebar, we assume the active rebar portion assigned as anode only generates electrons 
(Fe → Fe2+ + 2e−) and the passive rebar portion assigned as cathode only receives electrons 
( 2H2O + O2 → 4OH− ). Occasionally, the reverse process such as iron deposition is also 
considered for anode (Yu, Liu, & Li, 2017). Physically separating anodes and cathodes allows the 
computation of current distribution in concrete so that concrete resistivity becomes an influential 
factor in determining the corrosion current. The macro -cell model is particularly useful f or 
localized corrosion where the area of active rebar is small, and the corrosion rate is mainly 
contributed by macro-cell corrosion.   
Having said that, the macro-cell corrosion model has difficulties when dealing with non -uniform 
corrosion where the micr o-cell current is not negligible. Although neglecting the oxidation on 
passive rebar may be acceptable, the oxygen reduction in active rebar cannot be neglected when it 
is not largely polarized to its anodic direction, given the fact that, in concrete when the contribution 
of the macro-cell is limited due to high resistivity (Kranc & Sagüés, 1994) . Particularly, when a 
long segment of rebar is treated as an anode, the micro -cell corrosion at the location out of the 
reach of the macro-cell effect is rather large and cannot be neglected.  
\nPDF_PAGE 61\n 
 
21 
The macro-cell corrosion model also has greater limitations in uniform corrosion modelling. In 
some studies (Ghods et al., 2007; Isgor & Razaqpur, 2006; Pour-Ghaz et al., 2009a; Yu, Liu, & Li, 
2017), uniformly distributed anodes and cathodes with prescribed A/C ratios are used to model 
uniform corrosion. It was not clear whether the reported corrosion current density is only for the 
anodic rebar area, or it had been lumped onto the total rebar surface area. None theless, this 
treatment would raise three questions: First, the cathodic reaction in this type of macro-cell model 
is presumably oxygen reduction in the passive zone of the rebar. Therefore, it is not proper to 
model the cathodic reaction on an active reba r and a different cathodic kinetics representation 
should be used. Second, the choice of the proper size of the anodes and cathodes is rather guessed, 
and hence, is questionable. Ideally, when uniform corrosion is treated in a macro-cell fashion, the 
assigned separated anodic and cathodic sites should reflect the physical size of these micro-anodes 
and micro-cathodes, which could be extremely computationally costly due to the small grid size 
needed for this representation. Finally, although this macro -cell treatment of uniform corrosion 
has allowed saturation to affect the corrosion rate, this effect only reflects on the resistivity change, 
without consideration of the reduction of corroding area due to drainage of the pores. 
• Macro-and-micro-cell model 
The macro-and-micro-cell model is a combination of the micro -cell model and macro-cell model 
and is by far the most comprehensive treatment of corrosion cells. In this model, the previously 
separated pure anode and cathode in a macro-cell model are assigned as segments of active rebar 
and passive rebar, where both anodic and cathodic current are enabled (i.e., anodic current on 
active rebar, cathodic current on active rebar, anodic current on passive rebar, and cathodic current 
on passive rebar) (Cao et al., 2013; Sohail, 2013). Sometimes the anodic current on a passive rebar 
is neglected due to its low value (Kim & Kim, 2008; The et al., 2010). 
This model can capture both the macro - and the micro -corrosion currents, and hence the 
contribution of each component to the total non-uniform corrosion. However, its micro-cell feature 
and macro-cell feature still suffer from the previously mentioned limitations. 
\nPDF_PAGE 62\n 
 
22 
2.1.3 Corrosion parameters used in models 
The parameters used in corrosion modelling are typically measured from a corrosion test involving 
rebar in a simulated pore solution or directly from a rebar embedded in concrete. Due to the 
obvious advantages of repeatability and simplicity, the effects of pH and chloride content on rebar 
corrosion parameters were mostly studied in simulated concrete pore solutions. The effects of 
chloride and carbonates on the polarization cures have been studied mostly qualitatively (Moreno 
et al., 2004)  as there are o nly a very small number of studies that reported dependence of these 
parameters as a function of the chemical composition of the solution, such as chloride content and 
pH value, in a quantitative way. 
It is worth noticing that although the main objective i s to study the corrosion of rebars in RC 
structures, only a few of the reported corrosion parameters were  obtained by the direct 
measurement of rebar corrosion in concrete. A review of the various corrosion parameters used in 
corrosion models from the literature shows that values with a large range were used.  
Locke & Siman (1980)  analyzed the polarization curves of steel rebar in chloride -contaminated 
concrete using a galvanostatic polarization technique. They found that with different levels of 
chloride content, the observed Tafel slopes were in the range of 320-1570 mV/dec and the cathodic 
Tafel slopes were in the range of 380 -1080 mV/dec. and the calculated B value would be in the 
range of 82-278 mV. 
Alonso et al. (1998a)  tested the Tafel slopes of r ebar corrosion in aged concrete using the 
potentiodynamic polarization technique. The chloride salt was added to the concrete mix for the 
rebar segment under testing and the test was performed on the concrete samples under wet and dry 
conditions.  The forward scan from -400 mV to 400 mV (relative to 𝐸𝑐𝑜𝑟𝑟) was used to curve fit 
the Tafel slopes. They set the anodic Tafel slope to a fixed large value ( 1 × 1020 mV) to account 
for the sharp slope observed in the curve and fitted cathodic Tafel slopes as 289  and 441 mV/dec 
for wet concrete and concrete with ambient humidity.  
Chang et al. (2008a) measured the corrosion parameters in RC made with concrete containing flash 
and slag submerged in seawater. They used a “two-test procedure” in which the potentiodynamic 
scan was done separately in the anodic direction and cathodic direction to eliminate the initial 
\nPDF_PAGE 63\n 
 
23 
current surge and its influence on the distortion of scan curves. They noticed that the 
experimentally acquired polarization curve deviates from the But ler-Volmer equation form. In 
their companion paper (Chang et al., 2008b), they proposed a model based on the assumption of 
two parallel kinetic processes occurring at the steel/rust -film/concrete interfaces: an active 
corrosion process that follows the Buter -Volmer equation, and the possibility of growth or 
dissolution of a rust film on the steel surface. The latter process was used to account for that 
deviation. Some typical anodic Tafel slopes reported for fly ash concrete were 450, 600, 80 0 mV 
and the corresponding cathodic Tafel slopes were 240, 192, 263 mV/dec; for slag concrete, the 
anodic Tafel slopes were 205, 230, 295 mV/dec and the correspond cathodic Tafel slopes were 
480, 250, 235 mV/dec. For direct exposure to seawater, a new bar had Tafel slopes of 89 mV/dec 
and 1215 mV/dec, and a corroded rebar had Tafel slopes of 115 mV/dec and 900 mV/dec. 
Sohail (2013)  directly measured the corrosion parameters for rebar in partially submerged 
carbonated and non -carbonated concrete by the pot entiodynamic scan with explicit IR 
compensation between the reference electrode and the working electrode (corroding rebar). A 
similar “Two -test procedure” was also employed to test 24 replicate samples for each case: 
carbonated and non-carbonated to capture the uncertainty. The results are listed in Table 2.1.  
Table 2.1 Corrosion parameters for active and passive rebar (Sohail, 2013)

The anodic and cathodic Tafel slopes for active rebar were 244 mV/dec (range 180 -280 mV/dec) 
and 260 mV/dec (range 190-350 mV/dec). For passive rebar, the anodic Tafel slopes were 505 mV 
(range 374–700 mV/dec) and the cathodic Tafel slope ranges from 70 mV/dec to 160 mV/dec. 
Despite the complexity of the dependence of corrosion parameters on concrete -rebar conditions, 
many researchers have selected a single set of values for the Tafel slopes to model the corrosion 
kinetics. For some Tafel slopes used by different modeller s (Table 2.2), the calculated B values 
are 19 mV (Ghods et al., 2007; Isgor & Razaqpur, 2006; Kranc et al., 1992; Kranc & Sagüés, 
1994), 26 mV (Gulikers, 2005) with 19mV being closer to the case of an active rebar (26 mV). 
However, an 89 mV B value calculated using the averaged value of both Tafel slopes has also been 
used to model the active corroding rebar (Martın-Pérez, 1999).

There are a lot of cross-references of the corrosion parameters used, among which, some sources 
of highly referred Tafel slope values are presented below. 
• Source of 60 and 160 mV/dec  
\nPDF_PAGE 65\n 
 
25 
Kranc et al. (1992) pointed out that the cathodic Tafel slopes of corrosion in concrete should be in 
100 to 200 mV range when measured by very slow potential scan rates, and this range agrees with 
the Tafel slopes of about 160 mV commonly observed for oxygen reduction of steel in alkaline 
solutions (Fabjan et al., 1980). The iron dissolution reaction in concrete was assumed to be similar 
to the case in aqueous solution which is activation co ntrolled with a relatively small Tafel slope: 
60 mV/dec, typically, although the clear information of the aqueous solution was not stated 
(original article not accessible, cited as “Kaesche, H, Metallic Corrosion, NACE, Houston. Tex. 
1985” in the work of Kranc et al., 1992). 
• Source of 91 and 176 mV/dec 
Some studies found that the cathodic Tafel slope was 176 mV /dec from the testing of reinforced 
concrete in dry conditions  (Gulikers, 2005; Warkus et al., 2006) . The original test data source is 
not accessible. It was not stated whether the anodic Tafel slope of 91mV /dec was obtained from 
the same test. 
It has been well known that the experimentally acquired Tafel slopes from reinforced concrete 
tests remain remarkably uncertain, and their values are very different from the values used in some 
of the current models (Ghods et al., 2007; Gulikers, 2005; Isgor & Razaqpur, 2006; Kranc et al., 
1992; Kranc & Sagüés, 1994)  where the corrosion parameters employed were similar to the 
parameters found for steel corrosion in aqueous solutions (see the above -mentioned source of 
60/160 mV/dec). However, there are also some electrochemical corrosion models (Martın-Pérez, 
1999; Soh ail, 2013)  that used the corrosion parameters directly from reinforced concrete 
measurements. To conclude, to improve the prediction of the corrosion rate in cracked concrete, 
the proper corrosion parameters to use should be carefully and rationally dete rmined. Generally 
speaking, corrosion parameters directly measured in concrete with a uniform -corrosion-
assumption-based technique such as the potentiodynamic scan, should not be used in macro -cell 
models when microcell corrosion is not negligible; such pa rameters should be used in micro -cell 
or the micro-portion of micro-and-macro-cell models. A rational and systematic procedure for the 
determination of the corrosion parameters is needed to comply with the complicated issues 
associated with corrosion in cracked RC structures under various exposure conditions. 
\nPDF_PAGE 66\n 
 
26 
2.2 Microenvironment in concrete and natural exposure conditions 
Moisture penetration, carbonation and chloride ingress are the major processes that affect the 
microenvironment near the rebar such as pH value and chloride content, which determine the onset 
of the propagation stage and affect the ongoing corrosion rate. These processes have been studied 
by a lot of researchers, and a good collection of numerical models were proposed to simulate the 
process of moisture transport, carbonation, and ingress of chlorides in concrete under different 
boundary conditions.  In this chapter, the state of the art in this area will be summarized with a 
focus on the treatment of cracks in RC structures and the natural service conditions.  
2.2.1 Moisture transport 
The modelling of moisture evolution in cracked concrete is the most important task as moisture 
content and the transport of moisture affect both the ingress of chloride ions and carbonation. 
Water transport in partially  saturated concrete has been long investigated and a large number of 
models have been proposed (Bažant & Najjar, 1972; DaÏan & Saliba, 1993; Isgor & Razaqpur, 
2004; Künzel, 1995; Pel et al., 1996; Ramadani, 2013; Sun et al., 2018) . Although it has been 
shown to be usually in good agreement with experimental observations, the treatment of the 
moisture transport process could be confusingly different from one study to another. In order to 
avoid further confusion, the following review will start with the most generic treatment in general 
porous media before considering some concrete-specific studies. 
In porous media, such as concrete, water may transport in both vapour and liquid forms. Liquid 
and vapour flow are usually treated separately with respect to the different driving forces (Scheffler 
& Plagge, 2010) : The vapour diffusion flow is proportional to the gradient of vapour 
pressure/phase-averaged density with the water vapour permeability/diffusivity as transport 
coefficient; the liquid water flow, on t he other hand, is proportional to the pressure gradient with 
hydraulic conductivity/ permeability as transport coefficient. The general equations are typically 
written as: 
Water vapour diffusion models: 
 𝜕𝑚𝑣
𝜕𝑡 = ∇ ∙ [𝐷𝑣(𝜃𝑙)𝑅𝑣𝑇 ∇𝑝𝑣] + 𝜕𝑚𝑙→𝑣𝜕𝑡 = ∇ ∙ [𝐷𝑣(𝜃𝑙) ∙ ∇𝜌𝑣] + 𝜕𝑚𝑙→𝑣𝜕𝑡  (2.21) 
\nPDF_PAGE 67\n 
 
27 
Liquid water flow conduction models:

𝜕𝑡 = ∇ ∙ [𝜌𝑙𝐾𝑙(𝜃𝑙)(∇𝜓 + 1)] + 𝜕𝑚𝑣→𝑙𝜕𝑡 = ∇ ∙ [𝜌𝑙 𝑘𝑙(𝜃𝑙)𝜇 (∇𝑝𝑐 + 𝜌𝑙𝒈)] + 𝜕𝑚𝑣→𝑙𝜕𝑡  
 
(2.22) 
where 𝑚𝑣[𝑘𝑔/𝑚3] is total volume -averaged water vapour mass content, 𝐷𝑣[𝑚2/𝑠] is vapour
