density, 𝐾𝑙[𝑚/𝑠 ] is hydraulic conductivity  or coefficient of permeability as a function of water 
content, 𝑘𝑙[𝑚2] is permeability as a function of water content, 𝜓[𝑚] is pressure head, 𝑝𝑐[𝑃𝑎] is 
capillary pressure, 𝜇[𝑃𝑎 ∙ 𝑠] is dynamic viscosity of water, 𝒈[𝑚2/𝑠] is gravitational acceleration. 
Concrete generally has very high capillary pressure due to its fine pore structure, the gravity effect 
becomes negligible. If we neglect gravity: 
 𝜕𝑚𝑙
𝜕𝑡 = ∇ ∙ [𝜌𝑙𝐾𝑙(𝜃𝑙)∇𝜓] + 𝜕𝑚𝑣→𝑙𝜕𝑡 = ∇ ∙ [𝜌𝑙 𝑘𝑙(𝜃𝑙)𝜇 ∇𝑝𝑐] + 𝜕𝑚𝑣→𝑙𝜕𝑡  (2.23) 
In analogy with the diffusion treatment of water vapour transport, the transport of liquid water can 
also be modelled by using a diffusion model. Neglecting the gravity effect, the driving force is the 
gradient of water concentration and the diffusivity which is a function of water concentration is 
the transport coefficient.  
Liquid water diffusion: 
 𝜕𝑚𝑙
𝜕𝑡 = ∇ ∙ [𝜌𝑙𝐷𝑙(𝜃𝑙)𝛻𝜃𝑙] + 𝜕𝑚𝑣→𝑙𝜕𝑡  (2.24) 
where the liquid water diffusivity 
 𝐷𝑙(𝜃𝑙) = 𝑘𝑙(𝜃𝑙)𝜇 𝑑𝑝𝑐𝑑𝜃𝑙 (2.25) 
\nPDF_PAGE 68\n 
 
28 
The derivation of moisture transport models in both the vapour and the liquid phases of water has 
also been examined using homogenization techniques where it was shown that transport of 
moisture in the vapour and liquid phases can be unified using the Richards equation through the 
concept of “total diffusivity” as shown in Equation(2.27) (Samson et al., 2005). Considering 𝜌𝑣 =
𝑓(𝜃𝑙) (Daian, 1988), and the mutual phase change vanishes: 
 𝑚𝑙→𝑣 + 𝑚𝑣→𝑙 = 0  (2.26) 
Equation (2.21) and (2.22) can be combined as  
 𝜕𝑚𝑙+𝑣
𝜕𝑡 = ∇ ∙ [𝜌𝑙𝐷𝑡𝑜𝑎𝑙(𝜃𝑙)𝛻𝜃𝑙] (2.27) 
where, the total diffusivity of liquid and vapour 
 𝐷𝑡𝑜𝑎𝑙(𝜃𝑙) = 𝜌𝑙𝐷𝑙 + 𝑑𝜌𝑣𝑑𝜃𝑙 𝐷𝑣𝜌𝑙  (2.28) 
This unified equation fully describes the movement of both vapour and liquid water with one single 
variable. A similar unification of Richards’ equation could also be derived in the form of a liquid 
water flow conduction model, i.e. Equation (2.21) and (2.23) with a total hydraulic conductivity, 
𝐾𝑡𝑜𝑡𝑎𝑙  (DaÏan & Saliba, 1993).  
 𝜕𝑚𝑙+𝑣
𝜕𝑡 = 𝜕𝑚𝑙+𝑣𝜕𝜓 ∙ 𝜕𝜓𝜕𝑡 = ∇ ∙ [𝜌𝑙𝐾𝑡𝑜𝑡𝑎𝑙(𝜃𝑙)(∇𝜓)] (2.29) 
However, a separate solution of the liquid and the vapour transport is often needed in the case 
where the liquid water flow needs to be coupled with the transport of ions in the pore solution 
through advection. 
When the liquid water flow is modelled, it is worth noticing that the diffusion model in the for m 
of Equation (2.21) and the conduction model in the form of Equation (2.22) can be considered 
equivalent, provided that the proper choice of transport coefficient and the driving force are made. 
The distinguished difference between those two approaches is related to the choice of the driving 
force: gradient of water content vs. capillary pressure. The physical driving force of liquid water 
\nPDF_PAGE 69\n 
 
29 
transport, however, is the gradient of pressure. The diffusion approach, by introducing an artificial 
equivalent driving force (gradient of water content), is limited in its extendibility to hysteresis and 
solute transport modelling (Scheffler & Plagge, 2010). 
In various moisture transport models, the moisture flow (liquid, vapour, or combined) is 
proportional to the gradient of various variables such as mass water content, volumetric water 
content, hydraulic head, pressure head (neglectin g gravity), relative humidity (Bažant & Najjar, 
1971), vapour density , and accordingly various transport coefficient were developed to be 
compatible with the selected form of the driving force. 
Cracks, depending on their morphology and density, can be tr eated as discrete cracks (e.g., load-
induced) or smeared cracked material (e.g., freeze-thaw induced). When treated discretely, cracks 
can be treated as free surface or equivalent porous media (Boulfiza et al., 2003; Li, 2014) . When 
the cracks are treated as equivalent porous media, the concept of the relationship between the 
equilibrated relative humidity (RH) and the radius of the smallest empty pore given by the Kelvin 
equation is extended to the cracks. Given a distribution of the openings between the lips of a crack, 
the permeability of the equivalent porous medium can be estima ted under different exposure RH 
conditions. 
 ln(𝑅𝐻) = ln ( 𝑝𝑣
𝑝𝑠𝑎𝑡
) = 2𝛾𝑉𝑚
𝑟𝑅𝑇  (2.30) 
where 𝑝𝑣[𝑃𝑎] is vapour pressure, 𝑝𝑠𝑎𝑡[𝑃𝑎] is saturated vapour pressure above a flat surface at 
temperature 𝑇[𝐾] , 𝛾[𝑁/𝑚] is surface tension, 𝑉𝑚[𝑚3/𝑚𝑜𝑙] is molar volume, 𝑟[𝑚] is radius of a 
droplet (negative value for concave meniscus), which is related to the largest capillary pores size 
or a crack opening size of |𝑟𝑝|/𝑐𝑜𝑠𝛼. The parameter 𝛼 is the contact angle determined by the type 
of liquid and the solid surface. 
2.2.2 Transport of gas 
The gases of most interest in concrete are carbon dioxide and oxygen. Similar to water vapour, the 
diffusion of oxygen and carbon dioxide is driven by the gas density/partial pressure in the gas 
phase. Because the bulk movement of gas in concr ete is rare in practical scenarios, the advection 
and dispersion effects are often neglected. Knudsen diffusion is only significant when the mean 
\nPDF_PAGE 70\n 
 
30 
free path of gas is of the same order or greater than the pores or interparticle space (Kaviany, 2012). 
The Knudsen number is defined as 
 𝐾𝑛 = 𝜆(mean free path of gas molecules)𝐶(average pore size or interparticle clearance) (2.31) 
When 𝐾𝑛 ≫ 1, the flow is Knudsen flow and the diffusion is Knudsen diffusion. T he mean free 
path of air at room temperature and at ambient pressure is 68 nm (S. G. Jennings, 1988)  which 
could be comparable to the size of smaller capillary pores (10 nm – 5 𝜇m). Therefore, at very dry 
conditions where concrete small pores were emptied, the Knudsen diffusion needs to be considered 
and the diffusivity becomes a combination of molecular and Knudsen diffusion. 
For carbon dioxide and oxygen, t he phase change term 
𝜕𝑚𝑙→𝑣
𝜕𝑡  in Equation (2.21) becomes a sink 
term of the consumption rate. The consumption rate of carbon dioxide is determined by the 
saturation level and pH in the pore solution and the consumption rate of oxygen is det ermined by 
the oxygen-reduction cathodic reaction of the rebar corrosion. 
2.2.3 Transport of dissolved solutes  
• Diffusion-advection-dispersion 
The solutes in concrete pore solution such as chloride ions and dissolved gases are transported by 
diffusion, advection, and dispersion. The driving force for the diffusion of dissolved ions is the ion 
concentration gradient in the pore solution.  Advection is the ion movement carried by the pore 
solution through the liquid phase flow during wetting and drying process. Dis persion is a 
mechanical mixing phenomenon as the ions move through the porous network. The dispersion 
effect can be neglected when Peclet number 𝑃𝑒 < 1 – 10 (Kaviany, 2012) . In concrete, the 
dispersion effect is often neglected due to the slow water movement under water content gradient 
(Samson et al., 2005). The transport of salts in concrete can be described by the diffusion-advection 
equation, whereby: 
 𝜕(𝜃𝑐)
𝜕𝑡 = ∇ ∙ (𝐷𝑒𝑓𝑓𝜃 𝜕𝑐𝜕𝑧 + 𝒖𝑐) + 𝜃 (𝜕𝑐𝜕𝑡)𝑆𝑖𝑛𝑘/𝑆𝑜𝑢𝑟𝑐𝑒 (2.32) 
\nPDF_PAGE 71\n 
 
31 
where 𝑐 [𝑘𝑔/𝑚3] is liquid phase -averaged solute concentration, 𝒖 [𝑚/s] is Darcy’s velocity, 
𝐷𝑒𝑓𝑓 [𝑚2/s] is effective diffusivity, term (𝜕𝑐𝜕𝑡)𝑆𝑖𝑛𝑘/𝑆𝑜𝑢𝑟𝑐𝑒may be introduced as chloride binding  
(negative) and releasing (positive) or the sulphate consumption (negative).  
When dispersion is not neglected, such as the fast sorption of solution into dry concrete with a 
large Peclet number (Puyate et al., 1998), The dispersion coefficient 𝐷𝑑[𝑚2/𝑠] needs to be added 
to the effective diffusivity. 
2.2.4 Natural exposure boundary conditions 
Compared to a laboratory -controlled environment, natural exposure conditions feature daily 
fluctuation of RH, temperature, radiation, seasonal rainfall, snow melt, and scheduled application 
of de-icing salts.  The moisture content and the movement of water in RC structures may also 
fluctuate dramatically which may have a significant impact on the chloride ingress, carbonation 
rate, and corrosion rate. 
In many studies on concrete moisture, the internal RH is used to describe the saturation status of 
concrete given the measured RH -moisture content retention/isotherm curves. However, the 
continuous non -steady state moisture transport is caused b y the ever -changing micro -climate 
immediately surrounding a given RC structure. Therefore, a transient model must be employed to 
capture the evolution of water content in naturally exposed RC structures. Measurements of the 
internal RH of concrete samples under natural and simulated environments showed that for 
sheltered concrete, temperature was the main influential factor and for unsheltered concrete the 
dominant factor was rain periods (Andrade et al., 1999) . For known hysteresis and the effect of 
temperature on the retention curve, direct measurement of water content is more preferred than 
RH because a given RH may correspond to different water contents depending on the temperature, 
pore structure and wettin g-and-drying history. Ryu et al. (2011)  measured the relative moisture 
content of transversely cracked/uncracked concrete under simulated environments, it was found 
that the affected depth by 24-hour rain was within 30 cm for concrete (w/c=0.6) and within 10 cm 
for concrete (w/c=0.3) and that the rain intensity had little effect. For cracked concrete, it was 
found that larger cracks would lead to slightly faster drying of the surrounding concrete, but the 
effect became less significant for less dense concrete (w/c=0.6).  
\nPDF_PAGE 72\n 
 
32 
Given the availability of a good understanding of the mechanisms governing the transport of liquid 
water and water vapour in porous media, numerical simulation is considered a robust tool to 
accurately assess the multi-factor affected moisture evolution in naturally exposed RC structures. 
Having said that, there remain a few challenging tasks: Firstly, proper boundary conditions need 
to be developed for wetting (liquid/moist air) and drying. Secondly, hysteresis needs to be 
considered during wetting and drying. Thirdly, the effects of multiple environmental factors such 
as wind speed, radiation, and condensation need to be properly addressed. 
 
 
\nPDF_PAGE 73\n 
 
33 
3 On the Corrosion Parameters Acquired through Potentiodynamic Scans of Carbon 
Steel Rebar in Simulated Pore Solution and Mortar 
3.1 Preface 
It was introduced in Chapter 1 that one of the drawbacks in current numerical corrosion models on 
non-uniform corrosion of cracked/uncracked RC structures is that the corrosion kinetics described 
in these models are often constant, and the dependence of these critical corrosion parameters (i.e., 
equilibrium potential, exchange current density, Tafel slopes) on the chemical environment is 
usually not explicitly represented. In Chapter 2 (Section 2.1.3), A review of the corrosion 
parameters used in corrosion models from the literature shows that values with a large range were 
used. These parameters were either measured from a corrosion test involving rebar in a simulated 
pore solution or directly from a rebar embedded in concrete. Only a few of the reported corrosion 
parameters were obtained by the direct measurement of rebar corrosion in concrete. However, 
these tested reinforced concrete samples had diverse and separate conditions. Typically, these 
samples had limited variations in chloride content, moisture content, or carbonation state, with 
only one or two treatment levels. Although it is feasible to make qualitative assumptions about the 
impact of these parameters on the corrosion rate, quantifying it accurately is extremely difficult. 
Determining the specific causes of variations in the dat a due to the particular conditions of the 
concrete samples is a challenging task. 
The work presented in this chapter aims at an accurate measurement of the corrosion parameters 
in simulated pore solutions and mortar  and a quantitative analysis of the impac t of the major 
environmental parameters on the corrosion parameters. This chapter starts with examining the 
challenges of the accurate measurement and interpretation of corrosion parameters due to 
measuring techniques and unrealistic sample conditions, which will be addressed in this chapter. 
Realistic rebar conditions were used. The as -received rebar retained the mill scale and was 
prepassivated before exposure to chloride and carbonation. The long-term effect of depassivation 
in simulated pore solutions was investigated. 
Then a modified three-electrode potentiodynamic test procedure was carried out using a Faraday 
shield and floating ground to reduce the large electrical potential oscillations caused by real -time 
IR compensation, testing the partially saturated rebar embedded in mortar in a non -submerged 
\nPDF_PAGE 74\n 
 
34 
setting to avoid the open circuit potential drifting d ue to extensive surface wetting of samples, 
using two-step scans with adequate rest time to minimize the distortion of scan curves. 
The corrosion potential ( 𝐸𝑐𝑜𝑟𝑟), corrosion current density ( 𝑖𝑐𝑜𝑟𝑟), and Tafel slopes ( 𝛽𝑎, 𝛽𝑐) were 
measured in  four media: simulated pore solution (SPS); carbonated simulated pore solution 
(CSPS); non-carbonated mortar; and carbonated mortar with various chloride concentrations. 
This study examines the impact of depassivation, rust build -up, and chloride concentration on 
corrosion parameters. The investigation includes the evaluation of rebar in SPS and CSPS over 
different periods, as well as tests conducted on non -carbonated and carbonated mortars. The 
analysis explores the effects of chloride content, ca rbonation, conditioning duration, and relative 
humidity on corrosion parameters in these media. 
The manuscript of this chapter was submitted to “Construction and Building Materials”. Gang Li 
is the primary author who designed and performed the experimental  study and data analysis. Dr. 
Moh Boulfiza and Dr. Richard Evitts contributed to the review of the manuscript and provided 
feedback. 
3.2 Introduction 
Rebar corrosion is one of the most critical durability issues associated with reinforced concrete 
structures. Corrosion mitigation measures include selecting proper materials, on -site/laboratory 
corrosion testing and protection, and predictive corrosion modelling. Corrosion parameters such 
as corrosion potential, corrosion current density, and Tafel data are neede d to evaluate corrosion 
rate, design cathodic protection, and develop predictive electrochemical corrosion models. 
It is a well -known fact that the measured corrosion rate and corrosion potential of a given 
reinforced concrete (RC) structure are strongly d ependent on the severity (harshness) of the 
exposure service conditions (temperature, RH, moisture content, presence of aggressive chemical 
species such as chloride ions, and carbonation, cracks, etc.) as well as on the accuracy of the 
measurement procedure used. Hence, it is expected that estimation of how the measured quantities, 
at a given time, could fluctuate if some of those parameters change over time (seasonal variations, 
use of de-icing salts in winter, etc.) would be very helpful. Similarly, the a ccuracy of a corrosion 
measurement depends on the kind of precautions taken to minimize interference of “parasitic 
\nPDF_PAGE 75\n 
 
35 
phenomena” such as the use of IR compensation or the use of Faraday’s shield. Here also, 
measurements are often carried out without all these precautions, and it is often unclear how much 
improvement one should expect if they were used. Hence, the existence of a practical and reliable 
method remains rather elusive due to the large number of parameters that can affect the measured 
rate. 
In this paper, the protective dense oxide film that forms on the rebar surface in high alkalinity is 
termed as “passive film”, although it is not a passive film in the traditional sense. This passive film 
serves as a diffusion barrier that limits the corrosion rat e, when the rebar is in its “passive” state. 
The destruction of this oxide film due to low pH and/or the presence of chloride ions is called “de-
passivation”, and the rebar undergoes “active” corrosion. The minimum chloride content in 
concrete or the chloride ion concentration in a solution that leads to significant active corrosion is 
called critical chloride content or critical chloride ion concentration. 
Rebar corrosion parameters are typically measured using electrochemical testing of rebar in 
simulated pore solution or directly from rebar embedded in concrete. Due to repeatability and 
simplicity of experiment, the effects of pH and chloride content on rebar corrosion have been 
mostly studied in simulated concrete pore solutions (Figueira et al., 2017; G hods et al., 2010; 
Moreno et al., 2004; Shi et al., 2016) . In these research works there were various types of steel 
rebar samples used. Some potentiodynamic tests were performed on polished cross -sections of 
rebar (Figueira et al., 2017; Shi et al., 2016). It was found that the roughness of the polished surface 
and the pre-oxidization in aerated NaCl solution affect the critical chloride concentration threshold 
(Figueira et al., 2017). To mimic the passivated state of steel rebars in concrete, the polished rebar 
samples were pre-passivated in a simulated pore solution before the tests (Shi et al., 2016). In some 
tests, the mill scale (pre -existing oxide layer) was preserved, and the tests were performed on a 
cylindrical surface of the as-received rebar with no pre-passivation (Moreno et al., 2004) or with 
adequate pre-passivation (Ghods et al., 2010). The synergistic effects of chloride and carbonates 
on polarization characteristics have been studied qualitatively in solutions (Moreno et al., 2004). 
It is also worth mentioning that the chloride -induced depassivation state could be unstable in the 
first 10 days (Ghods et al., 2010) . To include the above -mentioned considerations,  the ideal 
potentiodynamic tests of rebar in simulated pore solutions need to be performed on rebar samples 
with mill scale, passivated in simulated pore solution before bein g subject to  chloride and/or 
\nPDF_PAGE 76\n 
 
36 
carbonation attack. Also, the exposure conditioning period should be longer than a few days before 
the test to allow for the possible depassivation to reach a stable condition. 
The corrosion parameters in aqueous solutions could be helpful with the qualitative comparison of 
the corrosion resistance of rebar in different environments. However, these parameters might not 
be representative of rebar corrosion in concrete, which leads to inaccurate prediction of corrosion 
rate in cor rosion models. A variety of numerical models were proposed to predict the corrosion 
rate in the propagation phase and mechanical behaviour of reinforced concrete structures under 
different exposure conditions (Otieno et al., 2012) . Despite the complexity,  modelling the 
electrochemical process of rebar embedded in concrete is the core component required to calculate 
the corrosion current and the corresponding rate of loss of metal. A parametric study with a 
separated anode and cathode and a fixed concrete b ulk resistivity shows that the corrosion rate is 
most sensitive to the anodic and cathodic Tafel slopes (Ge & Isgor, 2007). Therefore, the accurate 
prediction of corrosion rate relies on the correct choice of these two corrosion parameters. 
There have be en only a few corrosion parameters reported by the direct measurement of rebar 
corrosion in concrete (Alonso et al., 1998b; Chang et al., 2008b; Elsener, 2005; Garcés et al., 2005; 
Gulikers, 2005; Locke & Siman, 1980; Sohail, 2013). These corrosion parameters had a significant 
range of variation as they seem to be sensitive to different conditions in concrete, such as moisture 
content, pH, and chloride content. The reported corrosion parameters for rebar cast in 
concrete/mortar and their conditions are summarized in Table 3.1.

Figure 3.1 Stern-Geary diagrams plotted with the reversible potentials and exchange current 
densities of the half -cell reactions utilized by different modellers for reinforced concrete

In this table, the corrosion potentials and corrosion current densities were derived from the half -
cell reversible potentials ( 𝐸𝑜𝑎 , 𝐸𝑜𝑐 ) and exchange current densities ( 𝑖0𝑎 , 𝑖0𝑐 ) by the mixed -
potential theory treating corrosion as uniform corrosion with the same total area for anodic and 
cathodic reactions. Some corrosion current densities are comparable to the active rebar corrosion 
in solutions having the same order of magni tude (Ghods et al., 2007; Isgor & Razaqpur, 2006; 
Kranc & Sagüés, 1994), and some are significantly smaller (Gulikers, 2005; Martın-Pérez, 1999), 
where corrosion parameters are based on measured parameters in concrete. Moreover, the Tafel 
slopes, especially the anodic Tafel slopes measured from rebars in simulated pore solutions are 
significantly smaller than that observed in concrete. When macro -cell corrosion is modelled with 
smaller Tafel slopes, the macro -cell corrosion rate becomes ove rly sensitive to the overpotential 
on the rebar surface, leading to inaccurate large prediction of corrosion rate. Such overestimation 
would be most likely corrected by prescribing proper or actual discrete anodic and cathodic areas 
on the rebar, which are not feasibly accessible in real concrete structures. 

\nPDF_PAGE 81\n 
 
41 
The corrosion parameters in concrete are the critical component in non -destructive testing and 
numerical simulations as the calculated corrosion rate depend s on the corrosion parameters used. 
Although some studies have reported corrosion parameters, direct measurement of rebar corrosion 
in concrete is still challenging. In tests on rebar in concrete (Alonso et al., 1998b; Chang et al., 
2008b; Elsener, 2005; Garcés et al., 2005; Gulikers, 2005; Locke & Si man, 1980; Sohail, 2013), 
regardless of various concrete proportioning and different concentrations of chloride 
contamination, there are limitations in the utility of the data. The limitations are originated from 
measuring technique, and unrealistic sample preparation. For the measuring technique, the IR drop 
between the rebar and the reference electrode is either neglected or post -compensated by 
measuring the resistance through the concrete cover separately. Real -time IR compensation has 
been attempted (Chang et al., 2008a; Sohail, 2013) and reportedly causes scan curves to have large 
oscillations and noise, which makes the results very difficult to interpret. For sample preparation, 
when sodium chloride is added during concrete mixing, the embedded rebar does not have a chance 
to be pre-passivated before it comes into contact with the chloride ions. In addition, the reported 
data were acquired from reinforced concrete samples with scattered and isolated conditions, with 
only one or two treatment levels of chloride content, moisture content, or carbonation state. It 
would be possible to speculate qualitatively on how the increase or decrease of any of those 
parameters may affect the corrosion rate, a quantitative estimation is very challenging. It is difficult 
to interpret how the variation in the data was caused by these concrete sample conditions.  
In this research work, a quantitative analysis of the impact of the major environmental parameters 
and a measurement procedure is carried out. This present research explores a suitable setup of the 
three-electrode potentiodynamic test procedure to quantify the corrosion parameters of rebar in 
simulated pore solutions and mortar. The improvements in the setup are aimed at overcoming the 
above-mentioned experimental challenges by eliminating the large potential oscillation during the 
real-time IR compensation, the corrosion potential drift and the distortion of the scans. The 
comprehensive experimental program tests the as -received carbon steel rebar samples in fo ur 
media: simulated pore solution (SPS); carbonated simulated pore solution (CSPS); non-carbonated 
mortar; and carbonated mortar. The corrosion potential, corrosion current density, and Tafel data 
were measured. With this new corrosion data, the effect of chloride and carbonation on rebar 
depassivation and corrosion rate in simulated pore solutions and mortar are analyzed.  
\nPDF_PAGE 82\n 
 
42 
3.3 Material and methods 
3.3.1 Sample preparation 
No.10 deformed carbon steel rebar (nominal diameter: 11.3 mm) was used in this research. The 
rebar was cold cut into the desired lengths (3 cm and 14 cm) with a metal cutting band saw. The 
original mill scale of the rebar was preserved to represent the form in its practical application. A 
2.5 mm diameter hole was drilled into one end of the rebar to allow a lead wire to be attached. Any 
loose red rust visible on the rebar surface was gently removed by a wire brush. The rebar samples 
were rinsed with distilled water and then degreased with acetone to remove any contamination due 
to the cutting and dri lling process. An electrical connection was secured by wedging a wire lead 
inside the drilled hole and sealing the hole with epoxy. All rebars used in this study for both rebar 
samples and mortar samples were preconditioned in the simulated pore solution (pH=13.5) for one 
week to develop a stable passive film on the surface , which would achieve adequate pre -
passivation according to a study (Poursaee & Hansson, 2007). 
Two types of test samples were prepared: bare rebar samples and samples with rebar cast in mortar. 
Figure 3.2 presents a schematic of these samples.  
  
Figure 3.2 Schematic for rebar and mortar sample dimensions.  Rebar sample is a NO. 10 
deformed rebar and mortar sample has a water-cement ratio of 0.7. 
 

\nPDF_PAGE 83\n 
 
43 
Both ends of the rebar sample and mortar sample were coated with epoxy resin, with only the 
lateral or cylindrical surface being exposed to the electrolyte.  
Type GU cement was used for casting 36 mortar samples. The mix proportioning (cement: water: 
sand) was 1:0.7:1.65 by weight. Dry NaCl crystals (Manufacturer: Fisher Chemical, purity≥99.0%) 
were added to the mixing water as a source of chloride contamination. Six levels of contamination 
were considered ranging from 0% to 5% (wt%, by weight of mixing solution); the equivalent 
chloride contents with respect to cement mass are listed in Table 3.4. The mortar samples were 
demolded after 48 hours and cured in sealed plastic wrap for 14 days. Then two coats of epoxy 
were applied to the end surfaces of the cylinders and extended rebar surfaces; the lateral surfaces 
of the mortar cylinders remained wrapped in plastic film. The epoxy coatings were fully cured 
after another 14 days. The wrapped cylinders were cured in the ambient environment until they 
reached a total age of 60 days.  
Table 3.4 NaCl contamination levels in mortar 
Chloride level 0 1 2 3 4 5 
wt.% NaCl mixing 
solution 
 
0% 0.5% 1.0% 2.0% 3.0% 5.0% 
wt.% Cl by weight of 
cement 0% 0.21% 0.43% 0.87% 1.31% 2.24% 
In order to create carbonated mortar samples, half of the coated samples were then put into a 
carbonation chamber of 50% (vol%) CO 2 and 65% relative humidity (RH); the other half were 
stored in a humidity chamber of 65% RH. The samples were exposed for one month before the 
corrosion tests were performed. The carbonation progress was checked by spraying 1% (vol%) 
phenolphthalein solution on a freshly broken surface of the mortar samples. The mortar samples 
were fully carbonated after one month. The mortar samples (both non-carbonated and carbonated) 
were conditioned at various moisture levels, which includes the relative humidity of 65% , 85% 
and 95% for two months and finally submerged in fresh water for seven days. A uniform moisture 
distribution is preferred so that the moisture content at the rebar depth can be calculated by the 
overall moisture content of the mortar samples and a gra dual wetting procedure was chosen to 
achieve a uniform moisture distribution; drying of wet mortar is a slow process, and it produces a 
moisture profile along the depth of each sample. However, the wetting process proceeds much 
\nPDF_PAGE 84\n 
 
44 
faster than drying, with a c onsistent moisture front sweeping through the sample, creating a 
consistent moisture condition throughout the samples. In addition, with a shortened conditioning 
period, the reduced rust accumulation on the rebar will be less likely to crack the samples. 
Two types of simulated pore solutions were formulated to provide corrosive electrolytes for the 
rebar samples: non -carbonated simulated pore solution (SPS) and carbonated simulated pore 
solution (CSPS). The SPS and CSPS have a pH of 13.5 and 8.0, respectively. The composition of 
SPS follows previous studies (Figueira et al., 2017; Ghods et al., 2010)  (Table 3.5), and the 
composition of CSPS (Table 3.6) is based on the measured ionic concentration of calcium and 
potassium in carbonated concrete (Pu et al., 2012) . Sodium chl oride was added to the pore 
solutions with the same molar concentration as the mixing solution used for mortar casting.  
Table 3.5 Dry chemicals used for the simulated pore solution, SPS (pH=13.5) 
Dry chemicals added Ca(OH)2 NaOH KOH CaSO4∙2H2O 
Concentration [mol/L] 0.012 0.1 0.2 0.002 
 
Table 3.6 Dry chemicals used for the simulated carbonated pore solution, CSPS (pH=8.0) 
Dry chemicals added KHCO3 CaSO4∙2H2O 
Concentration [mol/L] 0.1 0.002 
In summary, there were six levels of chloride contamination and two levels of pH/carbonation 
state for both rebars in simulated pore solution and mortar. For the mortar samples, there are also 
four levels of moisture conditions: relative humidity of 65%, 8 5%, 95% and submerged. Three 
replicate samples were prepared for each condition level to capture the uncertainty of the test data.  
3.3.2 Test procedure 
The three-electrode potentiodynamic test was used to measure the corrosion parameters. The scan 
range was ±150 mV around the open circuit potential (OCP). A graphite rod or a stainless steel 
mesh was used as the counter electrode. A saturated calomel electrode (SCE) was used as the 
reference electrode; all potentials reported in this paper are with respect to the  saturated calomel 
\nPDF_PAGE 85\n 
 
45 
electrode (SCE) with real -time IR compensation activated on the Gamry Reference 600 
potentiostat that was used to perform the tests. 
The three-electrode system for rebar in simulated pore solution and mortar are shown in Figure 
3.3. The first series of tests showed that the counter electrode material type did not affect the 
potentiodynamic curve beyond normal experimental uncertainty. For the test of bare rebar samples 
in solution, a graphite rod was used as the counter electrode. For the test of mortar cylinders, the 
electrode was not submerged, and a stainless steel mesh was used as a counter electrode. A sponge 
sheet slightly moistened with simulated pore solution ser ved as electrolytic contact between the 
stainless steel mesh and the mortar cylinder surface. The stainless steel mesh was wrapped around 
the lateral surface of the mortar cylinder and secured with stainless steel hose clamps to achieve a 
uniform current d istribution over the embedded rebar surface. The reference electrode had a 
contact point with the sponge through a small cut-away area in the stainless steel mesh. The whole 
mortar sample testing setup was installed in a sealed PVC box to prevent the spong e from 
completely drying out during the scans. Both setups were enclosed in a Faraday shield connected 
to floating ground. The scan settings are listed in Table 3.7.  
It was noted that, although often used, epoxy-coated rebar ends were reported to be susceptible to 
crevice corrosion in a solution. A setup of the rebar cut end held tightly with Teflon washers was 
reported to eliminate visible signs of cr evice corrosion  (Ghods et al., 2010) . This setup was 
attempted. However, the rebar cut end is very sharp, where the rebar rib and the cut surface form 
a small angle. These sharp, thin edges create hot spots for corrosion, and any chamfering to remove 
the sharp edges would expose bare steel surfaces without mill scale and create gaps under the 
Teflon washers. The authors acknowledge that possible crevice corrosion near the epoxy coating 
may contribute to the source of error in the rebar test in a solution.  Additionally, the potential 
gradient around the rebar by using a rod counter electrode in the solution could also be a source of 
error.  
The pre-passivated rebar samples were conditioned for 20 days in the chloride -contaminated test 
solutions (SPS and CSPS) to allow adequate time for the breakdown of the passive film.  
\nPDF_PAGE 86\n

3.4 Data processing 
The experimental scan data were fitted to the Butler-Volmer Equation 
 𝑖𝑛𝑒𝑡 = 𝑖𝑐𝑜𝑟𝑟 ∙ [10(𝐸−𝐸𝑐𝑜𝑟𝑟)/𝛽𝑎 − 10(𝐸−𝐸𝑐𝑜𝑟𝑟)/𝛽𝑐 ] (3.1) 
where, 𝑖𝑛𝑒𝑡 is net current density, 𝐸𝑐𝑜𝑟𝑟 is corrosion potential, 𝑖𝑐𝑜𝑟𝑟 is corrosion current density, 
βa is anodic Tafel slope, and βc is cathodic Tafel slope. 
The data did not provide an exact fit to the equation and the deviation is thought to be related to 
the development of the oxide film that was formed during the conditioning of rebar in SPS, and 
similarly for rebar cast in mortar. Thus, the model was chang ed to incorporate this. Chang et al. 
(2008b) developed an empirical kinetic equation that considers the growth and dissolution of the 
SCE reference electrode
Working electrode (Rebar)
Counter electrode (graphite)
SCE reference electrode
Working electrode (Rebar)
Counter electrode (S.S. mesh)
sponge sheet
\nPDF_PAGE 87\n 
 
47 
rust film. The net current density due to the growth/dissolution of rust film is described by 
Equations (3.2) and (3.3): 
Anodic polarization: 
 
𝑖𝐹_𝑛𝑒𝑡 = 𝑖𝑐𝑜𝑟𝑟 ∙ √𝐸 − 𝐸𝑐𝑜𝑟𝑟𝑉𝑎 ∙ 10−(𝐸−𝐸𝑐𝑜𝑟𝑟)/𝛽𝑎 (3.2) 
Cathodic polarization: 
 
𝑖𝐹_𝑛𝑒𝑡 = −𝑖𝑐𝑜𝑟𝑟 ∙ √𝐸 − 𝐸𝑐𝑜𝑟𝑟𝑉𝑐 ∙ 10−(𝐸−𝐸𝑐𝑜𝑟𝑟)/𝛽𝑐 (3.3) 
where, 𝐸𝑐𝑜𝑟𝑟, 𝑖𝑐𝑜𝑟𝑟, 𝛽𝑎, 𝛽𝑐(negative) are corrosion parameters from the Bulter -Volmer equation. 
𝑉𝑎, 𝑉𝑐 (negative) are parameters indicating the rust film growth at a rate of 𝑖𝐹_𝑛𝑒𝑡 (negative) and 
dissolution at a rate of 𝑖𝐹_𝑛𝑒𝑡 (positive). 
The total net current is the summation of 𝑖𝑛𝑒𝑡  (Butler-Volmer equation) and 𝑖𝐹_𝑛𝑒𝑡  (film 
growth/dissolution equation), and is termed, in this paper, the Butler -Volmer-Film equation. All 
the scan data in this study was modelled with this equation using open -source software for 
interactive curve fitting on potentiodynamic scan data that h ad been previously developed by the 
authors (Li, Evitts, Boulfiza, et al., 2018). 
3.5 Results and discussion 
3.5.1 IR compensation noise 
Some preliminary tests were performed to develop the test settings for data acquisition. It was 
found that the use of a Faraday  shield and floating ground will significantly reduce the large 
potential oscillations caused by real-time IR compensation. Two examples of the use of these for 
rebar in 3% (wt%) NaCl solution are shown in Figure 3.4. 
\nPDF_PAGE 88\n 
 
48 
 
(a)  (b) 
Figure 3.4 The effect of Faraday shield on noise reduction caused by IR compensation, (a) an 
example of rebar sample in 3% (wt .%) NaCl solution and (b) an example of rebar cast in a 
mortar with 3% (wt .%) NaCl mixing water at ambient dry environment. The control 
datasets were acquired without the use of a Faraday shield. 
3.5.2 Open Circuit Potential (OCP) drift with mortar surface wetting 
Submersion or extensive surface wetting of the air-dried mortar samples was found to significantly 
alter the original open circuit potential. An example of this is presented in Figure 3.5, where the 
carbonated mortar sample was wrapped with a soaking-wet sponge while taking the OCP readings. 
The OCP drifted more than 100 mV within one hour and continue to drop afterwards. In fact, the 
OCP was measured at around -100 mV and drifted rapidly to -225 mV before the continuous 
reading started. The time to reach steady-state readings was denoted as the stabilization time. The 
stabilization time was found to vary from 30 minutes to more than 2 hours. Generally,  it took 
longer to stabilize if the sample was drier and had higher chloride content. A stabilization time is 
normal in a standard polarization test where a freshly polished metal is submerged in the test 
solution; it normally takes some time for the metal  to develop a stable or steady -state corrosion 
state. However, in the case of a mortar sample, the mortar is the electrolyte, and the corrosion state 
should have been stabilized prior to the external wetting of the exterior of the mortar. Within the 
stabilization period, the moisture front would likely have insufficient time to reach the rebar 
surface from the wetted external surface, and there is sufficient oxygen in the mortar to sustain the 
oxygen reduction half -reaction during the initial OCP process. I n the OCP logging process, the 
consumption rate of oxygen corresponds to the corrosion current density (𝑖𝑐𝑜𝑟𝑟), which is low. The 
partially wet concrete cover does not directly affect the microenvironment on the rebar surface , 
but it may affect the current distribution within that 2 cm thick mortar cover by altering the ionic 

\nPDF_PAGE 89\n 
 
49 
path and mass transfer (and corresponding electrical) resistance between the coupled anodic and 
cathodic sites. This could shift the localized potential of these sites and cause t he potential at the 
reference electrode contact point to drift. The use of a slightly moistened sponge thus minimize d 
this OCP drift. 
 
Figure 3.5 An example of open circuit potential (OCP) drift of the rebar  embedded in a 
carbonated mortar sample when the surface is wrapped with a wet sponge.  
 
3.5.3 Two-step scan vs. one-step scan 
A one-step scan procedure is the normal potentiodynamic scan procedure of ASTM G5-14, where 
the one-direction potential scan starts at the low end of the scan range from the cathodic branch 
and with a positive potential increment, scans towards the high en d of the anodic branch. A two -
step scan procedure consists of two separate anodic and cathodic scans. Both scans start from the 
OCP and scan in the positive direction for the anodic branch and in the negative direction for the 
cathodic branch. Figure 3.6 presents examples of scan curves from both scan procedures for the 
rebar samples in CSPS with 3% (wt%) NaCl. The potentiodynamic scan range is OCP ±150 mV. 
The scan curves were compared to the linear polarization resistance (LPR) scan curves, which is 
a one-step scan over a small-range potential (OCP±10mV) and considered to be “non-destructive”. 

\nPDF_PAGE 90\n 
 
50 
In this research the LPR scans were performed first and then followed by the potentiodynamic 
scans. The same sample was used to perform these scans. The sample was left to rest up to 24 
hours between the two potentiodynamic scans to allow the OCP to recover to the previous values.  
As shown in Figure 3.6, the one-step scan curve exhibits a “flat tail” where the cathodic branch 
scan starts. This flat tail occurs because a much greater current is required to sustain the -150 mV 
overpotential in the one-step scan procedure compared to the two-step scan procedure. This current 
surge, also observed by Chang et al. (2008a) , seems to significantly change the rebar surface 
condition. It caused 𝐸𝑐𝑜𝑟𝑟 to significantly shift from OCP in the negative direction, and distorted 
the cathodic scan curve to such an extent that the anodic branch was affected. The distortion effect 
on the anodic is confirmed by comparing it to the small range of “non -destructive” LPR scan 
curves. For the one-step scan (Figure 3.6a), the anodic branch deviates from the anodic portion of 
the LPR scan curve, which suggests that the rebar su rface condition has significantly changed 
during the cathodic scan history in the one-step scan. For the two-step scan (Figure 3.6b) because 
there was no immediate cathodic scan history, the anodic branch overlaps well with the LPR scan 
curve. 
 
(a) 
 
(b) 
Figure 3.6 Potentiodynamic scan curves of rebar in CSPS with 3% (wt%) NaCl solution: (a) 
One-step scan and (b) two-step scan. Note: the small difference in 𝑬𝒄𝒐𝒓𝒓 (less than 10 mV) of 
the two-step scan branches was offset in the plot for better comparison. 
When performing a one-step test with a scan range of from OCP -150 mV to OCP+150 mV, the 
reported 𝐸𝑐𝑜𝑟𝑟 shifts by 20–50 mV towards the negative direction from the OCP for rebar samples 
in both solutions and mortars, and sometimes even larger (about 100 mV).  This shift is more 

\nPDF_PAGE 91\n 
 
51 
pronounced for the tests in solutions. In addition, the distorted scan curve would also provide an 
inaccurate corrosion current density, 𝑖𝑐𝑜𝑟𝑟.  
In order to avoid these drawbacks, the two -step scan procedure was used in this resear ch. The 
cathodic branch was scanned followed by the anodic branch. Between each scan, ample time was 
given for the samples to restore to their original OCP naturally with a difference of less than 10 mV. 
The ample conditioning period was found to be four h ours for the tests in solutions and 24 hours 
for the tests in mortar with all air relative humidity conditions. The negligibly small drift in 
potential indicates the rebar surface change brought by the cathodic scan branch is reversible, but 
not within a s hort scan period. This reversible recovery of the rebar surface is further confirmed 
because there is no discrepancy between the LPR anodic branch and the anodic branch from a two-
step test as shown in Figure 3.6b. 
3.5.4 Depassivation time of rebar in the simulated solutions 
All the rebar samples were pre -oxidized in the simulated pore solution to form the passive film 
prior to exposure to chloride contamination and carbonation. It would take some time for the 
chloride ions to destroy the passive film. According to previous experiments by Ghods et al. (2010) 
where chloride was gradually added to the simulated pore solution, 𝐸𝑐𝑜𝑟𝑟 started to decrease when 
a critical chloride concentration of 0.1mol/L was reached and continued to decrease with an 
increase of chloride concentration of up to 1 mol/L. At each concentration increment, the corrosion 
potential fluctuated over a range of ±0.1 𝑉  within 100 -160 hours. The stabilization of the 
depassivation of the passive film likely depends on both chloride ion concentration and 
conditioning time. In the present study, the tests were performed for two depassivation periods:  
72 hours and 20 days for SPS, and 36 hours and 20 days for CSPS.  
For the SPS tests, as shown in Figure 3.7a, 𝐸𝑐𝑜𝑟𝑟 had higher variability for the repeated samples at 
72 hours, but at 20 days, this variability is dramatically reduced. After 20 days, 𝐸𝑐𝑜𝑟𝑟 became less 
noble 𝐸𝑐𝑜𝑟𝑟 and was not sensitive to chloride ion concentration greater than or equal to 2% (NaCl 
wt%). Above the 2% threshold , in SPS, a longer period of time is needed to achieve a stable 
depassivation of rebar in SPS with lower chloride ion concentrations compared to higher 
concentrations. However, eventually, both lower and higher concentrations will lead to the same 
depassivation result. This was not captured in the previous experiments (Ghods et al., 2010) where 
\nPDF_PAGE 92\n 
 
52 
the chloride was added in steps, and there was insufficient time between steps to enable changes 
in 𝐸𝑐𝑜𝑟𝑟  to be measured. Corresponding to the stabilized less noble 𝐸𝑐𝑜𝑟𝑟  after 20 days, the 
variation of 𝑖𝑐𝑜𝑟𝑟 for replicate samples is also de creased after 20 days. However, the 𝑖𝑐𝑜𝑟𝑟 value 
showed a slight decrease instead of a significant increase (Figure 3.8a). This suggests that in SPS, 
for chloride equal or greater than 2%, despite the ongoing depassivation indicated by 𝐸𝑐𝑜𝑟𝑟, there 
is no corresponding increase in 𝑖𝑐𝑜𝑟𝑟.  
 
(a) 
 
(b) 
Figure 3.7 The effect of the chloride concentration on the depassivation of rebar in simulated 
pore solutions: corrosion potential of pre -passivated rebar in (a) SPS for 72 hours and 20 
days and (b) CSPS for 36 hours and 20 days. The mean values are indicated by markers, and 
the error bars represent the range of  the three repeated test results. 
For the CSPS tests, as shown in Figure 3.7b, 𝐸𝑐𝑜𝑟𝑟 appeared to be stable with smal l variation for 
repeated tests both 36 hours and 20 days for various chloride concentrations. From 36 hours to 20 
days, there is an approximately 50 mV drop in 𝐸𝑐𝑜𝑟𝑟 which corresponds to a significant increase 
in 𝑖𝑐𝑜𝑟𝑟 (Figure 3.8b): it is about a three times increase in 𝑖𝑐𝑜𝑟𝑟 for chloride concentration larger 
than 1% (wt% NaCl).  
Both 𝑖𝑐𝑜𝑟𝑟 in SPS and CSPS at 20 days levels off and slightly decrease from chloride concentration 
at 2% (wt.% NaCl solution). However, there was a trend that shows an increase in 𝑖𝑐𝑜𝑟𝑟 for the 
shorter exposure period (72 h for SPS and 36 hours for CSPS, Figure 3.8). This suggests that the 
chloride concentration equal to or greater than 2% (wt.% NaCl solution) has reached the maximum 

\nPDF_PAGE 93\n 
 
53 
effect of depassivation in both SPS and CSPS. It is important to note that continuous depassivation 
occurs in CSPS, as indicated by a large increase in 𝑖𝑐𝑜𝑟𝑟 at 20 days. In conclusion, after the short 
term depassivation, the long-term depassivation additionally slightly decreases the 𝐸𝑐𝑜𝑟𝑟 for rebar 
in both SPS and CSPS but only significantly increases 𝑖𝑐𝑜𝑟𝑟 for rebar in CSPS.

(b)

pore solutions: corrosion current density of pre-passivated rebar in (a) SPS for 72 hours and 
20 days and (b) CSPS for 36 hours and 20 days. The mean values are indicated by markers, 
and the error bars represent the range of  the three repeated test results.  
 
3.5.5 Corrosion parameters for SPS and CSPS: the effect of chloride concentration and 
pH 
3.5.5.1 Corrosion potential and corrosion current density in SPS and CSPS 
The measured corrosion potentials and corrosion current densities for the rebars conditioned in 
solutions for 20 days are shown in Figure 3.9. The corrosion potential is lower at higher chloride 
ion concentrations (greater than 1% wt% NaCl) than that at lower chloride ion concentrations for 
both SPS and CSPS types. At approximately 2% (wt%) NaCl concentration and above, the 
corrosion potential is less dependent on the chloride ion concentration. It is interesting to notice 

\nPDF_PAGE 94\n 
 
54 
that the concentration invariant corrosion potentials for rebar in SPS are 100 -150 mV more 
negative than that for rebar in CSPS. 
The corrosion current densities of the pre -passivated rebar in both SPS and CSPS remain small 
(0.15 𝜇𝐴/𝑐𝑚2 for CSPS and 0.36 𝜇𝐴/𝑐𝑚2 for SPS) in the absence of chloride ions after 20 days 
of exposure comparing to their values during early short-term exposure (72 h for SPS and 36 hours 
for CSPS). After 20 days, for both SPS and CSPS, the rebar corrosion current densities increase 
with the chloride ion concentration and peak at 2% (wt%) and then slightly decrease at higher 
chloride ion concentration. 
In general, the rebar corrosion current densiti es in CSPS with chloride contamination was found 
to be approximately 3–10 times that of the SPS, even though the corrosion potentials are slightly 
more noble. 
 
(a) 
 
(b) 
Figure 3.9 Comparison of corrosion p otential (a) and corrosion current density (b) of pre -
passivated rebar in SPS and CSPS with chloride after 20 days of conditioning.  The mean 
values are indicated by markers, and the error bars represent the range of  the three repeated 
test results. 
 

\nPDF_PAGE 95\n 
 
55 
3.5.5.2 Anodic Tafel slopes in SPS and CSPS 
The anodic Tafel slopes of rebar corrosion in SPS and CSPS are shown in Figure 3.10. The fitted 
Tafel slopes for the rebar exclude the secondary reaction, i.e., film growth/dissolution Va/Vc. In 
SPS, for the first 72 hours, the anodic Tafel slope decreases with chloride ion concentration (Figure 
3.10a) but eventually converges to around 0.2–0.4 V/dec after 20 days of exposure.  
The anodic Tafel slope is the change of current corresponding t o the change of overpotential, 
contributed by both the dissolution of steel and the Fe2+/ Fe3+ conversion in the passive rust film. 
In SPS, at 20 days, when there is no chloride, the anodic slopes measured from the repeated tests 
varies from 0.2 V/dec to more than 1.0 V/dec. This large variation in the anodic Tafel slope could 
be caused by the competition between the iron dissolution reaction and the change of passivated 
film and the latter could be more significant when there was no chloride ion in the solution and the 
rebar sample remained passivated. When chloride ion is present in SPS, the anodic Tafel slopes 
measured from the repeated tests become more uniform and exhibit a range of around 0.2 –0.3 
V/dec, and it slightly increases with chloride concentra tion up to 3.0% (wt.%) NaCl solution and 
then slightly decreases at higher chloride ion concentrations. This value is significantly larger than 
89 mV for non-prepassivated rebar immersed in seawater for one hour (Chang et al., 2008b). This 
suggests the protective passivated film on the rebar significantly increases the anodic Tafel slope 
in chloride-containing solution with high pH.  
In CSPS, the anodic Tafel slope is shallow when there is no chloride ion, but it does not indicate 
the depassivation of the rebar as 𝐸𝑐𝑜𝑟𝑟 is about -0.1 V (Figure 3.9). Instead, it shows the current 
density on the rebar, excluding the contribution of the oxide film change, is sensitive to the 
overpotential before the net current density reached the passivated current density. When chloride 
ion is present, the anodic Tafel slope tends to stay near 0.4–0.5 V/dec, which is higher than that in 
SPS.  
It is noteworthy that the presence of chloride ions resulted in a higher anodic slope for the rebar in 
CSPS, which has a lower pH of 8.0 compared to SPS with a pH of 13.5. It is hypothesized that the 
corrosion accumulation on the rebar in CSPS with chloride ions led to an increase in the anodic 
Tafel slope. As shown in Figure 3.10b, at 36 hours, the anodic Tafel slope is around 0.1–0.2 V/dec 
\nPDF_PAGE 96\n 
 
56 
and it increased to 0.4–0.5 V/dec at 20 days with the rust buildup observed on the rebar surface in 
CSPS with chloride ions. However, it is no ticed in another study (Chang et al., 2008b)  that the 
four-year rust build -up on the rebar in neutral sea water only slightly caused an increase in the 
anodic Tafel slope from 0.089 V/dec to 0.115 V/dec. Therefore, the larger anodic Tafel slope 
observed in CSPS could be attributed to both the rust b uildup and the pH -buffering carbonate-
bicarbonate couple that is absent in the sea water. 
 
(a) 
 
(b) 
Figure 3.10 The effect of the chloride concentration on the depassivation of rebar in 
simulated pore solutions: anodic Tafel slopes of pre-passivated rebar in (a) SPS for 72 hours 
and 20 days (b) CSPS for 36 hours and 20 days. The mean values are indicated by markers, 
and the error bars represent the range of  the three repeated test results. 
This anodic Tafel slope increase accompanied by rust build-up was not significant for rebar in SPS 
at 20 days, but the depassivation process was. After 20 days of conditioning, the anodic slope did 
not increase but instead decreased to 0.2 –0.3 V/dec, which is significantl y lower than that at 72 
hours(Figure 3.10a). This suggests that chloride -induced depassivation is an ongoing process 
between 72 days and 20 days. The difference in the degree of depassivation at these two times (72 
days and 20 days) is greater when the concentration of chloride ions is low. This also shows that 
it needs a longer time for the rebar’s continuing depassivation to develop for a small chloride ion 
concentration.  

\nPDF_PAGE 97\n 
 
57 
To summarize, the anodic Tafel slope behaves differently in SPS and CSPS with chloride ions due 
to a competing effect of dep assivation and rust build-up: In SPS, chloride slowly depassivate the 
rebar and finally reaches down to a similar value, but in CSPS, chloride quickly depassivate the 
rebar, and the anodic Tafel slope drifts to a higher value with the rust build-up. 
3.5.5.3 Cathodic Tafel slope in SPS and CSPS 
The cathodic Tafel slope is related to the oxygen reduction reaction. The cathodic Tafel slope value 
is affected by both the chloride ion concentration and the conditioning time. As shown in Figure 
3.11, for the short-term conditioning time (72 hours for SPS and 36 hours for CSPS), the cathodic 
Tafel slope |𝛽𝑐| tends to increase with the chloride content from around 0.2  V/dec to 1.0 V/dec. 
The large slope at high chloride ion concentration indicated the concentration polarization, where 
the oxygen concentration was significantly reduced on the rebar surface due to the limited mass 
transfer rate of the dissolved oxygen.  
For the rebar in the chloride-containing solutions for both SPS and CSPS, the cathodic Tafel slope 
|𝛽𝑐| decreased after a long -term condition of 20 days, when the disruption of passive oxide film 
stabilized over time. At 20 days, |𝛽𝑐| was 0.23 V/dec in SPS with no chloride ions, and increased 
to 0.40 V/dec with chloride concentration increasing up to 2% (wt%) NaCl and then levelled off 
with a slight decrease to 0.38 V at 3% and further to 0.33 V/dec at 5% with a larger variance. In 
CSPS with no chloride ions, |𝛽𝑐| is 0.13 V/dec, and it gradually increased to about 0.55 V/dec with 
chloride concentration increasing from 0.5% to 5% (wt%) NaCl. 
The change of |𝛽𝑐| can be explained by the change of mill scale and protective film. In the short 
term, when the chloride io ns are present, oxygen in the mill scale is consumed faster due to a 
higher corrosion rate, which caused the concentration polarization, hence a large |𝛽𝑐| in the 
chloride-rich solutions. With continuous depassivation and corrosion, the protective film and mill 
scale were destroyed, which facilitated the transport of dissolved oxygen to the steel surface, hence 
a decreased |𝛽𝑐|. This oxygen-obstructing effect is more significant with a protective film formed 
at high pH. In neutral solution such as seawater,  where no protective film is formed on the rebar 
surface, the large |𝛽𝑐| only decreased slightly from 1.215 V/dec to 0.9 V/dec for rebar with 1-hour 
and 3.9-year exposure (Chang et al., 2008b). In CSPS, The decrease of |𝛽𝑐| is less significant for 
\nPDF_PAGE 98\n 
 
58 
the rebar sample with high chloride concentration (5% wt.%) due to a significant build rust on the 
surface after the disruption of mill scale and protective film. 
To summarize, the cathodic Tafel slope |𝛽𝑐| increases with the chlor ide concentration in short -
term exposure in SPS and CSPS. This correlation becomes less dramatic and  |𝛽𝑐|  overall 
decreases with the gradual disruption of the mill scale and protective film after long-term exposure.

hours and 20 days (b) CSPS for 36 hours and 20 days. The mean values are indicated by

3.5.6 Corrosion parameters in mortar: the effect of chloride content and conditioning RH 
3.5.6.1 Corrosion potential 
For both non-carbonated and carbonated mortars, the corrosion potential drops to more negative 
with the increase of chloride content, which indicates more active corrosion. The relative humidity 
conditioning levels significantly influence the effect of chloride content on the corrosion potential. 
This partially explains the large scatter of the critical chloride values reported in the literature (U. 
Angst et al., 2009). 

\nPDF_PAGE 99\n 
 
59 
As shown in Figure 3.12a, in the non-carbonated mortar samples, the corrosion potential decreases 
more rapidly with the increase of chloride content at a larger RH. For a given chloride content, the 
corrosion potential is a function of the conditioning RH of the mortar: the higher the RH, the less 
noble the rebar is, and the RH effects are more pronounced with higher chloride content. In the 
drier mortars (e.g. conditioned at 65% RH) with a chloride content of 2.24% (level 5) by weight 
of cement will only drop the 𝐸𝑐𝑜𝑟𝑟 to -200 mV, which can only be characterized as “uncertain” by 
ASTM criteria (ASTM International, 2015) , whereas, at a relative humidity of 95%, the same 
chloride content brings corrosion potential to “corrosion probability greater than 90%” category , 
and furthermore, in the saturated condition, only chloride content level 2 (0.43% by weight of 
cement) will cause the 𝐸𝑐𝑜𝑟𝑟 to fall in “corrosion probability greater than 90%”, which means the 
𝐸𝑐𝑜𝑟𝑟 is greatly affected by the moisture content. This raises the precautions to the selection of the 
field half -cell survey timing to capture the worst -case scenario, where seasonal fluctuation of 
moisture content is suspected.  
For carbonated mortar ( Figure 3.12b), the corrosion potential 𝐸𝑐𝑜𝑟𝑟 changes with the chloride 
content in two stages: it drops more rapidly with a small amount of chloride added (0.21%–0.43% 
by weight of cement) than that in a non -carbonated mortar. When the chloride content is higher 
(0.43%–2.24% by weight of cement), the decrease of 𝐸𝑐𝑜𝑟𝑟 with chloride content in the carbonated 
mortar become less significant. That is because the carbonated mortar has a pH of around 8, and 
at this pH the passive film becomes unstable. The additional chloride has less contribution in 
“activating” the corrosion on the rebar since the rebar has already been largely activated.  
The effect of chloride content on 𝐸𝑐𝑜𝑟𝑟 are also influenced by the conditioning RH for the mortar 
samples. Generally, the increase in the conditioning moisture  reduced 𝐸𝑐𝑜𝑟𝑟, but with a different 
pattern between carbonated and non -carbonated mortar samples. For the carbonated mortars 
(Figure 3.12b), the 𝐸𝑐𝑜𝑟𝑟 is similar for the samples with the same chloride content conditioned at 
RH of 65% and 85%. When the conditioning RH increased to 95%, 𝐸𝑐𝑜𝑟𝑟 slightly decreased for 
the chloride-containing carbonated samples, and significantly decreased in the carbonated mortars 
with no chloride contamination. Under the submerged condition, the 𝐸𝑐𝑜𝑟𝑟  is reduced to 
around -550 mV – -600 mV for carbonated samples with all chloride levels, indicating active 
corroding.  
\nPDF_PAGE 100\n 
 
60 
For non-carbonated mortar samples, as shown in Figure 3.12a, the 𝐸𝑐𝑜𝑟𝑟 declines with the increase 
in conditioning RH at all chloride levels. The greater the chloride content, the greater reduction in 
𝐸𝑐𝑜𝑟𝑟 caused by the increase in RH. The most dramatic 𝐸𝑐𝑜𝑟𝑟 reduction was found in the mortar 
under the submerged co ndition when the chloride content increased from 0.21% to 0.43% (by 
weight of cement), appearing as a threshold chloride content to cause depassivation. 
 
(a) 
 
(b) 
Figure 3.12 The effect of the chloride content and conditioning RH on the corrosion potential 
of rebar in (a) non -carbonated mortar and (b) carbonated mortar, after two months of 
conditioning under RH of 65%, 85%, 95%, and submerged for 7 days. The mean values are 
indicated by markers, and the err or bars represent the range of  the three repeated test 
results. 
In summary, for non-carbonated mortar under partially saturated conditions, the effect of chloride 
does not show a “threshold” effect on depassivation, but under saturated conditions, it shows a 
“threshold” effect by a dramatic decrease in 𝐸𝑐𝑜𝑟𝑟 with chloride content. Whereas it is the opposite 
in carbonated mortars, the threshold level is evident under partially saturated conditions, but there 
was not much change in the 𝐸𝑐𝑜𝑟𝑟 under the saturated condition. It is worth mentioning that the 
carbonated mortar alone effectively depassivated the rebar in the saturated condition while the 
CSPS did not depassivate the rebar when there is no chloride. 

\nPDF_PAGE 101\n 
 
61 
3.5.6.2 Corrosion current density 
The corrosion current density of the rebar embedded in mortar increased with the chloride content. 
However, the conditioning humidity affects the sensitivity of the current density’s dependence on 
the chloride contents in different ways for the carbonated mortar samples and the non-carbonated 
samples. For the non-carbonated mortar samples, as shown in Figure 3.13a, the corrosion current 
density increases with the chloride content in all humidity condition levels, and the trends are 
similar to the inverse of the 𝐸𝑐𝑜𝑟𝑟 in Figure 3.12a.  
  
(a) 
 
(b) 
Figure 3.13 The effect of the chloride content and conditioning RH on the corrosion current 
density of rebar in (a) non-carbonated mortar and (b) carbonated mortar, after two months 
of conditioning under RH of 65%, 85%, 95%, and submerged for 7 days . The mean values 
are indicated by markers, and the error bars represent the range of  the three repeated test 
results. 
For the carbonated mortar samples, as shown in Figure 3.13b, the “two-stage” trend found in the 
𝐸𝑐𝑜𝑟𝑟 does not appear for 𝑖𝑐𝑜𝑟𝑟 under drier condition s (RH of 65 –85%). The corrosion current 
density continues to increase for large chloride content (0.43% –2.24% by weight of cement), 
where the 𝐸𝑐𝑜𝑟𝑟 tends to level off. This phenomenon suggests that besides the depassivation effect 
by chloride, there are other factors that contribute to the corrosion current density and at the same 
time have less effect on 𝐸𝑐𝑜𝑟𝑟 . These factors are thought to be the moisture content and the 
resistivity. The moisture content and resistivity are concurring phenomena,  but they may have 

\nPDF_PAGE 102\n 
 
62 
different mechanistic effects on the corrosion rate. These effects will be discussed in the next 
chapter (Chapter 4). 
3.5.6.3 Anodic Tafel slopes 
For the non-carbonated mortar samples ( Figure 3.14a), under the partially -saturated conditions 
(RH of 65%–95%), the anodic Tafel slope increases with the chloride content until level 2 (0.43% 
by weight of cement), and after that (0.43% – 2.24% by weight of cement), the Tafel slopes 
fluctuates with no clear trend. For the submerged condition, the Tafel slopes significantly increase 
to a large value (1.0 –2.2 V/dec) with a large variance at chloride level 3 (0.87% by weight of 
cement) where the 𝐸𝑐𝑜𝑟𝑟 drops to -650 mV, and then decreases with the increase of chloride 
content.  
 
(a) 
 
(b) 
Figure 3.14 The effect of the chloride content and conditioning RH on the anodic Tafel slope

conditioning under RH of 65%, 85%, 95%, and submerged for 7 days. The mean values are 
indicated by markers, and the error bars represent the range of  the three repeated test 
results. 
For the carbonated mortar samples ( Figure 3.14b), the anodic Tafel slope does not appear a 
significant change with chloride content for the submerged condition. Samples conditioned at RH 
of 65% and 85% have similar Tafel slopes ranging from 0.3–0.7 V/dec. The samples stored at RH

of 95% have an overall higher anodic Tafel slope (0.8–1.0 V/dec) than the rest when the chloride 
content is larger than level 2 (0.43% by weight of cement). Under the submerged condition, the 
Tafel slope does not increase significantly when the chloride content is greater than level 3 (0.87% 
by weight of cement). 
3.5.6.4 Cathodic Tafel slopes 
For the non -carbonated mortar samples ( Figure 3.15a) under partially saturated conditions, the 
absolute value of cathodic Tafel slope, |𝛽𝑐|, increases with chloride content, but for RH of 85% 
and 95%, it tends to level off around 0.28 V/dec after chloride content reaches level 4 (1.13% by 
weight of cement). Under the saturated condition, the |𝛽𝑐| increases to its peak value (0.30 -
0.42 V/dec) at the chloride content level 2 (0.43% by weight of cement) and then decreases to 
around 0.22 V/dec for higher chloride content. 
 
(a) 
 
(b) 
Figure 3.15 The effect of the c hloride content and conditioning RH on the cathodic Tafel

conditioning under RH of 65%, 85%, 95%, and submerged for 7 days. The mean values are

results. 
For the carbonated mortar samples (Figure 3.15b) under partially saturated conditions, there is no 
clear trend of |𝛽𝑐| with respect to chloride content. |𝛽𝑐| ranges from 0.30–0.55 V/dec, higher than

the non-carbonated counterpart. Under the submerged conditions, it shows a similar pattern of the 
non-carbonated samples. It peaked at the chloride content level 2 (0.43% by weight of cement) 
and then declines with a large variation to around 0.32 V/dec. 
3.6 Conclusions 
An assessment of the impact of the major environmental parameters such as chloride concentration, 
carbonation state and exposure  moisture and duration, on the measured corrosion parameters 
(corrosion potential, corrosion rate, and Tafel slopes) of rebars in a simulated concrete pore 
solution and in mortar has been carried out. An improved three -electrode potentiodynamic test 
procedure was developed to measure the corrosion parameters of rebar in simulated pore solutions 
and mortar with various chloride concentrations and pH. The impact of using a Faraday shield, 
real-time IR compensation, and a two -step scan routine was also evaluat ed. The findings of this 
study are as follows: 
It was found that the use of a Faraday shield and floating ground significantly reduced the large 
electrical potential oscillations caused by real -time IR compensation. The air -dried rebar -
embedded mortar samp les were tested in a non -submerged setting to avoid the open circuit 
potential drifting due to extensive surface wetting of samples. It was also found that it was 
necessary to perform separate anodic and cathodic scans with adequate rest time in order to 
accurately determine the corrosion parameters. 
In chloride-contaminated SPS and CSPS  
1) The depassivation and rust build -up were simultaneous processes, and they both affected 
the corrosion parameters over the observed periods (36, 72 hours and 20 days). Relative to 
the short term depassivation (36 hours for CSPS, 72 hours for SPS), the long-term (20 days) 
depassivation slightly de creased the 𝐸𝑐𝑜𝑟𝑟 for both SPS and CSPS but only significantly 
increased 𝑖𝑐𝑜𝑟𝑟 for rebar in CSPS. There exist ed a chloride concentration (2% wt.% for 
SPS, and 1% wt.% for CSPS), above which higher chloride co ncentration had an 
insignificant long-term (20 days) effect on 𝐸𝑐𝑜𝑟𝑟 and 𝑖𝑐𝑜𝑟𝑟. At 20 days the corrosion current 
densities in CSPS were approximately 3 -10 times those of the SPS, even though the 
corrosion potentials were slightly more noble.  
\nPDF_PAGE 105\n 
 
65 
 
2) The competing effect of depassivation and rust build-up affects the anodic Tafel slope. The 
anodic Tafel slope in SPS decrease d with the chloride concentration at 72 hours and  
reached a similar value (0.2–0.3 V/dec) for all chloride concentrations at 20 days. In CSPS, 
the anodic Tafel slope reached similar values for all chloride concentrations and drifted to 
a higher value (0.4–0.5 V/dec) at 20 days with the rust build-up. 
 
3) The gradual disruption of the mill scale and protective film affect s the cathodic reduction 
reaction. The cathodic Tafel slope |𝛽𝑐| increased with chloride concentration in both SPS 
and CSPS but with a large variance for short-term exposure. More stable and less scattered 
values were found at 20 days. The |𝛽𝑐| increased with the chloride concentration in CSPS 
but only increased with chloride concentration up to 2% (wt.%)  from 0.23 V/dec to 0.38 
V/dec in SPS followed by levelling off at higher chloride concentrations. 
In the non-carbonated mortar and carbonated mortar contaminated with chlorides 
1) For both non-carbonated and carbonated mortars, the corrosion potential  𝐸𝑐𝑜𝑟𝑟 decreases 
to more negative with the increase of chloride content. In non -carbonated mortars under 
partially saturated conditions with chloride content up to 2.24% by weight of cement, there 
was not a threshold chloride content as seen in SPS.  However, in saturated conditions, it 
showed a “threshold” chloride content  (0.21% – 0.43% by weight of cement ), similar to 
the behaviour in SPS. For the carbonated mortar samples, the chloride content threshold 
showed under the partially saturated conditions, but the 𝐸𝑐𝑜𝑟𝑟 were similar in all chloride 
levels under the saturated condition and were more noble than the non-carbonated mortars 
just like the case in SPS and CSPS. 
 
2) An increase in the conditioning RH results in a decrease in 𝐸𝑐𝑜𝑟𝑟, but its extent depends on 
the carbonation state and chloride content. The most significant decrease in 𝐸𝑐𝑜𝑟𝑟 was 
found in the non-carbonated mortars with large chloride content and the carbonated mortars 
with no chloride. 
 
\nPDF_PAGE 106\n 
 
66 
3) Compared to the non -carbonated samples, the corrosion current densities  (𝑖𝑐𝑜𝑟𝑟) were 
significantly higher in the carbonated ones in drier c onditions with smaller chloride 
content. Increasing chloride content and RH minimized the difference. 
 
4) In non-carbonated mortar, 𝑖𝑐𝑜𝑟𝑟 increased with higher chloride content across all humidity 
conditions, similar to the inverse trend of 𝐸𝑐𝑜𝑟𝑟. However, in carbonated mortar under drier 
conditions (RH 65-85%), the two-stage level off trend observed in 𝐸𝑐𝑜𝑟𝑟 did not appear in 
𝑖𝑐𝑜𝑟𝑟 . 𝑖𝑐𝑜𝑟𝑟  continued to rise with larger chloride content (0.43% -2.24% by weight of 
cement), whil e 𝐸𝑐𝑜𝑟𝑟  levelled off. This indicates that factors other than chloride 
depassivation, such as moisture content and resistivity, contribute to corrosion current 
density with a lesser impact on 𝐸𝑐𝑜𝑟𝑟. Further discussion on these factors will be presented 
in the upcoming chapter (Chapter 4). 
 
5) In both non-carbonated and carbonated mortar, the anodic Tafel slopes 𝛽𝑎 did not show a 
clear dependence on chloride content and condition ing RH and had a range from 0.3 -1.0 
V/dec. The exceptional high 𝛽𝑎 values were found in non -carbonated mortar under the 
submerged condition with higher chloride content. 
 
6) Under partially saturated conditions, for non -carbonated mortar, the cathodic Tafel slope 
|𝛽𝑐| increased with the chloride content and level led off at higher chloride content. The 
|𝛽𝑐| is significantly larger in carbonated mortars (0.30 –0.55 V/dec) than that in non -
carbonated mortars (0.12–0.30 V/dec), but there is no clear concluded trend in carbonated 
mortar.  
 
7) Under saturated conditions in both types of mortars, |𝛽𝑐| had a peak value when chloride 
content reaches 0.43% by weight of cement and then decrease d with higher chloride 
content. 
3.7 Epilogue 
This chapter investigates the effects of chloride content, ca rbonation, and moisture conditions on 
the corrosion parameters of rebar in simulated pore solutions and mortar. It sheds light on the 
underlying mechanisms of how these factors affect corrosion kinetics, which are essential 
\nPDF_PAGE 107\n 
 
67 
considerations for the numerical simulation phase. Additionally, it uncovers plausible synergistic 
effects of chloride, water saturation, and carbonation on the corrosion parameters of rebar in mortar, 
which will be further discussed in the next chapter.

68 
4 Carbon Steel Rebar Corrosion in Mortar: the Interactive Effect of Moisture, Chloride, 
and Carbonation on Rebar Corrosion 
4.1 Preface 
In Chapter 3, we explored the influence of environmental parameters, including chloride content, 
carbonation, and relative humidity, on the corrosion rate and potential of rebars in mortar. The 
depassivation effects of chloride content and carbonation were  found to be influenced by the 
relative humidity to which the samples were exposed. Building upon these findings, Chapter 4 
investigates the combined effects of chloride content, carbonation, and moisture conditions on 
rebar corrosion parameters in mortar.  
The porous nature of mortar and the variable water saturation levels are the distinctive features 
that may cause the different corrosion behaviour of rebar in mortar than in bulk simulated pore 
solutions. The roles of water saturation in mortar are the c hange of volume fraction of the pore 
solution, the change of bulk resistivity of mortar, and the change of the chemical concentration. 
All of them can affect the apparent corrosion behaviour of rebar in mortar. However, to develop a 
mechanistic corrosion model, the root cause should be unravelled and considered in the model. 
This chapter will have a detailed analysis of the previous corrosion parameters measured in mortar 
by examining the water retention curves and bulk resistivity of these mortar samples. In this 
analysis, the effect of the water saturation level on corrosion was isolated from the combined effect 
brought by chloride contamination, carbonation and their effects on bulk resistivity and water 
retention capacity.  
It was determined that water saturation level affects corrosion rates due to three causes: 1) an area 
effect by the influenced rebar-pore solution area, 2) an ohmic control effect by the influenced bulk 
resistivity, and  3) an anode-to-cathode ratio effect.  
In non-carbonated mortar, when there is no chloride, the main contributor to rebar corrosion rate 
is the area effect. When there is chloride present, in addition to the area effect, there also exists an 
ohmic control effect and anode-to-cathode ratio effect. The chloride-induced rebar depassivation 
leads to non -uniform corrosion with a varying anode -to-cathode ratio dependent on water 
\nPDF_PAGE 109\n 
 
69 
saturation. This non -uniform corrosion cell is at a scale that is under ohmic controlled with a 
varying bulk resistivity which is also dependent on water saturation. 
In carbonated mortar, the rebar corrosion behaviour is affected by both the anode-to-cathode ratio 
and the surface area. The rust scale on the rebar affects the anode-to-cathode ratio and is influenced 
by the moisture level. When the rust is  dry, it is in a passivation state, and when it is wet, it is in 
an active state, which results in the dependence of the corrosion potential on the water saturation 
level in carbonated mortar. This dependence is more pronounced when the chloride content is low, 
as the conductivity of the rust is highly dependent on water content, and less significant when the 
chloride content is high. The non -uniform corrosion cell occurs on a scale that is not sensitive to 
bulk resistivity, hence less ohmic controlled. 
The manuscript of this chapter was submitted to “Construction and Building Materials” as a 
companion paper to the manuscript of Chapter 3. Gang Li is the primary author who designed and 
performed the experimental study and data analysis. Dr. Moh Boulfiza and Dr. Richard Evitts 
contributed to the review of the manuscript and provided feedback. 
4.2 Introduction 
In Chapter 3, the impact of environmental parameters on the corrosion rate and potential of carbon 
steel rebar cast in mortar was examined . A modified three -electrode potentiodynamic test was 
performed on the pre -passivated carbon steel rebars cast in mortar samples with six levels of 
chloride content that each contained a different concentration of chloride ion. The mortar samples 
were also carbonated or non -carbonated at each chloride ion concentration. The samples were 
conditioned at three relative humidity (RH 60%, 80%, 95%) and under fully submerged conditions. 
The corrosion potential and corrosion current density were tested by a potentiodynamic scanning 
technique that separately measured the cathodic and anodic current -voltage relationship around 
open circuit potentials. 
It was found that the depassivation effects of chloride content and carbonation on rebar corrosion 
in mortar were influenced by the relative humidity that the test samples were exposed to. An 
increase in the conditioning RH resulted in a decrease in corrosion potential 𝐸𝑐𝑜𝑟𝑟 and in increase 
in corrosion current density 𝑖𝑐𝑜𝑟𝑟, but its extent depended on the carbonation state and chloride 
\nPDF_PAGE 110\n 
 
70 
content. For non-carbonated mortar samples, the decrease of 𝐸𝑐𝑜𝑟𝑟 and the increase of 𝑖𝑐𝑜𝑟𝑟 with 
the increase of RH was more pronounced in mo rtar with large chloride content. For carbonated 
mortar samples, on the contrary, the decrease of 𝐸𝑐𝑜𝑟𝑟 and the increase of 𝑖𝑐𝑜𝑟𝑟 with the increase 
of RH was more pronounced in mortar with no chloride.  
4.2.1 Dependence of water saturation on RH in concrete 
In this paper, instead of the conditioning RH, the water saturation and volumetric water content 
are used to investigate the effect of moisture on corrosion potential, 𝐸𝑐𝑜𝑟𝑟 and corrosion current 
density, 𝑖𝑐𝑜𝑟𝑟 under the varying chloride contents and carbonation states.  The purpose is to steer 
away from correlating the relationship of 𝐸𝑐𝑜𝑟𝑟 and 𝑖𝑐𝑜𝑟𝑟  with RH, which does not represent the 
actual moisture level in mortar because the equilibrium moisture contents of the mortars under the 
conditioning environment is influenced by the ion concentrations in pore solution and porosity.  
For mortar samples, the ion concentrations in pore solution increases when sodium chloride is 
added to the mortar mix. When carbonation occurs, the carbonation reaction significantly reduces 
the ionic concentration of OH−, and the resultant hydrous silica gel from decalcification of C-S-H 
absorbs ions such as Na+, K+ (Pu et al., 2012). Accompanying the reduction in ion concentrations, 
carbonation also leads to possible porosity change, most likely a decreased porosity because the 
molar volume of the carbonation product, calcite is about 10% greater than that of calcium 
hydroxide, but it could also be an increased porosity with coarsened pore structure, especially in 
concrete made with pozzolanic supplementary cementitious materials where the calcium 
hydroxide content is relatively low and the excessive consumption of calcium hydroxide l eads to 
significant decalcification of C-S-H (Borges et al., 2010; Ngala & Page, 1997; Shah et al., 2018). 
There exist interactions between the retained moisture content, RH, ion concentration, pore size is 
discussed as follows. The relationship between the equilibrated relative humidity (RH) and the 
radius of the smallest empty pore given by the Kelvin equation (Kontogeorgis & Kiil, 2016).

where 𝑝𝑣[𝑃𝑎] is vapour pressure, 𝑝𝑠𝑎𝑡[𝑃𝑎] is saturated vapour pressure above a flat surface at 
temperature 𝑇[𝐾] , 𝛾[𝑁/𝑚] is surface tension, 𝑉𝑚[𝑚3/𝑚𝑜𝑙] is molar volume, 𝑟[𝑚] is radius of a 
\nPDF_PAGE 111\n
