critical pore radius 𝑟𝑝  i.e. the radius of the largest saturated capillary pores by 
   𝑟𝑝 = |𝑟|/𝑐𝑜𝑠𝛼  (4.2) 
where, the parameter 𝛼 is the contact angle determined by the type of liquid and the solid surface. 
   
Figure 4.1 Schematic representation of the meniscus formation of water in a capillary pore. 
For a given relative humidity (RH = 𝑝𝑣/𝑝𝑠𝑎𝑡), the capillary pores with a pore size smaller than 𝑟𝑝 
will be saturated. Therefore, concrete with a refined pore structure would retain more water at a 
certain RH. Therefore, the carbonation process of mortar will increase the water retention if it 
results in a reduced porosity and a refined pore structure. 
The concrete pore solution is not pure water. It contains various salt  solutes of different 
concentrations. According to Raoult's Law:  
 Δ𝑝 = 𝑝𝑣𝑥𝐵 (4.3) 
where, 𝑝𝑣[𝑃𝑎] is vapour pressure of water; Δ𝑝[𝑃𝑎] is the decrease in the vapour pressure of water 
by the addition of solute B with a molar fraction 𝑥𝐵. A solution with a higher ion concentration 

\nPDF_PAGE 112\n 
 
72 
will have a lower saturated vapo ur pressure at which the equilibrium between vapo ur and liquid 
water is established on the solution surface (Schmitz, 2017) . The pore solution has a lower 
equilibrium vapour pressure, and the measured RH with respect to the saturated vapour pressure 
above a flat surface 𝑝𝑠𝑎𝑡 is lower than 100% above the solution-air surface. In Equation (4.1), this 
is reflected as a lower 𝑝𝑠𝑎𝑡, or an increased 
𝑝𝑣
𝑝𝑠𝑎𝑡
 in the pores, which leads to an increased 𝑟 and an 
increased 𝑟𝑝 in Equation (4.2). This means that for a given RH, additional larger pores will be 
saturated due to the existence of salt. Therefore, for the same pore structure, concrete will retain 
more solution with a lower RH as defined with respect to a flat surface of pure water.  
In summary, for a given RH, an increase in chloride content will result in mortar retaining more 
water. Carbonation may reduce the salt concentration in mortar pore solution leading to reduced 
water retention, but it is possible that its refinement of the pore structure cause increased water 
retention.  
4.2.2 Dependence of rebar corrosion on water saturation in concrete 
In the context of rebar corrosion, the two main effects brought by water content variation in 
concrete is the change of the bulk resistivity and the rebar-pore solution interface area. In drier 
concrete, the reduced number of saturated pores causes the reduction in the rebar -pore solution 
interface, and the reduced interconnection between the saturated pores is the main reason for the 
dramatic incr ease in bulk resistivity, although the ion concentration in pore solution may be 
increased.  
The simultaneously occurring change in rebar-pore solution interface area and the change in mortar 
bulk resistivity have distinct mechanistic effects on the corros ion rate. Firstly, area effect: 
corrosion mainly occurs on rebar surfaces exposed to water -saturated pores, and the corrosion 
current density is expressed over the entire surface area of the rebar at a macroscopic level. 
Therefore, for drier concrete, a lower water saturation level means less rebar surface area exposed 
to the pore solution, which leads to a lower corrosion current density over the entire rebar surface. 
Secondly, ohmic control effect: the increase in bulk resistance of concrete between anodi c and 
cathodic sites on the corroding rebar, leads to a decrease in corrosion current through ohmic control. 
Ohmic control takes place whenever the ionic current of a corrosion cell flows through the porous, 
\nPDF_PAGE 113\n 
 
73 
saturated pore network. It can be reasoned that this type of ohmic control in porous medium 
diminishes when corrosion is nearly uniform, and the ionic current path of coupling anodic and 
cathodic reactions occur locally in the same pore without going through the network. However, 
non-uniform corrosion a t a macroscopic scale often happens in chloride -contaminated, non -
carbonated concrete with or without cracks (Angst et al., 2011; Raupach, 1996).  
The area effect and ohmic control effect are concurring. For example, in a corrosion study of rebar 
embedded in mortar samples with and without 2% Cl (López & González, 1993) , when the 
saturation level is below 0.75, the resistivity of mortar increased exponentially with decreasing 
water content which coincides with a linear decrease in corrosion rate. The au thors correlated the 
corrosion current density change to resistivity citing ohmic control, but the reduced rebar -pore 
solution interface area should also contribute to the corrosion current density change.  
In empirical corrosion models, corrosion is often related to the resistivity of concrete (Alonso et 
al., 1988; Liu & Weyers, 1998; Yu, Liu, & Chen, 2017) . The area effect can be considered 
mathematically lumped into resistivity since rebar-pore solution area and resistivity are correlated. 
In numerical models that model electrochemical reactions, the area reduction is usually not 
considered distinctly (Cao et al., 2013; Ghods et al., 2007; Isgor & Razaqpur, 2006; Kranc et al., 
1992; Yu et al., 2014). This effect is mathematically covered through the selected size and/or ratio 
of the anodic and cathodic sites and corrosion parameters. Only a few models have considered this 
effect by factoring the anodic/cathodic exchange current density by the liquid volu me fraction 
(Dangla & Dridi, 2009). 
Admittedly, these two controlling mechanisms are ubiquitous in the rebar corrosion context, but 
the significance of each varies. Generally, it is expected that ohmic control effect is less significant 
in carbonated reinforced concrete compared to the area effect. In a setup of a thin carbon steel wire 
(∅0.5 mm) embedded in a thin carbonated mortar sample (6 mm), the area reduction mechanism 
was found to control the corrosion of carbon steel in a porous medium with near -neutral pore 
solutions (Stefanoni et al., 2019) . However, even for sound carbonated concrete where the pore 
solution is considered more uniform, as -received carbon steel rebars usually come with non -
uniform surface conditions (Stefanoni et al., 2015), and also the rebar-concrete interface exhibits 
non-uniform conditions (Angst et al., 2019). Nonuniformity would most likely create coupling of 
\nPDF_PAGE 114\n 
 
74 
the corrosion cells across different pores at a distance where, depending on the saturation level, 
the resistance change between anodic and cathodic sites might not be negligible, hence ohmic 
control. The unanswered question is what the extent of the influence of saturation’s area effect and 
ohmic control effect is on controlling the corrosion rate in concrete with varying chloride content 
and carbonation states. 
4.2.3 Objective 
This research aims for investigating the role of moisture on rebar corrosion will be highlighted in 
terms of ohmic control and the rebar-pore solution surface area. The relationship will be explored 
between corrosion parameters such as corrosion potential 𝐸𝑐𝑜𝑟𝑟, and corrosion current dens ity 
𝑖𝑐𝑜𝑟𝑟, and microenvironmental factors such as water saturation, chloride content, and carbonation. 
The microenvironmental factors will be reduced to rebar -pore solution area and bulk resistivity 
which govern the area effect and ohmic control effec t.  This reduction can be achieved by 
analyzing the dependence of equalized water saturation level (area effect -linked) and bulk 
resistivity (ohmic control effect -linked) on the exposure RH, and pore solution ionic species 
concentration, and porosity affec ted by chloride ion addition and carbonation. The exhaustive 
treatments with combinations of RH, chloride content, and carbonation allows emphasizing one of 
the concurring rebar -pore solution area effect and bulk resistivity effect on corrosion, therefore 
making the isolation of these two effects possible. 
4.3 Resistivity test 
In Chapter 3, the corrosion parameters such as corrosion potentials and corrosion current densities 
were measured from pre -passivated, as-received rebars embedded in mortar. In this chapter, the 
corrosion parameters will be analyzed along with the resistivity test results. A n equal number of 
plain cylinder mortar samples were prepared at the same time from the same batch of raw materials 
and conditioned alongside the mortars with embedded rebar. The plain mortar samples also had 
six levels of chloride content that each contained a different concentration of chloride ion (as listed 
in Table 4.1). The mortar samples were also carbonated or non -carbonated at each chloride ion 
concentration. The samples were conditioned at three relative humidity (RH 60%, 80%, 95%) and 
under fully submerged conditions to achieve varying volumetric water content in the rebar -
\nPDF_PAGE 115\n 
 
75 
embedded mortars. The detailed sample preparation and improved corrosion measurement 
methods used for the rebar-embedded mortar samples are described in Chapter 3.

The bulk resistivity was tested using a direct current (DC) method to eliminate the  potential 
capacitive effect of the mortar matrix that can occur when using  alternating current. Two applied 
DC voltages (5 V and 10 V) were selected to offset the electrode polarization effect (Monfore, 
\nPDF_PAGE 116\n 
 
76 
1968). The test setup is illustrated in Figure 4.2. The mortar cylinder sample was sandwiched 
between moist sponge pads, carbon felt, and copper plates. The selected voltag es were applied 
across the copper plates. The applied voltage was regulated by the DC power supply and monitored 
by a voltmeter. The moist sponge pads were used to form low ionic resistance contact between the 
ends of the cylindrical mortar sample and the carbon felt electrodes, which was connect to the 
power circuit through the copper plates. Carbon felt electrodes are inert electrodes and served as a 
transition from moist sponges to dry copper plates.  They were used to eliminate the electrode 
changes such as corrosion if the copper plates were used directly in contact with electrolyte in 
moist sponge pads.

4.4 Results and discussion 
4.4.1 The effect of chloride content and carbonation on water retention 
As summarized in introduction, for a given RH, higher salt concentration in mortar pore solution 
and finer pore structure could increase water retention. As shown in Figure 4.3, the carbonation 
process has decreased the porosity of the mortar significantly from approximately 0.30 to 0.22. A 
slight increase in porosity with the increase in chloride content was observed in the chloride -
contaminated samples. 
 
Figure 4.3 Porosity of non -carbonated and carbonated mortar samples (w/c=0.7) with 
varying chloride levels 0-5 as listed in Table 4.1. Chloride levels 0: 0%, 1: 0.21%, 2: 0.43%, 
3: 0.87%, 4: 1.31%, 5: 2.24%, Cl wt.% by weight of cement. The mean value point is shown, 
and the error bars represent the range of the three repeated test results. 
The effect of chloride and reduced porosity on the equalized moisture level of the mortar samples 
conditioned at different RH is shown in Figure 4.4. Both non-carbonated and carbonated samples 
exhibit an increase in water saturation level with an increase in RH. When at the same RH, samples 
with higher chloride content have a higher saturation level. The effects of added chloride salt and 
porosity change are discussed in the following subsections. 

\nPDF_PAGE 118\n 
 
78 
  
Figure 4.4 Water saturation level of carbonated and non-carbonated mortar conditioned at 
various RH 65% 85% 95% and 100% (submerged). Legend: N (Non -carbonated), C 
(Carbonated), 0-5 (chloride levels 0: 0%, 1: 0.21%, 2: 0.43%, 3: 0.87%, 4: 1.31%, 5: 2.24%, 
Cl wt.% by weight of cement). The mean value point is shown, and the error bars represent 
the range of the three repeated test results. 
4.4.1.1 The effect of salts on water retention 
The effect of chloride content on water saturation level is more pronounced in carbonated samples 
(as shown in Figure 4.4) compared to n on-carbonated samples. In carbonated samples, the ion 
concentration is more sensitive to the addition of sodium chloride because the carbonation reaction 
transforms calcium hydroxide into calcium carbonate, which decreases the original ion 
concentration of the pore solution and simultaneously releases the bonded chloride ions (Mays, 
2004).  
4.4.1.2 The effect of capillary pore size on water retention 
According to the Kelvin-Laplace Equation (4.1), the saturated vapour pressure is lower in narrower 
pores. As a result, the carbonated mortar samples with a refined pore structure would retain more 
moisture at a given RH. With the porosity reduction due to carbonation, in finer po res, according 
to the Kelvin-Laplace equation, the condensation of water vapour will occur at a lower RH, which 
explains the slightly higher saturation level at 85% RH and 95% RH for carbonated samples 

\nPDF_PAGE 119\n 
 
79 
compared to non -carbonated samples. This carbonation -induced saturation increase is more 
significant especially at higher chloride content levels, where the reduced saturated vapo ur 
pressure reduced the critical pore radius, 𝑟𝑝 in Equation (4.2), so that more pores can be saturated 
at a given RH. At 65% RH, the carbonated mortar has a significantly higher saturation level 
compared to non-carbonated mortar samples. Besides the effect of the refined pore structure, the 
carbonated samples went through a different internal wetting history due to the water released 
during the carbonation process at 65% RH. Therefore, its re-wetting curve influenced by hysteresis 
(H. M. Jennings et al., 2015) would additionally contribute to a higher water content at 65% RH, 
and this influence is reduced at 85% and 95% RH. 
4.4.2 The effect of saturation, carbonation and chloride on Resistivity 
Although the water retained at various RH levels are not the same for mortar with different chloride 
content and carbonation states, we can observe the dependence of resistivity on the water saturation 
level by plotting the resistivity against saturation. 
Resistivity as a function of saturation is affected by chloride and carbonation. As shown in Figure 
4.5, the resistivity of carbonated mortar is very sensitive to chloride content, while the non -
carbonated mortar is almost only affected by the saturation level. This observation confirms the 
reasoning that the pore solution of non -carbonated mortar contains abundent dissolved ions 
(Ca2+, OH−, Na+ K+,Cl−), but in carbonated mortars, Cl− becomes the major charge-bearing ion. 
This phenomenon made it possible to isolate the two effects brought by saturation level, namely, 
ohmic control effect and rebar-solution area effect. The isolated ohmic -control effect is expected 
to be observed from the chloride -contaminated carbonated mortars This is because for a given 
saturation level, the rebar -solution area becomes a controlled variable, and various resistivity is 
obtained with different chloride contents in mortar samples at the given saturation level. 
\nPDF_PAGE 120\n 
 
80 
   
Figure 4.5 Resistivity of non -carbonated and carbonated concrete conditioned at various 
saturation levels. Legend: N(Non-carbonated), C(Carbonated), 0-5 (chloride levels 0: 0%, 1: 
0.21%, 2: 0.43%, 3: 0.87%, 4: 1.31%, 5: 2.24%, Cl w t.% by weight of cement ). The mean 
value point is shown, and the error bars represent the range of the three repeated test results. 
4.4.3 The effect of chloride and carbonation: comparing rebar corrosion between mortar 
and simulated pore solutions 
As discussed i n Chapter 3, the rebar corrosion potentials in carbonated mortar showed similar 
trends and values to the carbonated simulated pore solution (CSPS). However, unlike the case in 
CSPS, the 𝑖𝑐𝑜𝑟𝑟 in drier conditions (RH 65 –85%) does not reach a plateau with the increa se of 
chloride content. This may be because in CSPS, chloride is the main inducer to corrosion and this 
effect levels off when it reaches a specific concentration, so is the case in saturated carbonated 
mortar. However, in the partially saturated mortar, t he water saturation level also significantly 
affects the corrosion rate, even when the rebar is at a similar corrosion tendency (indicated by 
similar corrosion potential as in CSPS). This 𝑖𝑐𝑜𝑟𝑟’s dependence on chloride is probably affected 
by the increased saturation due to presence of chloride ions (as discussed in section 4.4.1), which 
results in increased exposed rebar surface to solution -filled pores (area ef fect) and the reduced 
resistance (as discussed in section 4.4.2) between the anodic and cathodic sites (ohmic control 
effect). The discussion of the contribution from the two mechanisms will be elaborated in Section 
0. 

\nPDF_PAGE 121\n 
 
81 
The companion study (Chapter 3) has shown the significant effect of saturation on both 𝐸𝑐𝑜𝑟𝑟 and 
𝑖𝑐𝑜𝑟𝑟.  Now we use simulated pore solution (SPS) as a reference to investigate the additional effect 
of chloride in mortar samples. When comparing the results between the non -carbonated mortar 
and the simulated pore solution (SPS), 𝐸𝑐𝑜𝑟𝑟 in the partially saturated non-carbonated mortars (RH 
65-85%) gradually decreased with increasing chloride content. In both SPS and the saturated non-
carbonated mortar, a threshold chloride content (critical chloride content) was observed, with a 
significant decrease in 𝐸𝑐𝑜𝑟𝑟 and a drastic increase in 𝑖𝑐𝑜𝑟𝑟. However, a similar pattern of 𝐸𝑐𝑜𝑟𝑟 
drop with chloride was not translated to a similar 𝑖𝑐𝑜𝑟𝑟 increases with chloride: 𝑖𝑐𝑜𝑟𝑟 reaches a 
plateau in SPS but increases with chloride continuously in both the saturated and the partially 
saturated mortars. To sum up, with the increase in chloride content,  
a) The trends in 𝐸𝑐𝑜𝑟𝑟 between SPS and saturated mortar are similar, but the trends in 𝑖𝑐𝑜𝑟𝑟 
are different,  
b) The trends in 𝑖𝑐𝑜𝑟𝑟 between partially saturated mortars and saturated mortars are similar 
but the trends in 𝐸𝑐𝑜𝑟𝑟 are different,  
c) The trends in  𝐸𝑐𝑜𝑟𝑟  and 𝑖𝑐𝑜𝑟𝑟  between SPS and partially saturated mortars are both 
different. 
The role of chloride in the carbonated mortar samples is also present in the non-carbonated samples. 
In the non -carbonated mortar samples, the increase in chloride content also induces saturation 
increase (as discussed in section 4.4.1). To a lesser extent, that increased saturation level will 
reduce the mortar’s resistivity, although the chloride ion itself does not significantly reduce the 
resistivity of the pore solution in non-carbonated mortar (as discussed in section 4.4.2) at a given 
saturation level. These effects of chloride on saturation level (referred as “indirect” effect) for a 
given conditioning relative humidity at least partially caused the gradual increase of 𝑖𝑐𝑜𝑟𝑟 with the 
increase in chloride content in partially saturated mortars, especially in the drier ones. 
The gradual drop of 𝐸𝑐𝑜𝑟𝑟 with chloride is probably caused by the depassivating effect of chloride 
on rebars, referred as “direct” effect. Firstly, in mortar samples, there could be reduced chloride 
concentration in pores. Although the chloride concentrations are the same between the SPS and 
the mixing solution of the mortar, the free chloride concentration in the pores is reduced due to the 
bonding effect of chloride during cement hydration process . Secondly, when the non-carbonated 
mortar samples have lower water content (conditioned at 65% or 85% RH) , the chloride salts in 
larger empty capillary p ores may crystalize , and the local chloride concentration in the pore 
\nPDF_PAGE 122\n 
 
82 
solution has not reached to the “threshold” level given a pH of 12.5 – 13.5, hence contribute little 
to the active corrosion of rebar . Thirdly, with the variable depassivated rebar area, the ohmic-
control mechanism also affects the 𝐸𝑐𝑜𝑟𝑟. This depassivation mechanism complements the partial 
contribution to the 𝑖𝑐𝑜𝑟𝑟 change. The chloride’s “indirect” and “direct” mechanism s overshadow 
each other, especially in terms of 𝑖𝑐𝑜𝑟𝑟. The effects of these two mechanisms will be discussed in 
the following section.  
4.4.4 The effect of water saturation on rebar corrosion in mortar (ohmic control effect vs 
area effect) 
The previous section discussed how the effects of chloride and carbonation (pH) on rebar corrosion 
in mortar are influenced by water saturation level, which distinguishes it from the case in the 
simulated pore solutions and adds complexity to the corrosion  in concrete. Figure 4.6 illustrates 
the path of influence of chloride ions and carbonation on the pore structure, pore solution, water 
retention in concrete, and rebar corrosion in concrete. 
 
Figure 4.6 The path diagram of quaulitative influence of chloride ion and carbonation on 
rebar corrosion in pore solution and concrete.  
The effects of saturation on the corrosion potential, 𝐸𝑐𝑜𝑟𝑟  and corrosion current density, 𝑖𝑐𝑜𝑟𝑟 are 
explored by plott ing them against the saturation levels for various treatments of chloride and 
carbonation.  
              
            
          
                      
               
                         
                     
           
\nPDF_PAGE 123\n 
 
83 
4.4.4.1 The relationship between corrosion potential and water saturation 
4.4.4.1.1 Non-carbonated mortar 
In the non-carbonated mortars (Figure 4.7a), when no chloride ions were present, the 𝐸𝑐𝑜𝑟𝑟 did not 
change significantly with the saturation level, indicating that the rebar remained in a passivated 
state. In the mortar with chloride ions, 𝐸𝑐𝑜𝑟𝑟 decreased with the increase in saturation level, which 
indicates a gradually increased extent of  depassivation with more rebar surface exposed to 
depassivating pore solution (high localized chloride ion concentration).  
  
(a) (b) 
Figure 4.7 Corrosion potential vs. saturation of mortar samples with c hloride contents (a) 
non-carbonated, (b) carbonated. Legend: Cl level 0-5 represent chloride content levels 0: 0%, 
1: 0.21%, 2: 0.43%, 3: 0.87%, 4: 1.31%, 5: 2.24%, Cl wt.% by weight of cement). The mean 
value point is shown, and the error bars represent the range of the three repeated test results. 
As shown in Figure 4.7a, the mortar samples with a lower chloride content (level 1) showed a 
slight linear decrease in 𝐸𝑐𝑜𝑟𝑟 from -80 mV to -150 – -300 mV. With higher chloride contents 
(level 2–5), 𝐸𝑐𝑜𝑟𝑟 decreased linearly with a similar larger derivative with the increase in saturation 
level, shown as parallel lines. This parallel trend represented a more negative corrosion potential 
for higher chloride contents at all observed saturation levels. The chlo ride content that 
differentiates the two observed patterns lies between level 1 (0.21 wt% cement) and level 2 (0.43 
wt% cement), which is consistent with the critical chloride content range of 0.2% to 2.0% wt% 
cement (Schiessl et al., 2005). To conclude, there was a synergistic effect between saturation and 

\nPDF_PAGE 124\n 
 
84 
chloride content on 𝐸𝑐𝑜𝑟𝑟. Higher water saturation facilitated chloride-induced rebar depassivation, 
leading to a larger depassivated area ratio of rebar and a more negative 𝐸𝑐𝑜𝑟𝑟. The negati ve 
correlation between the area ratio of depassivated rebar and 𝐸𝑐𝑜𝑟𝑟 measured from concrete cover 
was simulated in a circuit model (Li et al., 2023). 
4.4.4.1.2 Carbonated mortar 
The effect of saturation on corrosion for chloride-contaminated carbonated mortar is very different 
from that for chloride-contaminated non-carbonated mortar samples. As shown in Figure 4.7b, in 
carbonated mortar samples, the corrosion potential dropped rapidly with the increase of the water 
saturation level when there was no chloride present. For samples with chloride (levels 1 -5), the 
corrosion potentia l dropped steadily with the increase in saturation level but the steepness 
decreased with the increase in chloride content. The 𝐸𝑐𝑜𝑟𝑟 converged to a similar value in fully 
saturated conditions for all carbonated mortar samples regardless of the chlori de content level. 
Compared to the synergistic effect of chloride content and saturation level in non -carbonated 
mortar samples, in carbonated samples, the saturation had a dec reased effect on decreasing the 
𝐸𝑐𝑜𝑟𝑟 when chloride ions were present and became less significant at higher chloride content. This 
observation suggested that in carbonated mortar, the depassivated rebar surface area ratio does not 
significantly increase with saturation when chloride content is high. 
To summarize, the water saturation level has an effect on the 𝐸𝑐𝑜𝑟𝑟 in chloride -contaminated 
mortar. In non-carbonated mortars, the corrosion potential decreases with the saturation level when 
the chloride content exceeds a critical value, but is less affected by the saturation lev el if the 
chloride content is less than the critical value. In contrast, in carbonated mortars, the effect of the 
saturation level on the 𝐸𝑐𝑜𝑟𝑟 is most significant when there is no chloride present. As the chloride 
content increases, the effect of saturation becomes gradually less significant. 
4.4.4.2 Explaining 𝑬𝒄𝒐𝒓𝒓’s  dependence on saturation 
The influence of moisture content on corrosion potential (half-cell potential) has been noticed for 
a long time (Andrade et al., 2003) . Various explanations for this phenomenon are critically 
discussed here. 
\nPDF_PAGE 125\n 
 
85 
4.4.4.2.1 Preferential water adsorption 
As the water saturation increases, more rebar surface becomes exposed to solution-filled pores. If 
the active-to-passive rebar surface area ratio does not change,  𝐸𝑐𝑜𝑟𝑟 would remain the same while 
the corrosion current density, 𝑖𝑐𝑜𝑟𝑟 averaged over the whole reb ar surface would be scaled up by 
that increased rebar -pore solution surface area. However, a decrease in 𝐸𝑐𝑜𝑟𝑟  following a 
saturation increase indicates an increase in the active -to-passive rebar surface area ratio, which 
means the increased rebar-pore solution interface is depassivated to a greater extent with a greater 
active-to-passive rebar surface area ratio. The dependence of 𝐸𝑐𝑜𝑟𝑟 on active or passive rebar ratio 
was demonstrated with a circuit model (Li et al., 2023).   
The reasoned depe ndence of the active -to-passive rebar area ratio on mortar saturation can be 
explained by preferential water adsorption  in concrete i.e. the increased saturation water is 
preferably adsorbed or condensed in the pores with higher local chloride content beca use pore 
solution with a higher ionic concentration has a lower saturated vapour pressure according to 
Raoult's Law, Equation (4.3). This effect on the equilibrated water content in mortar has been 
shown and discussed in Section 4.4.1. This chloride -induced preferential water adsorption 
synergistic effect on 𝐸𝑐𝑜𝑟𝑟  is more significant in the non -carbonated mortar, as chloride is a 
competing factor to the high pH, whereas, in the carbonated mortar with lower pH, the chloride 
concentration in water -saturated pores readily surpasses the critical content and the additional 
depassivated rebar surface ratio does not add up much to the existing active rebar surface ratio.  
There is one case that can not be explained by the preferential water adsorption mechanism. In the 
carbonated mortar samples with no chloride ions, the 𝐸𝑐𝑜𝑟𝑟 was about -0.1 𝑉𝑆𝐶𝐸, much more noble 
than the ones with chloride. This indicated the depassivation was barely happening at its starting 
saturation level (conditioned at 65% RH), more significant depassivation occurred w ith the 
increased saturation and finally achieved the similar 𝐸𝑐𝑜𝑟𝑟  with other chloride -contaminated 
samples at the full saturated condition. It seems that water saturation facilitates the depassivation 
in the carbonated mortar with no chlorides. This  can be explained by the rust scale effect, 
elaborated in the next section.  
\nPDF_PAGE 126\n 
 
86 
4.4.4.2.2 Rust scale effect 
The iron corrosion potential depends on the moisture state of the iron oxides. For atmospheric 
corrosion of iron, oxygen reduction predominantly occurs on the oxides and the Fe2+/Fe3+ solid-
state conversion of the oxide scale during the wetting and drying process affects the oxygen 
reduction rate  (Stratmann, 1990) . At the beginning of drying, with good oxygen availability, 
oxygen is reduced on the rust cathode with a high surface area, which results in fast corrosion, and 
the dense rust precipitation passivates the steel. Without a fresh source of Fe2+, the rust is  all 
oxidized to Fe3+ which leads to poor electrical conductivity and a compromised rust cathode with 
a reduced oxygen reduction rate. The passivation state of iron with the dried oxidized rust scale 
(Fe3+ state) leads to more noble 𝐸𝑐𝑜𝑟𝑟. In the wett ing process, the Fe3+ is reduced to Fe2+ state 
with an improved conductivity, which again allows the fast reduction of oxygen on the rust cathode 
doped with Fe2+ states. Besides the effect of the rust scale state on oxygen reduction rate, this 
Fe2+/Fe3+ redox reaction was also regarded as a secondary cathodic reaction to interpret the 𝐸𝑐𝑜𝑟𝑟 
being more noble than −100 𝑚𝑉𝑆𝐶𝐸 in chloride-contaminated concrete (Alonso et al., 1998b).   
It is likely that both the rust scale effect and the preferentia l water adsorption effect contribute to 
the dependence of 𝐸𝑐𝑜𝑟𝑟 on saturation. The passivated state of rebar in drier mortar would have a 
limited ionic path coupling with other anodic corroding surfaces due to the high bulk resistivity or 
high localized resistance. Therefore, the oxide scale effect explains corrosion in the individual 
pores or locally connected pores where the electrical resistance through the bulk mortar pore 
network can be neglected , whereas the preferential water adsorption effect e xplains the increase 
in rebar corroding surface of  high active -to-passive ratio with ionic path coupling between non -
uniform rebar-mortar interfaces.  
4.4.4.2.3 Other factors to rule out 
• Higher oxygen concentration 
The small 𝐸𝑐𝑜𝑟𝑟 increase (100mV) with the drying of non -carbonated mortar was interpreted as 
forming differential aeration cells by (López & González, 1993) . With higher oxygen 
concentration in some pores, according to the Nernst Equation, there should be a slight increase in 
the reversible potential of the oxygen reduction reaction, 𝐸O2/OH−.  
\nPDF_PAGE 127\n 
 
87 
 𝐸O2/OH− = 𝐸O2/OH−𝑜 − 𝑅𝑇𝑛𝐹 ln 𝑎OH−4𝑎O2     (4.8) 
where, 𝐸O2/OH−𝑜  is reversible potential under standard cond ition,  𝑛 is number electrons being 
transferred, 𝑇 is temperature, 𝐹 is Faraday constant, 𝑎OH−  is activity of hydroxide ion, 𝑎O2  is 
activity of oxygen gas. 
For a given pH and temperature of 25 ℃, when the effective partial pressure of O2 increase from 
0.1 to 1,  𝐸O2/OH− will increase by Δ𝐸O2/OH− =14.8 mV, the coresponding change in 𝐸𝑐𝑜𝑟𝑟 can be 
calculated by 
 Δ𝐸𝑐𝑜𝑟𝑟 = Δ𝐸O2/OH−
1 + |𝛽𝑐|𝛽𝑎
 (4.9) 
where, 𝛽𝑎 is anodic Tafel parameters of the coupled iron dissolution reaction, |𝛽𝑐| is cathodic 
Tafel parameters of oxygen reduction reaction. The denominator 1 +

Δ𝐸O2/OH−=14.8 mV. It can be conc luded that he 𝐸𝑐𝑜𝑟𝑟  change due to the change in oxgyen 
concentration is insignificant in partially saturatred mortar samples. 
• Resistance in the measuring circuit 
The measured corrosion potential, is affected by the resistance of the voltmeter and the concrete 
cover. It can be expressed by 
 𝐸𝑐𝑜𝑟𝑟,𝑚𝑒𝑎𝑠𝑢𝑟𝑒𝑑 = 𝐸𝑐𝑜𝑟𝑟 ∙ 𝑅𝑣𝑜𝑙𝑡𝑚𝑒𝑡𝑒𝑟 𝑅𝑣𝑜𝑙𝑡𝑚𝑒𝑡𝑒𝑟 + 𝑅𝑐𝑜𝑛𝑐𝑟𝑒𝑡𝑒  (4.10) 
where 𝐸𝑐𝑜𝑟𝑟,𝑚𝑒𝑎𝑠𝑢𝑟𝑒𝑑 is the corrosion potential reported by a voltmeter, 𝐸𝑐𝑜𝑟𝑟 is rebar corrosion 
potential, 𝑅𝑣𝑜𝑙𝑡𝑚𝑒𝑡𝑒𝑟 is internal resistance of the voltmeter, 𝑅𝑐𝑜𝑛𝑐𝑟𝑒𝑡𝑒 is the resistance of concrete 
in the ionic path of the  measurement circuit. 𝐸𝑐𝑜𝑟𝑟,𝑚𝑒𝑎𝑠𝑢𝑟𝑒𝑑 will be non -negligibly smaller than 
𝐸𝑐𝑜𝑟𝑟 when the resistance in the ionlic path through the cover is not much less than the internal 
resistance of the voltage meter (Gu & Beaudoin, 1998). However, in our study, the 𝐸𝑐𝑜𝑟𝑟,𝑚𝑒𝑎𝑠𝑢𝑟𝑒𝑑  
\nPDF_PAGE 128\n 
 
88 
is greater in drier mortar with larger resistivivty, which contradicts to the expected outcome from 
the influence of the resistance in the measuring circuit and therefore can not be exp lained by this 
theory. Nevertheless, this phenomenon has been avoided by using a moist sponge on the concrete 
cover and a large internal resistance of the voltmeter, e.g. the known resistance between the work 
sensor and reference of Gamry Reference 600 is 100 TΩ (Gamry Instruments, 2012). 
4.4.4.3 The relationship between corrosion current density and water saturation 
The saturation has been long thought to be the major influential factor contributing to rebar 
corrosion in concrete with chloride or carbonation. As shown in Figure 4.8a, the 𝑖𝑐𝑜𝑟𝑟 increased 
with the saturation level for all chloride and carbonation cases. The 𝑖𝑐𝑜𝑟𝑟 spread across two orders 
of magnitude for the observed saturation levels. It appeared that the chloride content and 
carbonated state determined a baseline corrosion rate, and that rate alters with the saturation level.

4.4.4.4 Effect of saturation: Normalized corrosion current density’s dependence on 
saturation 
In order to extract the effect of water saturation on 𝑖𝑐𝑜𝑟𝑟  at dif ferent baselines controlled by 
chloride content and carbonation state, the normalized corrosion current density,  𝑖𝑐𝑜𝑟𝑟,𝑛𝑜𝑟𝑚, is 
introduced in Equation (4.11).  
 𝑖𝑐𝑜𝑟𝑟,𝑛𝑜𝑟𝑚 = 𝑖𝑐𝑜𝑟𝑟𝑖𝑐𝑜𝑟𝑟,𝑠𝑎𝑡 (4.11) 
It is the ratio of the corrosion current density, 𝑖𝑐𝑜𝑟𝑟 to the corrosion current density when the mortar 
is fully saturated, 𝑖𝑐𝑜𝑟𝑟,𝑠𝑎𝑡. As illustrated in Figure 4.9a, the 𝑖𝑐𝑜𝑟𝑟,𝑛𝑜𝑟𝑚 was linearly proportional to 
the water-saturated level for most cases including various chloride levels and carbonation states. 
The exceptional cases are the carbonated mortar samples with the highest chloride content (level 
5), where the linear regression line is significantly different from others.

saturation level for rebar in mortars. (a) non -carbonated, (b) carbonated. Legend: Cl level 
0-5 represent chloride content levels 0: 0%, 1: 0.21%, 2: 0.43%, 3: 0.87%, 4: 1.31%, 5: 2.24%,

4.4.4.4.1 Non-carbonated mortar samples 
It can be seen in Figure 4.9a, the effect of saturation on 𝑖𝑐𝑜𝑟𝑟,𝑛𝑜𝑟𝑚 is linear for all non-carbonated 
mortar samples with chloride content ranging from level 0 to level 5. The statistical significance 
of the fitted regression lines shown in Figure 4.9a were analyzed in Appendix 4.7.1.1. The first 
derivatives of the regression lines are plotted in Figure 4.10a. The results show that for non -
carbonated samples, the chloride content significantly affects the regressed linear relationship 
compared to the case with no chloride, but there is no significant difference between the regressed 
lines for various chloride levels. This suggests that saturation’s effect on corrosion rate is related 
to the mechanisms independent of chloride content (level 1-5). One of the mechanisms is thought 
to be the area effect: the area of rebar surface exposed to solution-filled pores is determined by the 
saturation level.  
The Ohmic control effect cannot be ruled out because the bulk resistivity at the same saturation is 
relatively less sensitive to the chloride content (as shown in Figure 4.5, and discussed in Section 
4.4.2). This suggests that there could be a similar ohmic control applies to the chloride-containing 

\nPDF_PAGE 131\n 
 
91 
mortars. However, the regressed lines of chloride -containing mortars are significantly different 
from the chloride -free mortar, indicating a different ohmic control effect between the chloride -
containing and chloride-free samples. The cause may be due to different allocation patterns of the 
anodic and cathodic sites. The chloride -free non -carbonated mortar retains an intact protective 
passivated film on rebar and provides a uniform environment with closely distributed micro -
anodes and micro -cathodes. Theref ore, the area effect is the dominant one for chloride -free 
samples, with a  regression line of 𝑑(𝑖𝑐𝑜𝑟𝑟,𝑛𝑜𝑟𝑚)/𝑑(𝑆𝑒)  = 1.08, close to 1. For the mortar with 
chlorides, the  𝑑(𝑖𝑐𝑜𝑟𝑟,𝑛𝑜𝑟𝑚)/𝑑(𝑆𝑒) ranges from 1.24 – 1.37, which indicates besides “area effect”, 
there are more effects of saturation on 𝑖𝑐𝑜𝑟𝑟. Therefore, the controlling effects of saturation on 
𝑖𝑐𝑜𝑟𝑟 in chloride-contaminated non-carbonaed mortar include the area effect,  and possible ohmic 
control effect. 
4.4.4.4.2 Carbonated mortar samples 
For carbonated mortar samples, as shown in Figure 4.9b, Figure 4.10b, the linear dependence of 
the 𝑖𝑐𝑜𝑟𝑟,𝑛𝑜𝑟𝑚 on saturation has no significant difference between the samples with no chloride 
present (level 0) and the ones with low to moderate chlorid e content (level 1 –3). However, the 
carbonated samples with high chloride content (levels 4 and 5) showed significant difference, 
where level 4 is identified as “significantly different” with a higher p -value (less confidence) and 
level 5 appears not to fo llow the linear trend due to the reduced corrosion rate in the saturated 
condition (see appendix 4.7.1.2). It suggests that there are similar effects of saturation on 𝑖𝑐𝑜𝑟𝑟,𝑛𝑜𝑟𝑚 
in the carbonated mortar with chloride content (level 0 –3) where the first derivative 
𝑑(𝑖𝑐𝑜𝑟𝑟,𝑛𝑜𝑟𝑚)/𝑑(𝑆𝑒) varies between 1.36 and 1.48. It has been observed that the bulk resistivity 
of carbonated mortar sensitively depends on the chloride content ( as shown in Figure 4.5, and 
discussed in Section 4.4.2). Since the area effect on rebar corrosion is independent of bulk 
resistivity, the remaining ohmic control effect, if any, should logically also be similar among these 
samples (level 0–3) and independent of bulk resistivity. This independence of the ohmic control 
effect on bulk resistivity is probably because the electrode potentials of rebar in the carbonated 
mortar do not change too much with chloride. With smaller potential differences, the different 
rebar surfaces will not polarize to near -discrete large anodes and cathodes and the micro anodes 
and cathodes will be closely located. Therefore, in carbonated samples, the ohmic control is 
\nPDF_PAGE 132\n 
 
92 
determined by the localized resistance between the neigh bouring connecting pores, which will 
remain low and not change with chloride concentration as much as the bulk resistivity. In 
conclusion, for carbonated mortar samples, the area effect and ohmic control effect on corrosion 
current density brought by change in water saturation is similar among carbonated mortar with low 
to moderate chloride content (level 0–3).  
4.4.4.5 Hypothetical corrosion current density in saturated pores 
To exclude the area effect brought by water volume fraction, the concept of hypothetical corrosion 
current density in saturated pores, 𝑖𝑐𝑜𝑟𝑟,𝑝𝑜𝑟𝑒, is introduced. The ratio of rebar -saturated pore area 
to total rebar area is expressed as the volumetric water content, then 
 𝑖𝑐𝑜𝑟𝑟,𝑝𝑜𝑟𝑒 = 𝑖𝑐𝑜𝑟𝑟𝜃  (4.12) 
where, 𝜃 is the volumetric water content. It is noted that 𝑖𝑐𝑜𝑟𝑟,𝑝𝑜𝑟𝑒 seems to share a similar concept 
of 𝑖𝑐𝑜𝑟𝑟,𝑛𝑜𝑟𝑚. However, 𝑖𝑐𝑜𝑟𝑟,𝑛𝑜𝑟𝑚 ignored the change in porosity caused by various chloride 
content and carbonation. It also set a baseline corrosion rate determined by the chloride content 
and carbonation of mortar. If the apparent corrosion current density is mainly scaled by the rebar-
saturated pore surface area, then the hypothetical corrosion current density, 𝑖𝑐𝑜𝑟𝑟,𝑝𝑜𝑟𝑒 should not 
be affected by the area effect. Any other dependence is a result of the ohmic control and the 
variation in the ratio of anodic and cathodic area.  
4.4.4.5.1 Non-carbonated mortar samples 
As shown in Figure 4.11, in the non-carbonated mortar with no chlorides, 𝑖𝑐𝑜𝑟𝑟,𝑝𝑜𝑟𝑒 does not seem 
to depend on the water content  and has a value similar to the value in the corresponding SPS. 
However, when the chloride is present, it becomes dependent on the water content, which indicates 
the corrosion is also “ohmic controlled” and has a changing anode/cathode ratio dependent on 
water content (as discussed in Section 4.4.4.1.1).  
\nPDF_PAGE 133\n 
 
93 
 
Figure 4.11 Hypothetical corrosion current density, 𝒊𝒄𝒐𝒓𝒓,𝒑𝒐𝒓𝒆 in saturated pores in non -
carbonated mortar and corrosion current density in the simulated pore solutions for 20 days. 
Legend: Cl level 0-5 represent chloride content levels in mortar 0: 0%, 1: 0.21%, 2: 0.43%, 
3: 0.87%, 4: 1.31%, 5: 2.24%, Cl wt.% by weight of cement , and in simulated pore solution 
0: 0%, 1: 0.5%, 2: 1.0% 3: 2.0%, 4: 3.0%, 5: 5.0%, NaCl wt.% by weight of solution. The 
mean value point is shown, and the error bars represent the range of the three repeated test 
results. 
The 𝑖𝑐𝑜𝑟𝑟,𝑝𝑜𝑟𝑒 deviates from the SPS when chloride is present: at low water content, 𝑖𝑐𝑜𝑟𝑟,𝑝𝑜𝑟𝑒  was 
lower than 𝑖𝑐𝑜𝑟𝑟 in SPS for all chloride levels but increased significantly with water conte nt. At 
full saturation, when chloride levels (level 3 –5) exceeded the critical values, 𝑖𝑐𝑜𝑟𝑟,𝑝𝑜𝑟𝑒  is 
significantly higher than the 𝑖𝑐𝑜𝑟𝑟 in SPS. Ohmic control with a fixed anode/cathode area ratio 
cannot explain this phenomenon. The explanation has to do with the changing in the anode/cathode 
area ratio. The ratio of the anodic area to total rebar area increases with the increase in the chloride 
concentration. At higher chloride levels (level 3–5) above the critical chloride content, the anodic 
area ratio in non-carbonated mortar is higher than that in SPS. This inference is possible,  and the 
reasoning is as follows: for chloride -induced rebar corrosion, the anodic area is stabilized by the 
surrounding cathodic sites and the anodic area cathodically protect the surround area. In SPS, due 
to the low resistivity in solution, the anodic area can catho dically protect a larger area of rebar 
resulting in a limit of anodic area ratio. However, in mortar, the rebar surface is divided into smaller 

\nPDF_PAGE 134\n 
 
94 
areas confined in pores with greater resistance in the path to catho dically protect the rebar at a 
greater distance, which will lead to a higher anodic area ratio. The increased anodic area ratio and 
the resistance become the two factors controlling the 𝑖𝑐𝑜𝑟𝑟,𝑝𝑜𝑟𝑒 . The 𝑖𝑐𝑜𝑟𝑟,𝑝𝑜𝑟𝑒  significantly 
increases with water content in the drier range where resistivity decreased dramatically.  
4.4.4.5.2 Carbonated mortar samples 
In the carbonated mortar samples, 𝑖𝑐𝑜𝑟𝑟,𝑝𝑜𝑟𝑒 increased dramatically with water content in the drier 
range for all chloride levels. At higher water content, 𝑖𝑐𝑜𝑟𝑟,𝑝𝑜𝑟𝑒 kept increasing with water content 
in no/low chloride samples but leveled off or decreased slightly with water content in high chloride 
content samples (Figure 4.12).  
 
Figure 4.12 Hypothetical corrosion current density, 𝒊𝒄𝒐𝒓𝒓,𝒑𝒐𝒓𝒆  in saturated pores in 
carbonated mortar and corrosion current density in the carbonated simulated pore solutions 
for 20 days. Legend: Cl level 0-5 represent chloride content levels in mortar 0: 0%, 1: 0.21%, 
2: 0.43%, 3: 0.87%, 4: 1.31%, 5: 2.24%, Cl wt.%  by weight of cement , and in carbonated 
simulated pore solution 0: 0%, 1: 0.5%, 2: 1.0% 3: 2.0%, 4: 3.0%, 5: 5.0%, NaCl wt.% by 
weight of solution. The mean value point is shown, and the error bars represent the range of 
the three repeated test results. 
 

\nPDF_PAGE 135\n 
 
95 
This trend is consistent with the resistivity trend found in carbonated mortar samples where 
resistivity decreased sensitively with water content in low -chloride samples, and decreased less 
dramatically or even increased with water content in high chloride samples at higher water content 
range.  
This indicates the ohmic control effect is still at least one of the controlling factors. However, this 
ohmic control is not sensitive to the pore solution conductivity among samples with different 
chloride content (level 2-5) but is sensitive to water content, especially in drier conditions. This 
observation again suggests that the anode/cathode area ratio has changed with the increase in water 
content. Such ratio change is not due to the preferential adsorption, but  due to the rust effect that 
passivates the steel in dry conditions and activates in wetting conditions (Section 4.4.4.2). It was 
also noted that the pre-passivated rebar remained passive in the carbonated simulated pore solution 
with no chlorides. One possibility could be the lack of disturbance in pH or external factors that 
would trigger the initiation of corrosion processes as it would occur in carbonated mortar. 
4.5 Conclusions 
Carbonation and chloride contamination affect the equalized water content and bulk resistivity of 
mortars. The effect of chloride content on water retention is more pronounced in carbonated 
mortars than non-carbonated ones. At a given RH, the carbonated mortars with higher chloride 
content significantly retain more water. At a given saturation level, the bulk resistivity of the 
carbonated mortars decreases significantly with increasing the chloride content, whereas for non-
carbonated mortars, the dependence of bulk resistivity on chloride content is insignificant. 
Water content affects 𝐸𝑐𝑜𝑟𝑟  in chloride -contaminated mortar. In non -carbonated mortar, the 
corrosion potential decreases with saturation wh en the chloride content exceeds a critical value, 
but is less affected if the chloride content is less than the critical value. In carbonated mortars, on 
the contrary, the effect of the saturation on the 𝐸𝑐𝑜𝑟𝑟 is most pronounced when there is no chloride 
and becomes less significant as chloride content increases. 
The 𝑖𝑐𝑜𝑟𝑟  increases with the saturation for all samples with various chloride content and 
carbonation states. 𝑖𝑐𝑜𝑟𝑟 spreads across two orders of magnitude for the observed saturation levels. 
\nPDF_PAGE 136\n 
 
96 
It appears that the chloride content and carbonated state determine a baseline corrosion rate, and 
that rate alters with the saturation level. 
The role of water saturation on corrosion includes 1) the area effect, which affects the rebar -pore 
solution area, 2) the ohmic control effect by changing the bulk resistivity, and 3) the anode -to-
cathode ratio effect by the altering the anodic and cathodic site area ratio. These effects complicate 
the role of chloride and carbonation on rebar corrosion in m ortar, rather than being solely 
dependent on chloride content and pH in simulated pore solutions. 
In non-carbonated mortar with no chlorides, the area effect is dominant over the other two effects 
i.e., the ohmic control effect and the anode-to-cathode ratio effect. In a chloride-contaminated non-
carbonated mortar and carbonated mortar with/without chlorides, the dependence of 𝐸𝑐𝑜𝑟𝑟 on the 
water content suggests the anode-to-cathode ratio effect. Different mechanisms are suggested for 
the change in the  anode-cathode area ratio: preferential adsorption of moisture for the non -
carbonated sample and rust-scale effect on the carbonated samples with the efficiency of the rust-
cathode dependent on conductivity (moisture content). 
With a change in the anode-to-cathode ratio, the ohmic control effect depends on the sensitivity of 
the resistance between the anodic and cathodic sites to bulk resistivity. In non -carbonated mortar 
with chloride, this ohmic control is significant . However, in carbonated mortar with/without 
chlorides, this ohmic control is less significant due to the closely located anodic and cathodic sites 
and weaker coupling between distant sites. 
4.6 Epilogue  
The findings in this chapter provide clarity on important questions regarding the interplay of water 
saturation, chloride contamination, and carbonation in relation to rebar corrosion in mortar. It 
highlights the effects of carbonation on water content and resistivity, the differential influence of 
chloride content on water retention, and the complex mechanisms that affect corrosion. Based on 
this analysis, the roles of water saturation on corrosion potential and corrosion current density of 
rebar in mortar with chloride contamination and carbonation are clarified. 
With t he analysis in this chapter, the measured corrosion parameters in the first phase of the 
experimental study (Chapter 3, 4) establish a database from which an empirical model of corrosion 
\nPDF_PAGE 137\n 
 
97 
parameters as a function of chloride content, water saturation, and c arbonation can be developed. 
Furthermore, it provides a more implementable basis for the subsequent development of a 
mechanistic corrosion model in the numerical simulation phase. 
4.7 Appendix for chapter 4 
4.7.1 Statistical significance of the fitted lines of 𝒊𝒄𝒐𝒓𝒓,𝒏𝒐𝒓𝒎 vs 𝑺𝒆 at various chloride and 
saturation levels.  
The fitted lines in Figure 4.9 are grouped with similar first derivative and intercepts. To determine 
if they are significantly different, an analysis of covariance (ANCOVA) was performed using 
categorical regression tests with the Statsmodels package (Seabold & Perktold, 2010) . The data 
points from various treatments (chloride content level) were fitted with each categorical variables 
using treatment (dummy) coding. The significance of the coefficient for each treatment indicates 
whether this treatment significantly contributes to capturing the variance of the 𝑖𝑐𝑜𝑟𝑟,𝑛𝑜𝑟𝑚. If not, 
the individually fitted line for that treatment is not significantly different from the baseline, which 
the fitted line was compared to. 
4.7.1.1 Non-carbonated sample data 
In the statistical results tables, the symbol T.7N(0-5) stands for the treatment of non -carbonated 
mortar samples with chloride level 0-5. Results show the treatments 1-5 are significantly different 
from 0, which suggests the added chloride at all studied levels (1 -5) significantly affects the 
dependence of 𝑖𝑐𝑜𝑟𝑟,𝑛𝑜𝑟𝑚 on saturation. However, among the chloride levels 1 -5, there is no 
significant difference in the dependence of 𝑖𝑐𝑜𝑟𝑟,𝑛𝑜𝑟𝑚 on saturation. In conclusion, the existence 
of chloride has a significant effect compared to no chloride, but the specific chloride content does 
not significantly affect the dependence of 𝑖𝑐𝑜𝑟𝑟,𝑛𝑜𝑟𝑚 on saturation.

4.7.1.2 Carbonated sample data 
In the statistical results tables, the symbol T.7C(0-5) stands for the treatment of carbonated mortar 
samples with chloride level 0-5. The treatment 4-5 are significantly different from 0 where 4 has 
a p -value of 0.035 which is slightly smaller than 0.05 i ndicating a weaker significance. This 
suggests adding chloride does not significantly affect the dependence of 𝑖𝑐𝑜𝑟𝑟,𝑛𝑜𝑟𝑚 on saturation 
unless there is a very high chloride content (4-5). When comparing among different chloride levels, 
only level 5 showed a significant difference and level 4 nearly reached the significance threshold, 
which means that higher chloride content affects 𝑖𝑐𝑜𝑟𝑟,𝑛𝑜𝑟𝑚  dependence on saturation. In

dependence of 𝑖𝑐𝑜𝑟𝑟,𝑛𝑜𝑟𝑚 on saturation.

5 The Impact of Non-uniformity and Resistivity on the Homogenized Corrosion 
Parameters of Rebars in Concrete - a Circuit Model Analysis 
5.1 Preface 
In the previous chapters, we explored the intricate relationship between water saturation, chloride 
contamination, carbonation, and rebar corrosion in mortar. The findings form the basis for 
developing mechanistic corrosion models. In the numerical simulation phase starting from this 
chapter, we will use the modelling method to explore how the overall cor rosion parameters are 
related to the non -uniform localized corrosion parameters at mesoscopic –macroscopic level and 
microscopic level.  
Chapter 5 presents the concept of the homogenized corrosion parameters of rebar in concrete with 
non-uniform surface conditions with a circuit model. This model starts from localized corrosion 
kinetics for active and passive rebar-concrete units (mesoscopic–macroscopic level) described by 
the Butler -Volmer equation and arrives at the simulated scan curve representing the ov erall 
performance of the rebar, from which the homogenized corrosion parameters can be derived. It 
explored the validity of describing the overall scan curve with the Butler -Volmer equation, and 
how much deviation can be caused by the non -uniformity of reb ar condition and concrete 
resistivity.  
It suggests that the homogenized corrosion parameters are a function of concrete resistance, and 
the area ratio of the active or passive rebar -concrete surface area — the necessity to employ 
corrosion parameters as a function of the microenvironments. In addition, the non-uniform surface 
condition at mesoscopic –macroscopic scale and concrete electrical resistivity distort the 
polarization curve, which may significantly deviate from the Butler -Volmer equation — the 
necessity of measuring corrosion parameters in a uniform rebar -concrete interface condition at 
mesoscopic–macroscopic scale, as was done in the first phase experimental studies in Chapter 3 
and Chapter 4.  
With the nonuniformity at mesoscopic–macroscopic scale , the degree of deviation is more 
significant in concrete with higher electrical resistivity, particularly when the active and passive 
rebar surfaces have a similar area ratio. In such cases, corrosion parameters obtained from n on-
uniform reinforced concrete samples using potentiodynamic scans may not accurately represent 
\nPDF_PAGE 142\n 
 
102 
the actual corrosion kinetics. Hence, they cannot be considered valid homogenized corrosion 
parameters.  
For nonuniform rebar at the meso -macroscopic scale, the  reinforced concrete samples should 
represent field conditions, and the corrosion model should utilize potentiodynamic curves instead 
of the fitted corrosion parameters derived from it. Such application might be applicable for large-
scale corrosion modelling but is not feasible for numerical simulation of rebar corrosion in cracked 
concrete with sharp spatial variation of microenvironment around the crack. 
In the next chapter, we will zoom in scale and use this circuit model to simulate the homogenized 
corrosion kinetics of the rebar -concrete interface (mesoscopic –macroscopic level) from the 
corrosion kinetics of the rebar-solution interface (microscopic pore level).  
Chapter 5 was adapted (with permission) from the manuscript " The Impact of Non -uniformity 
and Resistivity on the Homogenized Corrosion Parameters of Rebars in Concrete - a Circuit Model 
Analysis " published in Corrosion Engineering, Science and Technolog y1. Gang Li is the first 
author who designed and performed numerical simulation and data analysis. Dr. Richard Evitts 
and Dr. Moh Boul fiza contributed to the review of the manuscript and provided feedback. The 
manuscript was reformatted for inclusion in the thesis.  
5.2 Introduction 
The corrosion parameters of rebar, including corrosion potential, corrosion current density, and 
Tafel constants, are important parameters that are used to describe activation-controlled corrosion 
kinetics. These corrosion parameters are often used in mode ls to predict the service life of 
reinforced concrete structures. The corrosion potential can be used to estimate the likelihood of 
active rebar corrosion (ASTM International, 2015) . The widely used non -destructive linear 
polarization resistance method relies on the estimated values of the Tafel constants.  
 
1 Cited as “Li, G., Evitts, R., & Boulfiza, M. (2023). The impact of non-uniformity and resistivity on the homogenised 
corrosion parameters of rebars in concrete – a circuit model analysis. Corrosion Engineering, Science and Technology, 
58(4), 399–409. https://doi.org/10.1080/1478422X.2023.2190442” 
\nPDF_PAGE 143\n 
 
103 
Rebar corrosion parameters are often determined ex -situ using lab -created pore solutions due to 
ease of testing, but the results are expected to provide insights into the corrosion of rebar embedded 
in concrete. However, one immediate shortcoming is that in these solutions, the rebar surface is 
exposed to a uniform environment, but the microenvironmental conditions on the rebar in concrete 
tend to be non-uniform. For example, non-uniform chloride ion content can be found in uncracked 
concrete (Silva et al., 2012)  and this non -uniformity is often exacerbated in the case of cracked 
concrete contaminated with chlorides. In addition, the as-received rebar itself often has rust spots 
that create non-uniform rebar-concrete interfaces with a different electrochemical behaviour than 
that of rust -free areas (Stefanoni et al., 2015) . Regardless of the non -uniform rebar -concrete 
interface, the corrosion parameters and rate are frequently determined via a polariz ation curve 
acquired in a potentiodynamic scan by fitting the Butler -Volmer equation or its Tafel 
approximation to the test data. This method often involves testing a short rebar sample with the 
assumption that the sample will undergo uniform corrosion. Ho wever, this assumption is not 
frequently valid for rebar in-situ since most will experience distributed concentrations of chloride 
ions and varying levels of carbonation. Consequently, a potentiodynamic scan curve may not 
follow Butler-Volmer kinetics. This type of deviation has been noticed in a few studies: Elsener 
(2005) found the scan curves of rebar in concrete do not manifest Tafel behaviour (no linear portion 
found on the logarithmic current density scale); Chang et al. (2008b) reported that scan curve could 
only be fit by considering rust film growth/dissolution. For those who managed to apply Tafel 
extrapolation to determine the corrosion rate with a selected linear portion (Alonso et al., 1998b; 
Locke & Siman, 1980), or with modified Tafel eq uations (Chang et al., 2008b), the anodic Tafel 
slopes (205–1570 mV) can be significantly larger than the ones measured ex-situ in solutions (89–
115 mV) (Chang et al., 2008b) and also ones used by a few modellers (60 –91 mV) (Ghods et al., 
2007; Isgor & Razaqpur, 2006; Kranc & Sagüés, 1994) . In this study, it is hypothesized that 
deviation of the polarization curve of the rebar in concrete from Butler-Volmer kinetics is caused 
by the non-uniformity of the environment surrounding the rebar. 
For heat and mass transfer studies in concrete, non -uniformity in porous microstructure is often 
neglected. Concrete is treated as a continuum with the properties of its representative eleme ntary 
volume (REV) using a homogenization/volume -averaging technique. When the REV size is 
properly selected, properties such as effective thermal conductivity, diffusivity, permeability and 
\nPDF_PAGE 144\n 
 
104 
other thermophysical properties can be used in their representati ve equations to solve heat and 
mass transfer problems at a larger scale. Similarly, if the non -uniform corrosion rate at a 
microscopic level is homogenized at the scale of the REV, then the homogenized corrosion 
properties can be used at the larger scale. However, Butler-Volmer kinetics may no longer hold 
for the homogenized potential -current density relation since they are dependent on the localized 
surface interaction between the rebar and its surroundings.  This leads to the concerns raised by 
experimental observations (Chang et al., 2008b; Elsener, 2005).  
In this research, a model is developed to relate the homogenized, or area-blended corrosion kinetics 
of rebar in non -uniform conditions in concrete to the corrosion kinetics of rebar in uniform 
conditions. The non -uniformity could be due to non -uniform chloride ion contamination and/or 
non-uniform carbonation along the rebar or around its circumference. This model can be used to 
identify systems that follow Butler-Volmer kinetics with negligible error, and those that do not. It 
simulates the homogenized kinetics in the form of a potentiodynamic scan curve. This curve can 
be used in numerical models that require the input of rebar corrosion kinetics to predict large-scale 
rebar degradation.  
5.3 Model development 
5.3.1 Theory 
For rebar corrosion in concrete, the Butler -Volmer equation is used to describe the activation -
controlled corrosion kinetics.  
For a reversible half-cell electrode reaction (D. A. Jones, 1996): 
 𝑖𝑛𝑒𝑡 = 𝑖0 ∙ [10(𝐸−𝐸0)/𝛽𝑎 − 10(𝐸−𝐸0)/𝛽𝑐  ] (5.1) 
where, 𝑖𝑛𝑒𝑡 is measured or applied current density, 𝑖0 is exchange current density, 𝐸0 is reversible 
potential, 𝐸 is electrode potential, 𝛽𝑎 is anodic Tafel constant, 𝛽𝑐 is cathodic Tafel constant. 
For a mixed electrode reaction for corrosion (D. A. Jones, 1996): 
\nPDF_PAGE 145\n 
 
105 
 𝑖𝑛𝑒𝑡 = 𝑖𝑐𝑜𝑟𝑟 ∙ [10(𝐸−𝐸𝑐𝑜𝑟𝑟)/𝛽𝑎 − 10(𝐸−𝐸𝑐𝑜𝑟𝑟)/𝛽𝑐  ] (5.2) 
where, 𝑖𝑛𝑒𝑡 is measured or applied current density, 𝑖𝑐𝑜𝑟𝑟  is corrosion current density, 𝐸𝑐𝑜𝑟𝑟  is 
equilibrium potential or corrosion potential, 𝐸 is electrode potential, 𝛽𝑎 is anodic Tafel constant, 
𝛽𝑐 is cathodic Tafel constant. 
Despite the mathematical similarity of the Butler-Volmer equation when applied to either a single 
electrode with a reversible half -cell reaction or a mixed electrode reaction, there is a distinctl y 
different meaning of the net current density, 𝑖𝑛𝑒𝑡. Taking the corrosion of steel for example, with 
the reversible half-reaction (Fe2+ + 2e− ↔ Fe), 𝑖𝑛𝑒𝑡 in Equation (5.1) represents the total change 
of iron (either dissolution or deposition), dissolution case shown in Figure 5.1a. However, in a 
mixed electrode reaction (Figure 5.1b), when polarized anodically from its equilibrium potential, 
𝑖𝑛𝑒𝑡 in Equation (5.2) does not include all the change of iron: the “internal current”, 𝑖𝑂2 also 
contributes to a portion of the total change of iron (𝑖𝐹𝑒 = 𝑖𝑛𝑒𝑡 + 𝑖𝑂2). This contribution could be 
significant when the anodic polarization overpotential is small, which implies a non -negligible 
coexisting macro-cell (net current) and micro-cell corrosion (internal current).  
 
(a) 
 
(b) 
Figure 5.1 Evans diagrams for a single-electrode (a) and a mixed-electrode (b) reaction. 
Experimental observations (Hansson et al., 2006)  have indicated the micro -cell contribution in a 
seemingly macro-cell-dominant corrosion could be significant. This is especially true when the 

\nPDF_PAGE 146\n 
 
106 
macro-cell current density is low due to small potential differences between the neighbouring rebar 
segments. 
5.3.2 Model setup  
The proposed model aims at the goal of deriving a homogenized corrosion parameter from the 
corrosion parameters at the pore level. There are two types of rebar surfaces being considered in 
this model: passive and active. A passive rebar surface has a dense protective oxide film due to 
high alkalinity in concrete, and this film is disrupted for the active rebar surface due to localized 
environmental factors such as acidity (carbonation) or chloride ions. The passive rebar surface has 
a more noble electrode potential than the active one. In this model, two single electrode half -cell 
reactions are considered: iron deposition/dissolution ( Fe2+ + 2e− ⇄ Fe ), oxygen 
reduction/evolution ( O2 + H2O + 4e− ⇄ 4OH−) on both the active and passive portions of the 
steel surface. In this model those two electrode reactions on both active and passive steel surfaces 
are simplified by neglecting iron deposition and oxygen evolution, leaving one anodic or 
dissolution reaction and one cathodic or oxygen reduction reaction, (Fe → Fe2+ + 2e−) and (O2 +
H2O + 4e− → 4OH−); these are the normal corrosion reactions. It is assumed that on the same 
surface condition (either activated or passivated) at the capillary pore size (10  nm – 5 𝜇m (Mehta 
& Monteiro, 2006)), which is termed as “pore-level” (see Figure 5.2), the microscopic anodic and 
cathodic sites are physically close and uniform kinetics can be applied on the surface. Thus, there 
is no electric circuit modelled at this microscopic pore-level.  
At the mesoscopic-macroscopic level (a few millimetres), a rebar piece embedded in concrete in a 
non-uniform environment is comprised of a number of active or passive pore -level rebar pieces 
and non-reactive pore-level rebar pieces. In this research, this is called “unit -level” (Figure 5.2). 
Within each type of unit, due to the uniform environm ental condition, there is little microscopic 
level external current between pore -level rebar pieces; hence the effect of electrolytic resistance 
between these pore-level rebar pieces can be neglected and the corrosion is uniform, i.e., there is 
no physical  separation of micro anodes and cathodes and thus no internal current is modelled 
between them.  
\nPDF_PAGE 147\n 
 
107 
 
Figure 5.2 Schematic of the multi -level treatment of rebar corrosion in concrete (n, l, m is 
the number of “pore-level” rebar segments). 
At the larger macroscopic level (a few centimetres), a selected rebar piece might be exposed to a 
concrete environment that has n on-uniform environmental conditions. For example, flexural 
transverse cracks bring a non -uniform condition to the rebar in its longitudinal direction; or 
ongoing carbonation and chloride penetration through concrete cover may cause different exposure 
conditions on the rebar around its circumference. In these cases, a rebar piece is represented by a 
combination of active/passive corrosion units and there is current flow between the different units. 
Therefore, there is electrical resistance between these corr osion units, and this translates into 
possible ohmic control at the macroscopic level. In this research, rebar consisting of both active 
and passive units at the macroscopic level is termed “high-level” (Figure 5.2), the electrical current 
between units is termed as internal current at “high -level”. At this level, the uniform corrosion 
assumption is invalid, and the rebar is treated as micro-and-macro cell corrosion.  
The circuit model developed for this research work simulates a potentiodynamic scan test of 
reinforced concrete at its “high -level” from the known corrosion kinetics at the “unit -level”. The 
schematic circuit that combines these cells to si mulate corrosion in rebar is shown in Figure 5.3. 
Three battery symbols are used to represent electrochemical interfaces (red: active unit, blue: 
passive unit, and black: potentiostat-controlled concrete surface). When combined, the active unit 
and passive unit represent the rebar at “high level”. Each interface follows a characteristic 𝐸 − 𝐼 

\nPDF_PAGE 148\n 
 
108 
relationship defined by the Butler-Volmer equation, where 𝐸 is the potential difference from metal 
to electrolyte and 𝐼 is external current at the “unit level”. The concrete cover electrolyte potential 
is independent of current  and is maintained at 𝜙𝑎𝑝𝑝. Three resistors ( 𝑅0, 𝑅1, 𝑅2) represent the 
resistances in the concrete cover. The high -level “internal current” ( 𝐼0) goes through resistance 
(𝑅0) between those rebar units, and the high -level “external current” ( 𝐼e𝑎𝑐𝑡, 𝐼e𝑝𝑎𝑠) goes through 
resistance (𝑅1, 𝑅2) between the rebar units and the concrete cover.  
The current is defined as positive when flowing outwards from the rebar surface, and negative 
when flowing inwards to the rebar surface. The positive direction of current and potential symbols 
and grounding are presented in Figure 5.3. A summary of the symbol description is shown inTable 
5.1.  
 
Figure 5.3 Circuit diagram of the proposed model.

The equation (5.3) and (5.4) is the Butler-Volmer equation with the corrosion parameters 𝐸𝑐𝑜𝑟𝑟, 
𝑖𝑐𝑜𝑟𝑟, 𝛽𝑎, 𝛽𝑐 of the active and passive units designated with superscripts “act” and “pas”; AREA 
is the total surface area of the rebar; 𝑟𝑎 and 𝑟𝑝 are the area ratios of the activated rebar surface and 
the passivated rebar surface to the whole rebar surface, respectively. 
When the switch is in the “off” position, the circuit is in “open -circuit mode”, where th e current 
flows between the two corrosion units:  
 𝐼𝑎𝑐𝑡 + 𝐼𝑝𝑎𝑠 = 0 (5.5) 
The potential drop between the active and passive units is balanced with total resistance between 
these units being: 
 𝜙𝑎𝑐𝑡 − 𝜙𝑝𝑎𝑠 = 𝐼𝑎𝑐𝑡 ∙ 1 ( 1𝑅0 + 1𝑅1 + 𝑅2)⁄  (5.6) 
Four unknowns in open -circuit mode (i.e., 𝐼𝑎𝑐𝑡, 𝐼𝑝𝑎𝑠, 𝜙𝑎𝑐𝑡, 𝜙𝑝𝑎𝑠) can be solved with Equations 
(5.3) to (5.6). The area-averaged electrode potential or open circuit potential, OCP, is defined as 
−(𝜙𝑎𝑐𝑡𝑟𝑎 + 𝜙𝑝𝑎𝑠𝑟𝑝), and the corrosion current is 𝐼𝑎𝑐𝑡 or |𝐼𝑝𝑎𝑠|. 
When the switch is in “on” position, the circuit is at “scan mode”, where a range of electrical 
potentials,  𝜙𝑎𝑝𝑝, are applied to the concrete cover.  
Applying Kirchhoff’s Current Law at node 1, 2 yields: 
\nPDF_PAGE 151\n 
 
111 
 𝐼𝑎𝑐𝑡 − 𝐼0 − 𝐼𝑒𝑎𝑐𝑡 = 0 (5.7) 
 𝐼𝑝𝑎𝑠 + 𝐼0 − 𝐼𝑒𝑝𝑎𝑠 = 0 (5.8) 
Applying Kirchhoff’s Voltage Law for closed-circuits 3, 4, and 5 yields: 
 (0 − 𝜙𝑎𝑐𝑡) + 𝐼0𝑅0 − (𝜙𝑝𝑎𝑠 − 0) = 0 (5.9) 
 𝐼𝑒𝑎𝑐𝑡𝑅1 − 𝐼𝑒𝑝𝑎𝑠𝑅2 − 𝐼0𝑅0 = 0 (5.10) 
 (𝜙𝑎𝑝𝑝 − 0) + (0 − 𝜙𝑎𝑐𝑡) + 𝐼𝑒𝑎𝑐𝑡𝑅1 = 0 (5.11) 
The seven unknowns (i.e., 𝜙𝑎𝑐𝑡, 𝜙𝑝𝑎𝑠, 𝐼𝑎𝑐𝑡, 𝐼𝑝𝑎𝑠, 𝐼𝑒𝑎𝑐𝑡, 𝐼𝑒𝑝𝑎𝑠, 𝐼0) can be determined via the seven 
equations: Equation (5.3–5.4 and 5.7–5.11). 
5.3.3 Homogenized potential  
Due to the existence of the electrolytic resistance in the concrete or IR drop, for a “high -level” 
rebar piece, the localized electrode potentials of the active or passive units are different from the 
zero-resistance mixed potential of the two units. The potential difference between the two localized 
electrode potentials develops a potential and current field in the concrete. The mixed potential 
measured from the concrete cover lies between the two localized electrode potentials. During a 
simulated potential dynamic scan process, the following rebar potentials are present: the localized 
potentials on active and passive units (𝐸𝑎𝑐𝑡, 𝐸𝑝𝑎𝑠); homogenized area-averaged potential, 〈𝐸𝑟𝑒𝑏𝑎𝑟〉; 
and corrected rebar potential measured from concrete cover surface, 𝐸𝑟𝑒𝑏𝑎𝑟𝐼𝑅𝑐𝑜𝑚𝑝. This corrected 
potential is equivalent to the IR compensated potential read from a potentiostat. The mathematical 
expression of these potentials can be found from Equations (5.12) to (5.15). 
 𝐸𝑎𝑐𝑡 = 0 − 𝜙𝑎𝑐𝑡 (5.12) 
\nPDF_PAGE 152\n 
 
112 
 𝐸𝑝𝑎𝑠 = 0 − 𝜙𝑝𝑎𝑠 (5.13) 
 〈𝐸𝑟𝑒𝑏𝑎𝑟〉 = 0 − 〈𝜙𝑟𝑒𝑏𝑎𝑟〉 = −(𝜙𝑎𝑐𝑡𝑟𝑎 + 𝜙𝑝𝑎𝑠𝑟𝑝) (5.14) 
 𝐸𝑟𝑒𝑏𝑎𝑟𝐼𝑅𝑐𝑜𝑚𝑝 = 0 − 𝜙𝑟𝑒𝑏𝑎𝑟𝐼𝑅𝑐𝑜𝑚𝑝 = −[𝐸𝑎𝑝𝑝 − (𝐼𝑒𝑎𝑐𝑡 + 𝐼𝑒𝑝𝑎𝑠)  𝑅𝑒] (5.15) 
where the 𝑅𝑒 is the total resistance in concrete cover with 𝑅1, 𝑅2 in parallel.

= 1𝑅1 + 1𝑅2  (5.16) 
The total external current is 𝐼𝑒𝑎𝑐𝑡 + 𝐼𝑒𝑝𝑎𝑠, which is equal to total current 𝐼𝑎𝑐𝑡 + 𝐼𝑝𝑎𝑠 can be plotted 
against 〈𝐸𝑟𝑒𝑏𝑎𝑟〉 to create the homogenized potentiodynamic scan curve. 
 
5.3.4 Simulated potentiodynamic scan 
The model is firstly solved in “open circuit mode” to find the open circuit potential (OCP). Then, 
a simulated scan starts from OCP ( −𝜙𝑎𝑝𝑝 = OCP) and increases/decreases using small potential 
increments (e.g., 0.5  mV) to alter the homogenized area -averaged rebar electrode potential, 
〈𝐸𝑟𝑒𝑏𝑎𝑟〉. The scan stops when the prescribed scan range bounds are reached (e.g., OCP -200 mV 
or OCP+ 200 mV). The simulated potentiodynamic scan procedure was coded in Python and the 
program is documented in an open -source public repository (Li, Evitts, & Boulfiza, 2018) . The 
group of equations in both “open circuit model” and “scan mode” were numerically solved with 
an iterative optimization algorithm from SciPy (Jones et al., 2001)  in the Python ecosystem. To 
accelerate the convergence process, sol utions from the previous scan step were used as initial 
guesses for the next scan step.  
In this model, the base parameters used are listed in Table 5.2 and Table 5.3. The corrosion 
parameters listed in Table 5.2 are from the repeated tests of rebar embedded in both uncarbonated 
\nPDF_PAGE 153\n 
 
113 
and completely carbonated concrete, with both types of concrete subjected to water -saturated 
conditions (Sohail, 2013) respectively representing the corrosion kinetics of a passive rebar unit 
and an active rebar unit. At the " unit level", it was defined that the pore solution composition is 
similar within the active unit or within the passive unit and there is negligible internal current 
within each type of unit, and therefore the Tafel constant will be unaltered, and the corro sion 
current, 𝑖𝑐𝑜𝑟𝑟, of each unit will be reduced by the ratio of the area exposed to the saturated pores 
to the total rebar -concrete interface area. In real structures, the moisture level is not uniform 
throughout the concrete cover, and the moisture content at the rebar-concrete interface tends to be 
more stable than that in the cover in wetting and drying scenarios. For isolating the effect of 
resistance on the electrical field in this equivalent circuit approach, a constant corrosion current at 
the “unit-level” assumption will be used, and the values of the equivalent resistors reflect the 
resistivity distribution and geometry of concrete cover.

5.4 Results and discussion 
Parametric scan results from the model were conducted with various values of equivalent resistors 
across the concrete cover ( 𝑅𝑒) and between corrosion units ( 𝑅0). Both resistors were prescribed 
discrete values of 100, 1000, and 10000 Ω to represent variable saturation levels and geometry of 
\nPDF_PAGE 154\n 
 
114 
the concrete cover. The resistance was approximated as a spreading resistance (Angst et al., 2011; 
Hornbostel et al., 2015)  of a circular surface area with a radius of 1  cm. The “high -level” rebar 
was polarized to ±200 mV from its open circuit potential (OCP). 
5.4.1 Significance of IR compensation 
IR compensation is needed when there is a significant difference between the homogeniz ed 
potential on the rebar surface and the potential measured by a reference electrode. Due to field 
practicalities, the potential measured from concrete cover in a potentiodynamic scan is commonly 
used as rebar potential, although some solid-state embedded reference electrodes could be used to 
reduce the IR drop (Pech-Canul & Castro, 2002).  
It can also be seen from Figure 5.4 that the IR compensation is crucial for cases1 -3, where the 
resistance across the concrete cover (𝑅𝑒) is large. The IR compensation effect is insignificant when 
𝑅𝑒 is small (case 7 –9). For cases 4 –5, with reduced 𝑅𝑒 the IR compensation effect becomes less 
significant when th e potential gets closer to the OCP because the total external current going 
through the concrete cover becomes smaller when the over-potential is small. This suggests that a 
smaller scanning range such as the ones done in linear polarization resistance tes ts (range: 
OCP±10 mV) can be performed with less IR compensation. When comparing with group 1 –3 or 
group 4–6, with prescribed 𝑅𝑒, a smaller equivalent resistance between the corrosion units ( 𝑅0) 
increases the IR drop in the concrete cover. This is becau se a reduced 𝑅0 would increase the 
corrosion current at OCP, and hence a higher current through the concrete cover would occur when 
polarized.  
\nPDF_PAGE 155\n 
 
115 
 
Figure 5.4 IR drop increases with an increase in resistance through concrete cover and a 
decrease in resistance between the dissimilar corrosion units. 
 
5.4.2 Deviation of localized potential affected by resistance 
As shown in Figure 5.5, the homogenized potential of the rebar, 〈𝐸𝑟𝑒𝑏𝑎𝑟〉 equals the reference -
electrode potential measured from the concrete cover with IR compensation (𝐸𝑟𝑒𝑏𝑎𝑟𝐼𝑅𝑐𝑜𝑚𝑝) regardless 
of resistance. That means at the “high-level” scale, when the rebar is treated as being under uniform 
corrosion, the experimentally acquired potential of the rebar from the concrete cover corrected by 
IR compensation can be used to represent the homogenized potential of the rebar without the need 
of knowing the details of the localized potential distribution. 
Nonetheless, the localized potential of the active and passive units ( 𝐸𝑎𝑐𝑡, 𝐸𝑝𝑎𝑠) deviate from the 
homogenized potential 〈𝐸𝑟𝑒𝑏𝑎𝑟〉. As sho wn in Figure 5.5, this deviation significantly decreases 
with decreasing 𝑅0, because a smaller resistance between the active and passive units means the 
rebar condition is more like the situation in the solution where there is uniform corrosion.  In 
addition, 𝑅𝑒 has a similar effect: this deviation would be minimized if the resistance through the 
concrete cover is reduced. The simulations also show that this deviation is more pronounced when 
the samples are more polarized. 
 

\nPDF_PAGE 156\n 
 
116 
 
Figure 5.5 The influence of resistance on localized potentials and homogenized potential. 
 

\nPDF_PAGE 157\n 
 
117 
5.4.3 Polarization curve affected by resistance  
Since there is a deviation between homogenized and localized potential, the homogenized 
potential-current relationship also deviates  from that described by the Butler -Volmer equation. 
The degree of deviation is similar to the deviation between the localized potentials and the 
homogenized potential (Figure 5.5).  
Such deviation distorts the polarization curve. For the case with a large concrete cover resistance, 
shown in Figure 5.6 (case 1), the homogenized potential-current plot (green curve) appears to have 
a non-linear slope change, and this distortion becomes more significant at larger polarization levels. 
When the resistance is small ( Figure 5.6, case 9), this distortion becomes negligible, and the 
polarization curve becomes closer to a Butler -Volmer equation type, with the homogenized scan 
curve having a linear Tafel portion. 
Consequently, if one directly applies the Butler-Volmer equation or Tafel extrapolation technique 
to the polarization curve, the homogenized corrosion current density of the rebar at the “high-level” 
will have large errors and is overestimated. As illustrated in Figure 5.7, for Case 1 (large concrete 
cover resistance), the estimated corrosion current density is significantly greater than the corrosion 
current density at the corrosion potential; this difference diminishes for cases where the resistance 
is lower. 
\nPDF_PAGE 158\n 
 
118 
 
Figure 5.6 The influence of resistance on simulated polarization scan curves at “unit level” 
and homogenized “high-level”. 
 

\nPDF_PAGE 159\n

Figure 5.7 Tafel extrapolation on a distorted polarization curve: Case 1 - significantly 
distorted and Case 9- insignificantly distorted (same line legend as Figure 5.5 and Figure 5.6, 
omitted for simplicity).

5.4.4 Effect of the area ratio of activated and passivated rebar area and resistance on the 
fitted corrosion parameters  
To fully investigate how the distortion of the scan curve affects the fitted corrosion parameters 
(𝐸𝑐𝑜𝑟𝑟, 𝑖𝑐𝑜𝑟𝑟, 𝛽𝑎, 𝛽𝑐), the scanned potential -current den sity curves were fitted with the Butler -
Volmer equation using a non -linear regression method (Li, Evitts, Boulfiza, et al., 2018) . The 
active area was set to have a ratio ranging from 0.01 -0.99, and the remaining area was passive. 
Two values of resistance were used: 𝑅0, 𝑅𝑒  = 1000 Ω to represent concrete with higher resistivity 
and 𝑅0, 𝑅𝑒 = 10 Ω to represent concrete with lower resistivity.  
The fitted 𝑖𝑐𝑜𝑟𝑟 from the scan curve is compared with 𝑖𝑐𝑜𝑟𝑟 from the open-circuit mode. As shown 
in Figure 5.8a, 𝑖𝑐𝑜𝑟𝑟 has a significant overestimation when the resistance is high especially when 
the active and passive area is comparable. When the resistance is low, the fitted 𝑖𝑐𝑜𝑟𝑟 does not 
significantly deviate from the actual 𝑖𝑐𝑜𝑟𝑟value. (Figure 5.8b).

However, the resistances between the rebar units and through the concrete cover do not 
significantly affect the corrosion potential, 𝐸𝑐𝑜𝑟𝑟.  The corrosion potential is mainly affected by 
the area ratio of the active or passive unit to the total area, as is shown in Figure 5.10.
