The modelled Tafel constants of the rebar at “high level” depend on both the resistance and active 
or passive area ratios. As shown in Figure 5.11, for a large concrete resistance, the apparent anodic 
Tafel constant of the high-level rebar obtained from the scan curve is greater than the anodic Tafel 
constant of the active unit (𝛽𝑎𝑎𝑐𝑡).  
 
Figure 5.11 Effect of the active unit area ratio and resistance on the fitted anodic Tafel slope. 
 

\nPDF_PAGE 163\n 
 
123 
The difference reaches its maximum when the areas of the active and passive units are comparable. 
However, when the resistance between the active and passive units is low (10 𝛀), the two Tafel 
constants are similar, regardless of the area ratios. This phenomenon explains why the anodic Tafel 
constant measured in concrete could be greater than the value in solutions. 
The apparent cathodic Tafel constant ( Figure 5.12) is also significantly affected by the area ratio 
of active unit to total rebar surface, when 𝑅𝑜, 𝑅𝑒 are large (1000 𝛀). At greater area ratios of  the 
active unit to the total area, the contribution to the reduction of oxygen on the active unit becomes 
more important, which causes the cathodic Tafel constant to attain values that are close to that of 
the active units,  𝛽𝑐𝑎𝑐𝑡. However, when the a ctive unit area is greater than 90% of the total rebar 
area, the cathodic Tafel constant changes in a direction towards the value of the cathodic Tafel 
slope of the passive unit,  𝛽𝑐𝑝𝑎𝑠.. For the lower resistance case. The influence from the cathodic 
Tafel slope of the active unit is not significant unless of all the rebar surface is an active unit. 
 
Figure 5.12 Effect of the active unit area ratio and resistance on the fitted cathodic Tafel 
slope. 
In summary, when the concrete resistivity is low (low 𝑅0 and 𝑅𝑒), the anodic Tafel constant is 
similar to that of the active unit, and the cathodic Tafel constant i s similar to that of the passive 

\nPDF_PAGE 164\n 
 
124 
unit except when there are nearly no passive units (passive unit ratio = 0.01). For this case, a single 
set of Tafel slopes ( 𝛽𝑎𝑎𝑐𝑡, 𝛽𝑐𝑝𝑎𝑠) can be used for the rebar at the “high -level”. However, when the 
concrete resistivity is high, the value of Tafel constant greatly depends on the area ratio of the 
active or passive area to that of the whole rebar. The combined effect from the resistance and non-
uniformity (active/passive area ratio) explains why measured anodic  Tafel constants are 
significantly larger and have a large scatter for concrete under partially saturated conditions (400-
1570 mV or even infinite)(Alonso et al., 1998b; Locke & Siman, 1980). It is also worth mentioning 
that compared to the anodic Tafel constant changes, the increase in the cathodic Tafel constant is 
significantly smaller (180-480 mV) (Alonso et al., 1998b; Chang et al., 2008b; Locke & Siman, 
1980). This is because the fitted cathodic Tafel constant falls between the cathodic Tafel constant 
of the basic active/passive units, which are not as different as the anodic ones. 
5.5 Conclusions 
An electrical circuit model was developed to simulate the potentiodynamic scan of a non-uniform 
reinforced concrete to capture the homogenized polarization b ehaviour at a “high-level” scale. A 
parametric study was carried out to capture the effect of the electrical resistance of the concrete 
cover on the homogenized polarization curve.  
It is concluded that the area -averaged localized potential (homogenized po tential) of the rebar is 
equivalent to the IR -compensated potential measured from the concrete cover. The difference 
between the localized potentials of the active and passive rebar portions may cause the apparent 
polarization curve to significantly deviat e from the polarization curve of a rebar coupon in 
synthetic pore water that is described by the Butler-Volmer equation.  
When fitted to the Butler-Volmer equation, the distorted scan curve exhibits larger Tafel constants, 
which leads to an overestimation of corrosion current density and corresponding corrosion rate. 
Such changes are more pronounced when the electrical resistance of the concrete is higher, 
especially when the active and passivated rebar units have comparable surface areas. The corrosion 
potential is not significantly affected by this distortion. 
The homogenized corrosion parameters are thus a function of concrete resistance, and the area 
ratio of the active or passive rebar surface area. These factors cause the deviation of the 
\nPDF_PAGE 165\n 
 
125 
homogenized corrosion kinetics from those described by the Butler-Volmer equation for coupons 
immersed in a solution. When the deviation is small, the kinetics represented by homogenized 
corrosion parameters can be used in practical large -scale modelling when the non -uniform rebar 
is treated at the “high -level” scale. However, when the deviation is large, the potentiodynamic 
curves representing the kinetics for the anodic and cathodic reactions should be obtained directly 
from scans of reinforced concrete samples representative of field conditions or calculated using an 
averaging technique as per this model. 
Similar to the polarization curves described by Butler -Volmer kinetics, the total anodic and total 
cathodic curve predictions approach the overall polarization curve when the overpotential from of 
the homogenized corrosion potential is large. Therefore, an experimental potentiodynamic scan 
curve can be used to represent the corrosion kinetics of the homogenized rebar at large 
overpotentials and often the directly accessible data from the experiment is the overall polarization 
curve (green curve, Figure 5.6). However, because the total polarization curve shows the net 
current density, it does not quantify the internal current density within the modelled rebar segment, 
which could be significant when the overpotential is small. For example, the current is nea r zero 
when the potential is near 𝐸𝑐𝑜𝑟𝑟 on the overall polarization curve. Therefore, for a corrosion model 
that has the capability of handling a full range of rebar potential, the complete anodic (orange 
curve, Figure 5.6 ) and cathodic (blue curve, Figure 5.6) curves need to be determined from the 
overall potential dynamic scan curve. 
5.6 Limitations and suggestions for future works 
In this model, the counter electrode on the concrete cover is used as the means of excitement 
providing or extracting electrons to get the overall polarization curve, which resembles the typical 
setup of potentiodynamic scan test and some cathodic protection configurations. However, the 
equivalent resistors used in this circuit model are affected by the concrete cover geometry and the 
setup location of the counter electro de. Therefore, when the homogenized corrosion kinetics are 
required for rebar with a different geometry, for example, a coupled neighbouring rebar, the value 
of the equivalent resistance may change which affects the homogenized corrosion kinetics. More 
investigation needs to be done to quantify this effect.  
\nPDF_PAGE 166\n 
 
126 
5.7 Epilogue 
Chapter 5 introduced the concept of homogenized corrosion parameters for rebar in concrete with 
non-uniform surface conditions, utilizing a circuit model to simulate the polarization behaviour. 
The chapter emphasized the necessity of considering corrosion parameters as a function of 
microenvironments and the potential deviations caused by non -uniform surface conditions and 
concrete resistivity. This deviation is highly dep endent on the area ratio of active passive rebar -
concrete interface units and the resistance between them at the meso -macroscopic scale, which 
calls for a zoomed-in homogenization from the pore scale. In the upcoming chapters, we will delve 
further into th e numerical simulation phase, utilizing the insights from experimental studies in 
Chapter 3 and Chapter 4 and the corrosion circuit model in Chapter 5 to explore the link between 
homogenized corrosion parameters with the localized corrosion parameters at the pore scale.

6 A Pore-scale Corrosion Model for Rebar in Concrete: the Effect of Water and 
Chloride Distribution at the Pore Level on Rebar Corrosion in Concrete 
6.1 Preface 
In the preceding chapters, we explored the concept of homogenized corrosion parameters for rebar 
in concrete, emphasizing the importance of considering microenvironments and the deviations 
caused by non-uniform surface conditions and concrete resistivity. Chapter 5 introduced a circuit 
model that simulated th e polarization behaviour and highlighted the need for zoomed -in 
homogenization from the pore scale. 
Building upon the insights from experimental studies conducted in Chapter 3 and Chapter 4 and 
the homogenization method with the corrosion circuit model fro m Chapter 5, Chapter 6 explores 
the mechanistic link between homogenized corrosion parameters and localized corrosion 
parameters at the pore scale. 
The introduction of this chapter discusses the differences between rebar corrosion in bulk pore 
solution and  concrete, particularly in terms of non -uniform chloride distribution and varying 
chemical environments at rebar-pore solution interfaces. 
By selecting the representative elementary volume (REV) based on pore structure, a model is 
developed to quantitatively describe the allocation of active and passive rebar -solution corrosion 
sites at the representative elementary volume based on the non -uniform water and chloride 
distribution in the concrete. The model describes how water and chloride ions are distributed given 
the total water content and total chloride content taking into account the water adsorption -
condensation phenomena, chloride binding effect, and chloride concentrate effects due to the 
electrical double layer. The corrosion circuit model from Chapt er 5 is then employed to simulate 
the homogenized corrosion parameters of the rebar-concrete interface in REV from the corrosion 
parameters of the rebar -solution interface at the pore level. The simulated corrosion parameters 
capture the observed pattern from the empirical functions from the experimental data.  
The homogenized corrosion parameters as a function of the environmental conditions can be 
applied to every point on the rebar surface with surrounding concrete of non -uniform 
microenvironment caused by cracking of concrete and varying exposure conditions. 
\nPDF_PAGE 168\n 
 
128 
The manuscript of this chapter will be submitted to “Construction and Building Materials”. Gang 
Li is the primary author who designed and performed the experimental study and data analysis. Dr. 
Moh Boulfiza and Dr. Richard Evitts contributed to the review of the manuscript and provided 
feedback. 
6.2 Introduction 
Rebar corrosion in concrete is not uniform. It is related to but quite different from rebar corrosion 
in bulk pore solution. Chloride contents in the pore solution at the steel-concrete interface are not 
uniform due to more restricted diffusivity. This non -uniformity leads to different non -uniform 
corrosion behaviour in concrete than bulk solutions.  
In bulk solutions, actively corroding rebars have closely allocated microscopic anodic and cathodic 
sites with negligible resistance between them. These micro-sites are treated as uniform corrosion, 
and both the anodic and cathodic reactions happen on the same surface, with the corrosion current 
averaged over the whole steel-solution interface. In concrete, however, although the chloride test 
may suggest uniform chloride content with a specific sampling size, the steel surface may be 
exposed to pores with varying chloride concentrations. Therefore, the r ebar-pore solution 
interfaces may have very different chemical environments and hence different corrosion states. If 
electrically isolated, the rebars would corrode at their own corrosion rates. However, they are all 
electrically connected with non-negligible resistance in concrete.  
Suppose we still treat them as uniform corrosion by mathematically describing the anodic and 
cathodic reactions happening all over the steel -concrete interface, which is the basis for the 
commonly used electrochemical tests wit h rebar in concrete. In this case, several consequences 
must be considered: Firstly, the averaged corrosion kinetics would be sensitive to the rebar -pore 
solution interface area and the localized chemistry in pores. This limitation would make the 
averaged corrosion kinetics less generalizable when applied to concrete with different spatial 
moisture content and pore solution compositions commonly found in service environments.  
Secondly, the possibly non -negligible resistance of the concrete cover also affec ts the overall 
corrosion behaviour making the test results unreliable. The previous circuit model (Chapter 5) 
shows that when non -uniform rebar is treated as a homogenized rebar piece, the homogenized 
\nPDF_PAGE 169\n 
 
129 
activation-controlled corrosion kinetics may deviate from the typical Butler -Volmer kinetics of a 
mixed electrode reaction where the resistance between the anodic and cathodic sites are not 
negligible. This deviation can be seen by the distorted polarization curve with a greater icorr and 
apparent Tafel slopes when the polarization curve is forced to be fitted using Butler-Volmer, or its 
Tafel extrapolation. The distortion is more pronounced when the concrete resistivity is high and 
the rebar severely non -uniformly corrodes. It suggests that the rebar’s homogenized corrosion 
kinetics depends on the non -uniformity of the rebar and the resistance between the non -uniform 
rebar segments. 
In the previous lab studies, we conditioned the rebar-embedded concrete samples with the known 
surface areas to a specific moisture content, chloride content, and carbonation  condition in a lab 
(Chapter 4). For each given condition, the homogenized corrosion rate was found quite different 
from what is measured in the bulk solution, even  after the correction of the rebar -pore solution 
area. These results suggest that the concrete pore structures alter the local electrochemistry process 
by potentially changing the active area ratio, the rebar -pore solution area, and the resistance 
between the anodic and cathodic sites. 
The corrosion kinetics for rebar in concrete at a macroscopic level is of great interest from the 
service life prediction and corrosion mitigation perspectives. Without exercising experimental 
tests as was done in Chapter 3 and Chapter 4 with exhausting many major test factors, it is very 
beneficial to figure out the mechanistic link of the rebar corrosion kinetics (including its 
polarization behaviour) between bulk solution and concrete. The results from the corrosion test of 
rebar in a bulk solution can be translated into the rebar corrosion behaviour in concrete. 
In this paper, the representative elementary volume (REV) will be selected based on the pore 
structure. A comprehensive model will be developed to link the rebar corrosion behaviour in the 
pore solution to the overall corrosion behaviour in the REV. The model will describe how water 
and chloride distribution in the concrete affects the distribution of the active and passive rebar 
corrosion sites at the REV scale.  
\nPDF_PAGE 170\n 
 
130 
6.3 Methodology 
6.3.1 Homogenized corrosion kinetics 
The concept is to seek for the averaged corrosion behaviour of rebar in the porous medium. For 
instance, in chloride-contaminated concrete, the localized pore solution may have a non -uniform 
chloride concentration, resulting in non -uniform corrosion state s of rebar ( Figure 6.1). The 
macroscopic representation of these characteristics involves treating the porous medium as a 
continuum with a selected representative elementary volume (REV). The goal is to smooth out the 
localized "spikiness" and obtain REV-level "smoothness". 
 
Figure 6.1 Schematic of rebar condition and chloride concentration distribution: localized 
pore level non-uniformity vs. REV-level homogenization. 
We usually do not have access to the microscopic chloride concentration profile on the rebar-pore 
solution interface. H owever, the averaged total chloride content from the regular drilling test 
(ASTM C1152) provides the chloride content level. This is a volume-averaged value and is related 
to the localized non -uniform chloride concentration. Similarly, the averaged corrosi on 
characteristic is also associated with the rebar-solution interface in the pores in certain respects.  
However, rebar corrosion kinetics cannot be treated the same way as other properties such as 
chloride content. Simply arithmetically averaging the values such as potential and current density, 
assuming localized uniform corrosion with averaged anodic and cathodic reactions over the whole 
surface does not reflect the interactive coupling of localized corrosion kinetics. It is not well 

\nPDF_PAGE 171\n 
 
131 
understood how this “averaged kinetics” is related to localized corrosion kinetics. It is questionable 
if it is even valid to treat rebar corrosion kinetics in a homogenized uniform fashion with the same 
Butler-Volmer kinetics used in the metal -solution system. In the previo us circuit model analysis  
(Li, Evitts, & Boulfiza, 2018) , it has been demonstrated that the homogenized kinetics may 
significantly deviate from the local kinetics (Butler-Volmer equation, activation control) at given 
conditions when the resistivity is high.  
The overall polarization performance or corrosion kinetics can be directly measured in 
experiments, as shown by previous studies (Alonso et al., 1998b; Chang et al., 2008b; Elsener, 
2005; Garcés et al., 2005; Gulikers, 2005; Locke & Siman, 1980; Sohail, 2013). The homogenized 
kinetics then become empirical functions of the material properties and chemical species 
conditions (see Appendix for Chapter 4). It will be tedious and prohibitively time -consuming to 
capture even a few factors (Chapter 3 and Chapter 4). If we can manage to model the underlying 
phenomenon that mechanistically controls the overall corrosion kinetics, then we can simulate all 
the scenarios, which provides a link between the rebar corrosion in pore solution and the rebar 
corrosion in concrete. 
To link the activation -controlled corrosion kinetics in solution to that in concrete, some key 
differences between these environments need to be considered due to the distribution of water and 
chloride in the pore system.  
• the reactive rebar-solution surface area 
• distribution of the active and passive rebar-solution sites 
• ionic resistance between the active and passive corrosion sites 
It is worth mentioning that the restrained oxygen diffusivity can be captured with a limiting current 
at a macroscopic level. Therefore, it is not taken into account at the pore level. 
6.3.2 Corrosion modelling from microscopic (pore-scale) to mesoscopic scale (REV) 
In the proposed model (Figure 6.2), three types of rebar-steel interfaces are considered: “active”, 
“passive”, and “inert”. The rebar -solution interface is assumed to be “reactive,” with either an 
active or passive state. Both anodic and cathodic activation-controlled kinetics occur on the same 
\nPDF_PAGE 172\n 
 
132 
area on each interface in a fashion of uniform corrosion in the bulk solutions. The rebar surface 
exposed to the drained pores or solid matrix, i.e., rebar-gas or rebar-solid interface, is assumed to 
be inert. The homogenized steel-concrete interface of a representative elementary volume (REV) 
is a combination of these interface types: active rebar-solution, passive rebar-solution, and inert. 
The distribution of the three types of interfaces would be affected by pH, Cl, water, and pore size 
distribution at the rebar-concrete interface. 
 
Figure 6.2 A schematic depicting the corrosion of the rebar surface, from pore level to REV. 
The rebar condition within the REV encompasses a range of conditions, whereas at the pore 
level, it is binary. 
The proposed model, as illustrated in Figure 6.3, starts with quantifying the pore size distribution, 
which forms the basis for deriving the moisture and chloride distribution based on the known total 
moisture and chloride content. The moisture and chloride conditions are then used to define the 

\nPDF_PAGE 173\n 
 
133 
corrosion cell at the REV scale. Finally, the corrosion cell is used to gene rate a potentiodynamic 
scan that characterizes the corrosion kinetics. 
 
Figure 6.3 Proposed model for characterizing corrosion kinetics in reinforced concrete: 
schematic representation of the process flow from pore size distribution to corrosion kinetics. 
6.4 Pore model development 
6.4.1 Pore size distribution and water distribution 
The quantification of the pore -water-chloride system starts with determining the pore size 
distribution. Water is distributed in the pores as adsorbed water, capillary -condensed water, and 
vapour. The pore distribution can be determined using the volumetric water content retention curve 
and the classic adsorption -and-condensation model, based on the bundled capillary pore model 
(Daian, 1988). In this section, we implement this method with improvements in mass conservation, 
which are solved through an iterative algorithm. 
As illustrated in Figure 6.4, all pores in the hardened cement paste can be conceptually connected 
into one single pore, with the pore diameter varying across the whole range of pore sizes. The pore 
size distribution can be quantified by the area function 𝑆(𝑟) representing the surface area of pores 
with radii greater than 𝑟 per unit volume of the porous medium. Equivalently, it can als o be 

\nPDF_PAGE 174\n 
 
134 
quantified by the volume function 𝜂(𝑟) which represents the pore volume of the pores with radii 
smaller than 𝑟 per unit volume of the porous medium. 
 
Figure 6.4 Adsorption-condensation model of a capillary pore: Illustration of the 
conceptualization of pores as a single pore with varying diameter. The change in adsorbed 
water (𝚫𝛉𝐚) and condensed water content ( 𝚫𝛉𝐜) is due to an increase in relative humidity  
(𝚫𝒉). 
When the water vapour is neglected, the total volumetric water content contains two parts: 
adsorbed water and condensed capillary water.  In the relative humidity (RH) range from zero to 
near one, there are two regimes: “pure adsorption” and “adsorption + condensation” (Figure 6.5).  

\nPDF_PAGE 175\n 
 
135 
 
Figure 6.5 Schematic depicting the transition from adsorption to adsorption + condensation 
regime as relative humidity increases in pores. 
6.4.1.1 Pure adsorption regime 
In this regime, the adsorbed water is the only contribution to the total water content, given by the 
equation: 
 𝜃 = 𝑒𝑎(ℎ) ∙ 𝑆0 (6.1) 
where, 𝑒𝑎(ℎ) is the water adsorption thickness as a function of the relative humidity, ℎ, 𝑆0 is total 
pore surface area per unit volume, which equals the maximum value of 𝑆(𝑟) when 𝑟 is taken as 
the minimum value. In this paper, the value 1.5 × 107 𝑚2/𝑚3 (Daian, 1988) will be used as an 
initial guess for 𝑆0 in the iterative calibration process with experimental data. 
The volumetric water content 𝜃 attributed by pure adsorption is linearly relate d to the logarithm 
of the metric potential of capillary water (Daian, 1988): 
 log|Φ| = 𝑞𝑓𝑖𝑡 − 𝑠𝑓𝑖𝑡 ∙ 𝜃 (6.2) 

\nPDF_PAGE 176\n 
 
136 
where, 𝑞𝑓𝑖𝑡 and 𝑠𝑓𝑖𝑡 are fitted parameters and the metric potential of capillary water Φ is expressed 
by Kelvin-Laplace Equation: 
 Φ = 𝑝𝑐 = 𝜌𝑙𝑅𝑇𝑀 ln(ℎ) (6.3) 
where, 𝜌𝑙 is density of water, 𝑀 is molar mass of water. This linear relationship Equation (6.2) 
was used to fit the isotherm in the low RH region (adsorption region), and the results were used to 
construct the water adsorption thickness function 𝑒𝑎(ℎ). It is assumed this relationship is also valid 
at higher RH where capillary condensation occurs. 
6.4.1.2 The transition from pure adsorption to adsorption + condensation 
The capillary condensation is caused by the pore water’s concave meniscus where, at equilibrium 
state, the water vapour partial pressure above this meniscus is less than that above a flat water 
surface. The relative humidity, ℎ is related to the meniscus curvature by Kelvin’s Equation: 
 ln(ℎ) = ln ( 𝑝𝑣

In summary, the following improvements has been made compared to (Daian, 1988): 
1) In the adsorption regime, the critical RH is calculated iteratively with the fitted adsorption 
function accounting for the reduction in pore size caused by adsorbed water, which results 
in a lower critical RH. 
2) In the condensation regime, the altered geometry of the adsorbed water volume is treated 
as pipe-like, rather than sheet-like. The effective pore size changes dynamically with the 

\nPDF_PAGE 181\n 
 
141 
adsorbed water layer, leading to a more precise calculation of the condensed water volume 
change within the pores.

6.4.1.6 Determine the proper size of the REV 
At the pore scale, the pore size is sampled from the pore size dist ribution. The chloride 
concentration is also affected by the water saturation condition of the selected pores (adsorbed 
water or condensed water). These randomnesses in sampling will lead to the uncertainty in 
arranging the active and passive rebar -pore solution interface and the resistance between them. 
Therefore, efforts should be made to determine the minimum size of the REV where the volume -
averaged porosity stabilizes and hence stable water content, chloride content. The typical length 
scale of mortar is listed in Table 6.1. The REV size is selected as 1 mm which satisfies the length 
scales criterion  𝐾1 2⁄ ≪ 𝑑 < 𝑙 ≪ 𝐿 for the validity of local volume averaging treatment (Kaviany,

6.4.2 Chloride ion in the pore solution  
While the rebar-pore solution is reactive in corrosion, the corrosion state could be active or passive 
depending on the local pH-Cl ratio. There have been studies in this regard in bulk simulated pore 
solutions. However, there are additional mechanisms inv olved for the dissolved chloride ions in 
pore solution in concrete. First, the total chloride ion amount in the pore solution is less than the 
total chloride content in concrete due to the chloride bonding effect (Glass et al., 1996; Pargar et 
al., 2017). Second, the chloride ions would concentrate in finer pores due to the double layer effect 
(Adamson & Gast, 1997) , and it has been demonstrated that the chloride ion concentration is 
significantly higher in pores with a diameter of a few nanometers (Friedmann et al., 2008).  
Chloride exists in various forms in the concrete. The free chloride (including partially loosely 
bound chloride) is affected by the pore wall distance with respect to the electrical double layer 
formed by the charged solid and adsorbed chloride. The free chloride concentration is higher near 
the pore wall, which is considered the main contributor to the “chloride concentrate” phenomenon 
at a macroscopic scale. The raised concentration is more pronounced near the pore wall, leading 
to a much higher chloride concentration in gel pores (a few nanometres) and smaller capillary 
pores. 
6.4.2.1 Total free chloride: chloride isotherm  
The chloride ions in concrete can be classified into four categories: free, loosely bound, bound and 
strongly bound (Glass et al., 1996). The free chloride is dissolved in the solution and can equalize 
\nPDF_PAGE 188\n 
 
148 
with the external bulk solution; the loosely bound chloride is electrostatically adsorbed on the 
hydration products and can be extracted under high pressure. The rest of the chloride is chemically 
bonded with the chloroaluminate hydrates (AFt and AFm) and forming Fridel’s salt, and they can 
be extracted by acid solution (bound) or strong acid solution (strongly bound). The controlling 
mechanism for each category is summarized in Table 6.3. 
Table 6.3 Classification of chloride, summarized from (Glass et al., 1996; Pargar et al., 2017) 
Cl category      Bound type  Mechanism 
Free             Dissolved in solution Dissolved, high activity 
Loosely bound    Physically bound       Electrostatically with C-S-H, Friedel’s 
salt, Portlandite, adsorbed , low 
activity 
Bound and  
strongly bound   
Chemically bound       Friedel’s salt: chloroaluminate 
hydrates, with AFt, AFm 
The physical chloride adsorption is dominated by C -S-H as the C -S-H gel is the most abundant 
hydration product with a very high specific area. This dominance was shown by comparing a 
surface complexation model and experimental results (Elakneswaran et al., 2009). 
Therefore, the main sources of chloride bonding effects are the C -S-H adsorption (physical) and 
Friedel’s salt formation (chemical). These effects were evaluated in an analytical model based on 
the cement composition (Method 2.3 in Baroghel-Bouny et al., 2012).  
 sCl = sClP + sClc = NC−S−H ∙ αcCl−cOH− + βcCl− + δN(C3A)eq (6.32) 
 N(C3A)eq = NC3A + 0.5 ∙ NC4AF (6.33) 
where sClP  [𝑚𝑜𝑙/𝑘𝑔]  is the physically bound chloride by C-S-H adsorption  per unit weight of 
cement, which is a function of the C-S-H quantity, NC−S−H[𝑚𝑜𝑙/𝑘𝑔 ] per unit weight of cement, 
chloride concentration in solution cCl−[𝑚𝑜𝑙/𝑚3], and hydroxide ion concentration cOH−[𝑚𝑜𝑙/
\nPDF_PAGE 189\n 
 
149 
𝑚3] . the chemically bound chloride  by Friedel’s salt formation  per unit weight of cement  
sClc [𝑚𝑜𝑙/𝑘𝑔] is calculated by the residual quantity of equivalent aluminates  per unite weight of 
cement, N(C3A)eq[𝑚𝑜𝑙/𝑘𝑔], which is a function of the residual quantity of the C3A, NC3A and 
NC4AF. The coefficient 0.5 is arbitrarily chosen to account for weaker contribution from C4AF. The 
coefficients  𝛼 = 0.0603, 𝛽 = 0.0698 and 𝛿 = 1.916 are calibrated parameters based on the 
experiment (Baroghel-Bouny et al., 2012). 
It is noted that Friedel’s salt formation is only considered from the residual calcium aluminates. 
The ion exchange from hydrated AFt and AFm is not taken into account. However, this part of the 
bound chloride is mathematically included on the physical adsorption term.  The chloride binding 
isotherm is shown in Figure 6.11 for cement with NC−S−H = 1.3893 mol/kg  N(C3A)eq=0.0458 
mol/kg.

6.4.2.2 Chloride concentrate effect in fine pores 
With the chloride binding isotherm, the total free chloride content can be calculated from the total 
chloride content. As mentioned in the introduction of this section, the adsorbed ions are separated 
from the free ions in the diffusion layer by the outer Helmholtz plane (OHP) due to the electrical 
double layer (EDL) phenomena. the concentration of the counter -ion increases as it approaches 
the OHP, and the concentration of the co-ion decreases (see Figure 6.12).  
  
Figure 6.12 Schematic of the EDL effect on the 1 -D infinite concentration profile in the 
diffusion layer from OHP. 
The chloride concentrate phenomenon is caused by the positively charged surface of the hydrated 
cement. In small pores, the concentrate effect is more pronounced. The mean value of the chloride 
ion concentration, as co -ion, in the overlapped diffusion layer of a pore can be approximated by 
an analytical expression, Equation (6.34) (Dufrêche et al., 2001; Friedmann et al., 2008)

In summary, with the bound chloride isotherm, the total chloride in the pore solution can be 
determined from the total chloride. The solving procedure is summarized in a flow chart shown in 
Figure 6.13. The concentration of free chloride ions in the pore solution is a function of pore size. 
It can be expressed in terms of chloride concentration in bulk solution (or largest pore), 𝑐𝑏, and the 
pore size distribution and pore water saturation level (covered in previous sections).  The value of 
𝑐𝑏 is unknown and can be solved iteratively such that the sum of the total free chloride in the 
solution and the corresponding bound chloride content equals the total chloride content. When the 
𝑐𝑏 is solved, the averaged chloride concentration in pores, 𝑐̅𝑐0, are simultaneously determined.  
 
Figure 6.13 Flowchart of the iterative solving procedure to determine the chloride 
concentration distribution in pore solution. 
 
The chloride concentration distribution in pores is presented for concrete with a total chloride 
content of 0.6% by weight of cement in Figure 6.14. The chloride ion has a much higher 
concentration in fine pores, which leads to uneven distribution of chloride content at pore level 
even in connected saturated pores. 

\nPDF_PAGE 193\n 
 
153 
 
Figure 6.14 Chloride concentration distribution in saturated pores for concrete with a total 
chloride content of 0.6% by weight of cement. 
 
6.4.2.3 Chloride in unsaturated pores 
The movement of water in concrete has two forms: vapour diffusion and liquid conduction. When 
the concrete is contaminated with chloride and undergoes a drying process, the concentration of 
chloride ions is increased due to the drying of water in vapour form. The water loss due to liquid 
conduction is considered n ot changing the chloride concentration in the pore solution since the 
chloride ions are moved by advection. Therefore, with these two mechanisms, the total chloride in 
the adsorbed water layer is reduced but with a higher concentration.  
While there is little research on the quantification of these two mechanisms on the contribution of 
the chloride movement, we may consider advection ratio ranges from 0 to 1, with 0 being pure 
vapour transport with no chloride moving out the drying pore and 1 being all chloride ions moved 
out of the drying pore through advection. Although moisture transport through vapour phase was 
reported as negligible in a sand column experiment when moisture is greater than 4% by weight 
(Samson et al., 2005), it is suspected that the c ontribution ratio of liquid movement or advection 

\nPDF_PAGE 194\n 
 
154 
may significantly decrease in drier concrete where the liquid phase continuity is significantly 
reduced.  
In addition, when the chloride concentration reaches a saturated level during the drying process, 
and it may also transport through salt creeping (Van Enckevort & Los, 2013) . In this paper, an 
arbitrary factor of 0.5 is used to account for the free chloride amount reduction in drained pores 
due to advection and salt creep. The remained chloride ions remain in the saturated pores with 
finer radius and cause the increase in the chloride concentration. 
The chloride concentrations in the adsorbed water layers are considered to remain the same as the 
saturated condition and are not changed by the concentration gradient-driven diffusion because the 
mobility of ions in an adsorbed layer is reduced at least by five orders of magnitude (Tokunaga et 
al., 2017). 
6.4.3 Allocation of the active and passive rebar-pore solution interface 
With the previous solution fo r the pore size distribution and the adsorption -condensation model 
(Section 6.4.1), plus the chloride concentration distribution as a function of the pore size (Section 
6.4.2), it is, therefore, possible to quantify the local saturation condition and the chloride 
concentration in all pores. The rebar-pore solution interfaces are categorized into four types based 
on the pore saturation condition and [Cl−]/[OH−]value (Table 6.4).  
Table 6.4 Rebar-solution interface types 
 [Cl−]/[OH−] ≥ threshold [Cl−]/[OH−] < threshold 
Saturated pore  
(condensed water) 
Active Passive 
Unsaturated pore  
(adsorbed water) 
Passive Passive 
The [Cl−]/[OH−] ratio in the solution is a criterion determining if the protective film once formed 
in the high pH solution is stable. When the [Cl−]/[OH−] exceeds the threshold value, the protective 
film is broken, and the rebar -solution interface becomes actively corrod ing. However, in 
unsaturated pores with only the adsorbed water, even with the possible high [ Cl−]/[OH−] ratio, 
the rebar is considered passive due to the rust effect. This dense rust formed in the transient from 
\nPDF_PAGE 195\n 
 
155 
wet to dry in the unsaturated pores will effectively passivate the steel surface, and the high 
electrical conductivity of the 𝐹𝑒3+ -rich rust makes it a poor cathode for oxygen reduction 
(Stratmann, 1990). The dried rusted rebar surface also features a more noble corrosion potential, 
i.e., −100 𝑚𝑉𝑆𝐶𝐸 in chloride -contaminated concrete (Alonso et al., 1998b) . Such a passivated 
interface will turn active only  if the pore is re -saturated with the maintained high [ Cl−]/[OH−]. 
Based on the previous rebar corrosion test in the simulated pore solution (Chapter 3), the threshold 
[Cl−]/[OH−] is set as 0.54.  
The combined effect of water and chloride distribution on the corrosion state at the pore level can 
then be quantified with the pore distribution, water distribution and chloride distribution results. 
The area of the active and passive ratio can be calcula ted following the classification shown in 
Table 6.4. 
6.5 Implementation of the corrosion model at the REV level  
The area of those two rebar -pore solution corrosion conditions: active and passive, is calculated 
based on the water and chloride distribution. The corrosion kinetics of the rebar in the simulated 
pore solution (active and passive rebar) in the previous studies ( Chapter 3) will be used to model 
the micro-corrosion cells of the two rebar states and the coupling within the REV.  
6.5.1 Corrosion circuit model 
The information of the active and passive rebar -solution area ratio has been calculated. We can 
incorporate this information into the existing circuit model, which includes active and passive units 
with equivalent resistors (see Chapter 5). It is worth mentioning that although the same model 
scheme is used, the meaning of the “unit” is slightly different. In the previous applications, the 
unit contains the inert interfaces (rebar -solid, and rebar -air), and the corrosion kin etics for each 
unit are the ones measured from the rebar-concrete interface with a linear dependence on the water 
saturation level. In the application for the corrosion model within REV, the unit represents the 
rebar-pore solution interface with the corros ion kinetics which is independent of the water 
saturation level (Figure 6.15). The same solving scheme was used to solve this model (Li, Evitts, 
& Boulfiza, 2018). 
\nPDF_PAGE 196\n 
 
156 
 
Figure 6.15 The illustration of the updated corrosion circuit model that homogenize the 
corrosion kinetics in REV from pore level. 
 
6.5.2 On the limitation of the corrosion circuit model 
Although using equivalent resistors can describe the resistance between the coupled non -uniform 
rebar segments, there is no analytical solution for the equivalent resistance due to the complicated 
potential field in 3-D volume, i.e., concrete. The saturation significantly  affects the resistivity of 
concrete and, at the same time, affects the rebar surface exposed to the solution-filled pores. Also, 
the non-uniform active units and passivated units could distribute randomly on the rebar while 
retaining the same area ratio. This geometrical uncertainty may lead to other effects on the 
homogenized corrosion kinetics of the rebar.  
The rebar’s homogenized polarization behaviour may also be affected by the shape, orientation, 
and distance of the coupling electrode by altering the potential field and current distribution in the 
concrete. The coupling electrode could be the counter -electrode attached to the concrete cover 
surface, similar to the mesh anode used in the impressed -current cathodic protection application. 
It could be an electrically connected rebar with a different electrode rest potential forming a macro-
corrosion cell. 

\nPDF_PAGE 197\n 
 
157 
However, in the application for the rebar-concrete interface in a chosen REV size, i.e. 1 𝑚𝑚3, the 
equivalent resistor and the homogenized corrosion kinetics can be insensitive to the active and 
passive rebar -pore solution interface area sizes and their allocations randomness and counter 
electrode size and location and deviate from the Butler-Volmer equation. It is assumed that is true 
and we proceed to apply the circuit model for the corrosion cell simulation in the REV, and then 
this assumption will be examined by checking the difference between the corrosion current density 
generated from the model and the corrosion current density fitted from the si mulated 
potentiodynamic scan curve. The assumption would be valid if the relative error between the two 
is small. 
6.6 Corrosion kinetics in solution 
The corrosion parameters used for the active rebar -solution interface and passive rebar -solution 
interface are from the previous corrosion test for rebar in simulated pore solutions (Chapter 3). 
The values are listed in Table 6.5.

6.6.1 Mortar REV resistivity 
The resistivity of concrete depends on the resistivity of the pore solution and is dramatically 
affected by the water saturation level. Although the bundled capillary pore model can address the 
pore size distribution problem it does not describe the interconnectivity of these pores. Therefore, 
the complexity of the serial or parallel arrangement of the pores is unknown. There are empirical 
models such as (Weiss et al., 2013) that uses a single exponential component to account for pore 
solution’s volume, concentration, and the interconnectivity. In this paper, the dependence of the 
electrical conductivity of concrete is divided into two parts: the conductivity of the pore solution 
and the volume and connectivity of the pore solution. This can be described by 
\nPDF_PAGE 198\n 
 
158 
 𝜎𝑟𝑒𝑣 = 𝑎 ∙ 〈𝜎𝑠𝑜𝑙〉 ∙ 𝑆𝑒𝑏 (6.41) 
where 𝜎𝑟𝑒𝑣 is the electrical conductivity of the porous medium, 〈𝜎𝑠𝑜𝑙〉 is the local volume-averaged 
electrical conductivity of the pore solution which depends on the water saturation level, 𝑎, 𝑏 are 
fitted parameter with 𝑎 accounting for the baseline pore connectivity and pore solution volume 
fraction, and 𝑏 accounting for the change in interconnectivity due to the change in water saturation 
level. For the resistivity test results of non -carbonated mortar sample with variable chloride 
w/c=0.7, 𝑎 = 0.0125917, 𝑏 = 4.12841666.

6.6.2 Equivalent resistors in the circuit model 
In the equivalent circuit model (Figure 6.16), the internal resistance between the active and passive 
units near the rebar, 𝑅0, is approximated with 𝑅𝑟𝑒𝑣, and the external resistance through the cover 
𝑅𝑒 = 𝑅1𝑅2/(𝑅1 + 𝑅2) is approximated by the infinite grid of resistors with a resistance of 𝑅𝑟𝑒𝑣. 
The total resistance of the infinite grid between two neighbouring points is 1/2 ⋅ 𝑅𝑟𝑒𝑣. Therefore, 
the external resistance 𝑅𝑒 is also 𝑅𝑟𝑒𝑣 and 𝑅1 = 𝑅2 = 2𝑅𝑟𝑒𝑣. The total resistance between two 
units is 1.5 𝑅𝑟𝑒𝑣. This estimation is thought to be reasonable because in a 3 -D concrete domain 
with a volume of 10 cm × 4 cm × 4 cm, the numerical solution of the resistance between the rebar 
surfaces in two neighbouring REVs is 1.74 ⋅ 𝑅𝑟𝑒𝑣.

160

6.7 Simulation results 
6.7.1 The effects of water saturation and chloride content on active rebar-solution 
interface area ratio in concrete 
The area ratio of the active rebar-solution interface as a function of the water saturation level and 
total chloride content is shown in Figure 6.17. The values of water saturation level and chloride 
content were evenly distributed within the ranges of 0.2–0.95 and 0.1%–2.5% by weight of cement, 
respectively.  

\nPDF_PAGE 201\n 
 
161 
 
Figure 6.17 Active rebar-solution surface area  ratio as a function of water saturation and 
total chloride content. 
As shown in Figure 6.17, at high chloride content levels (Cl% >2%), rebar in the saturated pores 
with high chloride ion concentration is active and remains passive in the unsaturated pores. The 
increase of water saturation level leads to a nearly linear increase in active rebar area. At low 
chloride content levels (Cl%<0.4%), the rebar-solution surface is mainly passivated, and the active 
rebar area remains near zero. At moderate chloride content levels, the active rebar -solution area 
ratio can be affected by both chloride content and the water saturation. At these chloride content 
levels, the active rebar -solution increase with the increase in water saturation level but starts to 
become independent to the water saturation level when the saturation reaches a threshold value. 
This saturation threshold value increase with the chloride content. 
6.7.2 Simulated homogenized corrosion parameters for rebar-concrete interface 
The combined effects of water saturation and chloride on the active and passive area and their ratio 
will significantly affect the overall corrosion kinetics at the REV level. With the same solving 

\nPDF_PAGE 202\n 
 
162 
scheme introduced in Chapter 5, the corrosion circuit model returns corrosion parameters such as 
corrosion potential, 𝐸𝑐𝑜𝑟𝑟 , corrosion current density, 𝑖𝑐𝑜𝑟𝑟 , and potentiodynamic scan curves. 
𝐸𝑐𝑜𝑟𝑟 and 𝑖𝑐𝑜𝑟𝑟  are affected by total chloride content and water saturation. They are plotted in 
Figure 6.18 together with the experimental test results from Chapter 4.  The simulated 𝐸𝑐𝑜𝑟𝑟 and 
𝑖𝑐𝑜𝑟𝑟 as a function of chloride content captured the pattern in the experiment data.

Figure 6.18 Corrosion parameters of rebar-concrete interface affected by the total chloride

\nPDF_PAGE 203\n

It was previously assumed that the homogenized corrosion kinetics of the rebar-concrete interface 
simulated in the REV does not deviate from the Butler-Volmer equation. This assumption is now 
examined by checking the difference between the corrosion current density generated from the 
model and the corrosion current density fitted from the simulated potentiodynamic scan curve 
using the Butler -Volmer equation. The relative error between the two is plotted in Figure 6.19. 
The relative error of the corrosion current density is mostly within ±10%. Higher relative error 
values (10%–17.9%) are observed among the low 𝑖𝑐𝑜𝑟𝑟 values under the condition of high chloride 
content and low water saturation levels, which results in a decreased absolute error. This error is 
considered insignificant comparing to the experimental random error. Therefore, the homogenized 
corrosion kinetics in REV can be described with the Butler -Volmer equation. This allows the 
application of the homogenized corrosion kinetics in the form of Butler -Volmer equation on the 
rebar-concrete interface similar to the application of volume-averaged properties such as porosity, 
permeability, and diffusivity in cracked concrete structures, where the spatial variation of these 
factors is common. 
 
(a) 
 
(b) 
Figure 6.19 The relative error of corrosion current density generated from the model and 
the corrosion current density fitted from the simulated potentiodynamic scan curve using 
the Butler-Volmer equation. (a)  relative error – 𝒊𝒄𝒐𝒓𝒓 relationship (b) relative error - water 
saturation and chloride content relationship. 
6.8 Conclusion 
This paper aims to model the averaged rebar corrosion behaviour in the representative elementary 
volume (REV) in concrete from total wate r and chloride content and the rebar corrosion 
characteristics in solution.  

\nPDF_PAGE 204\n 
 
164 
A novel and comprehensive model framework was developed to link the corrosion -affecting 
factors, such as water content and chloride content, at a macroscopic level to their non -uniform 
distributions at the pore level in a quantitative manner.  
The pore size distribution determined by the water retention curve was used to quantify the water 
distribution at a given total moisture content. The water distribution at the pore level was 
determined through an adsorption -condensation model, and the rebar -solution area for mortar in 
different water saturation levels was quantified. The chloride distribution at the pore level captured 
the bonding effect, chloride concentrate effect due to the electrical double layer.  
The rebar corrosion states at the pore level were determined by the combined effects of the non -
uniform distribution of water and chloride concentration at the pore level , which were  derived 
from the macroscopic water content and chloride content. The water distribution control led the 
rebar-solution area, while the water saturation and chloride concentration at the pore level 
controlled the active and passive areas. 
The corrosion model implemented the simulated allocation of the active and passive rebar-solution 
interfaces at pore level within the REV (l 𝑚𝑚3 ) and modelled the homogenized corrosion 
parameters as a function of the water saturation level, and chloride conten t. These corrosion 
parameters can be used to model rebar corrosion in cracked concrete structures, where the spatial 
variation of these factors is often present. 
6.9 Epilogue 
Chapter 6 provides a crucial bridge between the homogenized corrosion parameters disc ussed in 
Chapter 5 and the localized corrosion parameters at the pore scale. By leveraging experimental 
insights and the corrosion circuit model, this chapter provides a modelling approach to derive the 
environment-dependent corrosion kinetics that can be used in the comprehensive corrosion model. 
The next chapter will discuss the simulation of the microenvironment evolution at the rebar depth 
of concrete structure in its service conditions: the “environment model”. 
 
\nPDF_PAGE 205\n 
 
165 
7 Effect of Accurate Microclimate Representation on the Corrosion Performance of a 
Bridge Subjected to Carbonation and Chloride Attack  
7.1 Preface 
In line with the previous chapter's focus on environment -dependent corrosion kinetics, Chapter 7 
continues on the simulation of the microenvironment evolution at the rebar depth of concrete 
structures under service conditions. The developed model is named "environment model" in the 
project objectives.  In this model, a series of transport models for porous media were employed to 
simulate the transport of water, chloride ions, and carbon dioxide gas in concrete. The model uses 
a realistic representation of the microclimate immediately surrounding the bridge in terms of 
temperature, relative humidity, and precipitation. Sensors were also installed for in-situ monitoring 
of oxygen content, moisture, chloride, and carbonation in the concrete cover. 
This model was applied to an uncracked RC structure — an old concrete arch bridge subjected to 
service conditions with the source of chloride, ca rbonation, varying air relative humidity, and 
exposure to unevenly sheltered run-off water. The modelled microenvironment at the rebar depth 
determines the rebar corrosion rate by employing empirical uniform corrosion kinetics specifically 
for the century -old steel rebar.  It also explores the impact on rebar section loss from different 
climate scenarios, such as elevated CO2 levels and changes in precipitation. 
Simulation results for the past 100 years of service agree well with the current condition of th e 
bridge while results for 50 years into the future suggest that for this bridge, as well as for other 
existing bridges, for which design did not consider the effects of climate change may deteriorate 
more prematurely than originally planned. The implicati ons of climate change on the corrosion 
performance of concrete structures and their resilience over the next 50 years are discussed. 
The manuscript of this chapter titled “ Effect of Accurate Microclimate Representation on the 
Corrosion Performance of a Bridge Subjected to Carbonation and Chloride Attack” was submitted 
to Case Studies in Construction Materials. Gang Li is the first author who designed and performed 
most of the experimental study, numerical modelling, and data analysis. Dr. Moh Boulfiza 
contributed to providing feedback on the experiment design and simulation methods. Both Dr. 
Moh Boulfiza and Dr. Richard Evitts contributed to the review of the manuscript and provided 
feedback. 
\nPDF_PAGE 206\n 
 
166 
7.2 Introduction   
Infrastructure is critical to modern society, and b ridges are essential components of the 
transportation network. The long -term durability of these structures is crucial to ensuring public 
safety and avoiding costly repairs or replacements. However, the durability of bridges is heavily 
influenced by the se rvice environment, including factors such as temperature, humidity, 
precipitation, and exposure to de -icing salts or seawater. With the observed trends of climate 
change, it is crucial to understand how these environmental factors impact the corrosion 
performance of bridges.  
The service environment  can create varying microenvironments around the reinforcing rebars. 
Exposed weather conditions, chloride from different sources, carbonation of concrete, and the 
geometry of the structural member all affect the concrete condition and hence the corrosion rate 
of embedded steel rebars. To model the corrosion performance of bridges accurately, existing 
approaches have used empirical and numerical models. 
Empirical models (Alonso et al., 1988; DURACRETE, 1999; Liu & Weyers, 1998; Vu et al., 2005) 
relate corrosion rate with various factors but may not capture exposure conditions and concrete 
conditions in practical environments. Numerical models (Ghods et al., 2007; Isgor & Razaqpur, 
2006; Kim & Kim, 2008; Kranc et a l., 1992; Muthulingam & Rao, 2015; Sohail, 2013; Yu, Liu, 
& Li, 2017; Zacaruk, 2013) use electrochemical corrosion kinetics to model the corrosion rate, but 
rely on accurate corrosion parameters that have a larger scatter, especially in concrete, and fac e 
challenges when micro -cell corrosion is dominant  (Alonso et al., 1998b; Chang et al., 2008b; 
Elsener, 2005; Garcés et al., 2005; Gulikers, 2005; Locke & Siman, 1980; Sohail, 2013). Moisture 
and ion transport in concrete is commonly modelled using the diffusion -advection-dispersion 
model, but sudden precipitation events require small time steps that make s it computationally  
expensive to solve these models (Isgor & Razaqpur, 2004; Samson et al., 2005; Scheffler & Plagge, 
2010). 
In this context, modelling the corrosion of old reinforced concrete bridges, which often experience 
an overall impact from carbonation and chloride contamination with seasonal precipitation, 
presents additional challenges. The overall corrosion rate is closer to uniform corrosion, except for 
the very localized corrosion due to localized damage (cracking). To address these challenges, a 
\nPDF_PAGE 207\n 
 
167 
new approach has been proposed, which handles the rapid changing and on -and-off precipitation 
using a smooth equivalent boundary condition to capture the moisture fluctuation at the rebar depth 
in a large-scale 3-D model. Given that the bridge under study was built more than 100 years ago 
(commissioned in 1916), it was imperative to make sure that the material parameters used in the 
corrosion and transport models provided a realistic representation of the materials used in those 
days. Cores of actual concrete and rebars were extracted from the arch and used to determine the 
transport properties of concrete (porosity, permeability, etc.) and the po re solution composition 
which was used later as the electrolyte in electrochemical potentiodynamic tests to determine the 
corrosion behaviour of those old rebars.  The outcomes of the tests revealed that the concrete and 
steel reinforcements used were sign ificantly different from modern concrete and steel to warrant 
site-specific calibration for the material properties that need to be used for a reliable prediction of 
the future performance of old bridges. 
Accurate microclimate modelling can provide valuable insights into the local environment in the 
vicinity of a structure, and how it affects the structure’s durability. In this paper, the effect of 
accurate microclimate modelling on the corrosion performance of a bridge was investigated. A 
realistic represe ntation of the microclimate immediately surrounding the bridge, incorporating 
temperature, relative humidity, and precipitation, and the use of in-situ sensors to monitor critical 
parameters such as oxygen content, moisture, chlorides, and carbonation in the concrete cover are 
presented. The study highlights the importance of reliable tools and degradation models to assess 
the resilience of structures operating in harsh corrosive environments, and the implications of 
climate change on their long-term durability. 
7.3 Research significance 
With the observed trends of climate change, it is crucial to understand how environmental factors 
impact the corrosion rate of reinforced concrete, and this study presents a new approach to 
accurately model and predict corrosion  performance. The proposed methodology overcomes the 
limitations of existing models, which can be critical for assessing the resilience of structures 
operating in harsh corrosive environments. 
\nPDF_PAGE 208\n 
 
168 
The proposed model used meteorological data to simulate the con dition of an old concrete arch 
bridge. The smoothed equivalent pressure head boundaries were used to reduce the computational 
cost while retaining adequate accuracy.  
The optical fibre oxygen sensor was first employed to measure the actual oxygen content 
distribution on the rebar of the reinforced arch structure. The test results set oxygen as a controlled 
variable for the corrosion model.  
The rebar corrosion kinetics were tested based on the extracted concrete pore solutions and the 
site-extracted one-century-old rebars with the oxygen content measured from the arch structure. 
The synergistic effect of pH and chloride on the corrosion kinetics was quantified in the model.  
The approach combines field test s and numerical modelling and provides a more reliable 
prediction of the service life. 
Overall, this study highlights the importance of reliable tools and degradation models for ensuring 
the longevity and safety of critical infrastructure in the face of changing environmental conditions. 
7.4 Field and experimental investigation 
The University Bridge in Saskatoon is a multi -span concrete arch bridge that entered service in 
1916. It spans the South Saskatchewan River between 25th Street on the West bank and College 
Drive on the East bank  (Figure 7.1a). Over the years, the bridge has undergone repairs and 
modifications focusing on the deck, sidewalks, railings, and spandrel walls, while the arch 
component remains a century old. 
The moisture content and oxygen content were measured at multiple locations in a diagonal pattern 
on the arch. The locations of the probes are shown in Figure 7.1b. The average moisture content 
through the concrete cover was measured with time-domain reflectometry (TDR) probes that were 
calibrated for the old concrete. The oxygen level was measured by the optical oxygen sensors at 
the rebar depths. The measured m oisture and oxygen levels in August 2014 are shown in Figure 
7.2a and  Figure 7.2b.

that had high moisture content near the top of the arch and was located towards the interior of the 
arch.  This outlier was later identified due to a leakage in the drainage pipe near that location. 
These results demonstrate that the deck, curtain walls, and spandrel walls provide varying 
sheltering for the arch from the rain or run -off water from the deck. The uneven moisture 
distribution may cause varying corrosion conditions in the arch. At the location of the highest 
moisture content near the pier, the mapped half -cell potential (Figure 7.2c) shows very negative 
readings (-350 – -416 mV CSE), which corresponds to a corrosion probability of “more than 90%” 
according to the ASTM standard (ASTM International, 2015). 
The oxygen content results show a uniform saturated oxygen content (8.2mg/L at 25 °C) at all 
locations regardless of the varying moisture content and the possible varying corr osion rate. This 
shows that for the current condition of the arch, the concrete cover does not limit oxygen transport 
in the corrosion process. It is even this case for the location where the possible high corrosion rate 
(high consumption rate of oxygen) and high moisture content (low gas permeability) coexist. This 
observation ruled out the possibility that the air -dried concrete may limit oxygen transport when 
the corroding rebar has a larger area (Hussain, 2011). Therefore, the oxygen content was set t o 
100% saturated, a controlled variable, when constructing the corrosion model.

The experimental investigation then provides the material properties for the numerical model. 
Twelve concrete cores were removed from the arch . The locations of these cores are shown in 
Figure 7.1b. The pore solutions were extracted using the centrifuge technique (see Appendix 7.9.1). 
The chemical composition was tested  (see Appendix 7.9.2), based on which the simulated pore 
solution was produced. The simulated pore solution was then used for the electrochemical test of 
the rebar to provide input to the corrosion model. These concrete cores were also used to test the 
transport characteristics such as permeability, chloride diffusiv ity (Ramadani, 2013), and water 
retention curve (Appendix 7.9.3) for the numerical model. 
7.5 Numerical modelling 
The model consists of two components: the transport  model and the corrosion model. The 3 -D 
transport model defines the geometry of the arch component, where the governing equations that 
describe the transport of water, chloride ion, and carbon dioxide gas were applied. Different 
boundary conditions were al so selected to properly reproduce the exposure environment of the 
arch. The corrosion model predicts the corrosion rate based on the microenvironment at the rebar 
depth simulated by the transport model.  The models were developed with a commercial FEM 
software, COMSOL Multiphysics and solve on the Plato high -performance computing cluster at 
the University of Saskatchewan. 
7.5.1 Transport model 
7.5.1.1 Water flow in concrete 
In many practical conditions, a porous medium like concrete is not fully saturated. The degree of 
saturation depends on the surrounding environmental moisture conditions. When concrete is not 
fully saturated, a negative capillary pressure develops and facilitates the absorption of water, and 
this pressure is the driving force of water flow wi thin concrete. Under such conditions, the 
permeability becomes dependent on the water content. Darcy’s law has been extended to describe 
water flow for this kind of problem in unsaturated porous media. One of the earliest and most 
widely used models for wa ter flow in unsaturated porous media is known as Richard’s equation. 
When coupled with the mass balance equation for an unsaturated porous medium, the governing 
equations become

7.5.1.3 Carbonation 
Carbonation is modelled as gas diffusion with the sink term representing the consumption of CO2 
due to the carbonation reaction (Equation 7.11 and 7.12). The calculated concentration of carbon 
dioxide is calibrated to pH in a carbonated synthetic pore solution. 
 
𝜕[𝜖𝑔〈𝜌𝐶𝑂2〉𝑔]
𝜕𝑡 = ∇ ∙ [𝐷𝑒𝑓𝑓𝑔 ∇〈𝜌𝐶𝑂2〉𝑔] + sink (7.11) 
 sink = −Φ‘(Se)[1 − Φ(ϵs)]𝜖𝑔〈𝑚̇ 𝐶𝑂2〉𝑔 (7.12)

density change of the CO2 in the gas phase due to carbonation reaction,  Φ(ϵs) and Φ‘(Se) are 
factors considering the effect of carbonation completeness and moisture content. It is assumed that 
CO2 will dissolve in the pore solution and instantaneously react with Ca(OH)2 dissolved in the 
solution. The resultant CaCO3 precipitates onto the solid surface. The total reaction is described as 
 Ca(OH)2(s) + CO2(g) H2O→  CaCO3(s) + H2O(l) (7.13) 
The Deposition of CaCO3will increase the solid volume. The carbonation reaction keeps going 
until certain layers of CaCO3 form on the surface of concrete pores. It is assumed that the partially 
covered solid surface will not sufficiently supply Ca(OH)2 to the pore solution hence a reduced 
CO2 consumption rate. This phenomenon is accounted for by a reduction function of carbona tion 
degree Φ(ϵs), which is the ratio of the increased solid volume fraction to its maximum, expressed 
in Equation (7.15). Eventually, a maximum solid volume fraction will be reached, and the 
\nPDF_PAGE 217\n 
 
177 
carbonation reaction stops. The effect of moisture content is accounted for by the factor Φ‘(Se) 
(Saetta et al., 1993).

7.5.1.4 The implementation of boundary conditions to different surfaces of the arch 
The field investigation showed that the structure was sheltered with varying effectiveness. It is 
necessary to mark the arch component into three categories (Figure 7.4): 
• Sheltered area: all the columns, curtain walls, arch soffit, and selected arch top area that 
are well protected by spandrel walls and deck. 
• Unsheltered area: sides of the arch exposed to the water. 
• Partially sheltered are a: the selected arch top that is not sufficiently protected by the 
spandrel walls and deck. 
\nPDF_PAGE 218\n 
 
178 
 
Figure 7.4 Sheltering categories of the arch: Sheltered, Unsheltered, and Partially sheltered 
areas based on field investigation findings. 
 
7.5.1.5 Boundary conditions for water flow in concrete 
Pressure head is a function of temperature and relative humidity as expressed in Equation (7.7). It 
was chosen as the boundary conditions, which represent the moisture environments the concrete 
arches were exposed to, and fundamentally affect the transport properties of arch concrete. T he 
raw weather data (temperature, RH, precipitation) were collected from two weather stations: 
SASKATOON DIEFENBAKER INT'L A, and SASKATOON RCS.  All raw hourly data were 
smoothed through a moving average filter.  
• Sheltered area 
Hourly and daily data from the years 2007–2014 and normalized monthly data from year 1981 to 
year 2010 were used to determine the averaged boundary conditions for the sheltered portion of 
the arch. The averaged value of the pressure head was determined through non -linear regression 
analysis (Figure 7.5). The averaged boundary conditions were applied to the sheltered area (Figure 
7.4). 
 

\nPDF_PAGE 219\n 
 
179 
 
(a) 
  
(b) 
Figure 7.5 Moisture boundary conditions for the sheltered portion of an arch: (a) Raw data 
of pressure head obtained from weather stations; (b) averaged data fitted from the raw data. 
• Unsheltered areas 
The unsheltered areas are exposed to both air moisture and rain. Figure 7.6 shows the unsheltered 
boundary condition for the year 2013, where the vertical lines switch pressure head instantly to 0 
representing soaking wet on the concrete surface when the rain amount is greater than 1 mm. These 

\nPDF_PAGE 220\n 
 
180 
on-and-off rain incidents, however, are not favourable in terms of computation cost: smaller time 
step needs to be used to prevent skipping the rain incidents, and during the rain incidents because 
of the drastic change of the boundary condition, extra smaller time steps were needed for the solver 
to find converged solutions. The timing of rain incidents is usually handled directly by imposing 
a water flux (Künzel, 1995) or setting the pressure head as zero. As mentioned in the introduction, 
this rapid change in the boundary condition is not suitable for the large -scale 3-D model. There 
are practices to allocate the monthly total rain hours evenly throughout the month, hence repeated 
wetting-drying cycles in a given month (Shimomura, 2015). Although their sensitivity analysis 
shows that this approximation gives acceptable accuracy, this approximation still retains rapid 
change of the boundaries. 
 
Figure 7.6 Moisture boundary conditions for the unsheltered portion of the arch (eg, Year 
2013). 
In this paper, an equivalent boundary condition is proposed to speed up the solving in the large -
scale 3 -D model. The equivalent boundary condition was sought to produce similar moisture 
content near the rebar depth (10 cm) as the original “spiky” boundary conditions in a 1 -D model 
where the top surface is unsheltered, subject to rain and air, and the bottom surface is sheltered 
subject to only air. A reverse modelling iterative workflow was developed to find such boundary 
conditions (BC). As illustrated in Figure 7.7, the saturation results from the 5 cm depth were used 
as the initial value for the updated top BC, and the 10 cm depth saturation level is set as the target 
value for the updated model to approach at the same depth. The deviation of the saturation level at 
the 10 cm depth in the updated model  was used to adjust the previous BC assignment with a 

\nPDF_PAGE 221\n 
 
181 
relaxation constant ranging from 0 to 1. This iterative workflow ensures that the saturation level 
acquired by the equivalent BC gives a similar saturation as the original boundary conditions with 
the occurrence of rain incidents. 
 
Figure 7.7 Workflow to get the equivalent boundary conditions for the arch top surface 
exposed to ambient humidity and rain. 
Following this workflow, the original boundary condition data from 2009–2014 was used to create 
the original water saturation level at 5 cm, 10 cm, and 15 cm. The average saturation level at 10 
cm was then fitted and used as the target for the equivalent BC to approach. The simulations ran 
for three repeating years for each year’s BC to get a stable moisture distribution. The saturation 
levels at three depths were compared between the full spiky BC and the equivalent BC, as shown 
in Figure 7.8. The smooth equivalent boundary captured the seasonal moisture fluctuation at all 
the selected depths. 

\nPDF_PAGE 222\n 
 
182 
 
Figure 7.8 Comparison of the saturation results simulated from the equivalent boundary 
conditions vs. the spiky boundary conditions of year 2009 -2014 for the unsheltered portion 
of the arch. 

\nPDF_PAGE 223\n 
 
183 
• Partially sheltered area 
For the areas exposed to a varying combination of air moisture and rain , three 2-D linear ramp 
functions were used to describe the varying combinations, such that the weights of the sheltered 
conditions and unsheltered conditions were 0 and 1 on the edge of the arch and 1 and 0 on the 
diagonal line on the arch (Figure 7.4). 
7.5.1.6 Boundary conditions for chloride ion transport and carbonation 
Boundary conditions for chloride ion transport consisted of two conditions: “concent ration” and 
“no-flux”. Under the “concentration” condition, the concrete surface was subject to 3%  wt. NaCl 
solution, while in “no -flux,” there was zero flux of chloride ions across the surface and existing 
chloride ions on the surface transport with the c oncentration gradients and water flow in the  
concrete domain. The “concentration” boundary condition was implemented for 90 days of 
simulated annual time from May 15 to July 15 due to a high volume of precipitation and the 
washed-off accumulated de -icing salts on deck from the winter season. For the rest of the time, 
“no-flux” was implemented. These conditions were applied to the sheltered and partially sheltered 
regions as illustrated in Figure 7.4. 
For the carbonation process, a constant 0.035% CO2 concentration was applied to all exposed arch 
areas, including curtain walls, columns, arch top and soffit. 
7.5.2 Corrosion model 
In this large-scale 3-D model, the corrosion rate of rebar in concrete was estimated based on the 
chloride concentration, pH, and s aturation in concrete at rebar depths. The microenvironment 
difference in concrete resulting from the sheltering effect is expected to have a large length scale 
over the arch. Therefore, the macro -cell coupling in the rebar mesh is considered negligible, a nd 
the microcell form is the major contributor to total corrosion. 
The microcell corrosion model was constructed, avoiding the use of modelling of microcell 
corrosion with the macro -cell fashion, where the single set of corrosion parameters and discrete 
anodes and cathodes and their area ratios  are used. T herefore, the associated limitations such as 
the need for define the anode and cathode area ratio, discretizing the anode and cathode surface at 
\nPDF_PAGE 224\n 
 
184 
a microscopic scale, were avoided. Instead, finding and solv ing the corrosion current density for 
rebar exposed to different chemical environments was done by the potentiodynamic scans of rebars 
in the simulated pore solution with three levels of chloride concentration (0 M, 0.01 M, 0.2 M), 
three levels of pH (8.5, 12, 13.5), which was described in the previous work (Kennell et al., 2015).  
The simulated pore solution used in the test was formulated based on the pore solution from the 
concrete cores from the arch of the bridge. Pore solution was extracted with the  centrifuge 
technique (Appendix 7.9.1), and the chemical composition of the major ions are tested with

7.6 Simulation results and discussion 
For the simulated case of 100 years of exposure, the predicted distribution of pH and concentration 
of chlorides are plotted in Figure 7.11 and Figure 7.12, respectively. As the carbonation and 
chloride ion ingress proceeded through the concrete cover, the once passivated rebar became active 
and thus had a higher corrosion rate occurred. The corrosion loss in thickness was calculated by 
the integration of the corrosion rate over time. The results of corrosion loss in thickness on the 
rebar after 100 years of exposure are plotted in Figure 7.13 and the prediction of additional 
corrosion between 101 years and 150 years of service is plotted in Figure 7.14. 
 
Figure 7.11 pH distribution in Arch C  (Bay 5, Bay 6, Bay 7) after 100 years of carbonation.

Figure 7.12 Chloride concentration distribution in Arch C  (Bay 5, Bay 6, Bay 7) after 100 
years of exposure. 
As shown in Figure 7.13, where the corrosion thickness loss is plotted on log scale, the majority 
of corrosion is predicted to occur on the upper layer of lo ngitudinal rebars that are close to the 
most exposed arch edge where the highest localized corrosion (0.2 mm and 0.1 mm loss in 
thickness) is predicted on that layer of rebars near the columns. High corrosion losses are mainly 
found on the two longitudinal rebars near the most exposed edges, and occasionally on the upper 
layer rebars in the partially sheltered area near the curtain walls. These results agreed well with the 
current condition of the arch, where recent concrete cover patches were found near th e high 
corrosion loss region.

The upper layers of rebar in the partially sheltered area have both carbonation and chloride 
exposure to a lesser extent than the arch edge rebars, and thus have a moderate predicted corrosion 
loss. In the sheltered area of the arch, a low corrosion rate shows that without exposure to direct 
rain and runoff water, chloride transport has a limited effect on corrosion. The vertical column 
rebars have an insignificant corrosion loss due to limited exposure to moisture, even though these 
rebars have the least concrete cover thickness and the carbonation-induced pH drop would increase 
the overall corrosion rate. If this part of the bridge remains untreated for another 50 years, corrosion 
loss is predicted to increase signi ficantly due to deeper carbonation and more accumulation of 
chloride ions. As shown in Figure 7.14, the maximum additional loss in thickness is slightly larger 
than that in the previous 100 years altogether. The severity of corrosion in a partially saturated 
area approaches the condition in the most exposed area. It is suggested to take proactive 
rehabilitation actions in the next few years to prevent major repairs in the long run. Such actions 
could include one or more of the following: lowing the moisture content in the concrete by the 
application of surface sealant, cathodic protection for the most severe hot spots, or increasing the 
pH, by re-alkalization of the old concrete cover.

7.6.1 Climate change: the effect of elevated CO2 concentration and change in 
precipitation on rebar corrosion 
The impact of climate change on the built environment may exacerbate the problem of rebar 
section loss in arches . Climate change will lead to more frequent and intense extreme weather 
events, changes in temperature and precipitation patterns, which can all affect the corrosion and 
mechanical properties of rebar. In particular, changes in moisture and CO2  can alter the 
microenvironment in concrete that affects rebar corrosion and section loss. 
In this study, we explore d the impact of various climate scenarios on the probability density 
distribution of rebar section loss over a 150-year period, encompassing both the present (100 years) 
and the next 50 years. Simulation results for the past 100 years of service agree well with the field-
surveyed condition of the arch in 2014 , where concrete patches were made in the most exposed 
area with high rebar section loss/ru st accumulation (Figure 7.15). For the next 50 years into the 
future, we consider three different climate scenarios, as shown in Table 7.2. Scenario 1: baseline, 
Scenario 2: with increased CO2 and higher precipitation, Scenario 3: with increased CO2 and lower 

\nPDF_PAGE 231\n 
 
191 
precipitation due to drought. The increasing CO2 emission is linearly extrapolated from the historic 
atmospheric CO2 concentration (US Department of Commerce). The change of CO2 concentration 
is shown in Figure 7.16. 
 
Figure 7.15 Field observation showing concrete patches for corrosion -induced damage on 
Arch C in the unsheltered area  where high corrosion s ection loss/rust accumulation was 
predicted.

A comparison of rebar section loss over a service life in the next 50 years in these three different 
climate scenarios are presented in Table 7.3. The section loss difference in Scenario 2 and Scenario 
3 compared to the baseline scenario is calculated as a percentage change.  
In Scenario 2, the carbonation and chloride penetration progress faster compared to the baseline 
Scenario 1. The rebar depassivation occurs earlier than the baseline scenario. The total rebar 
section loss in 50 years significantly increases in the sheltered and partially sheltered area, by more 
than 30 times for the maximum section loss. Scenario 2 not only increases the maximum section 
loss but also exacerbates broader corrosion section loss in the partially sheltered area. The sheltered 
area showed little difference in rebar section loss.  
In Scenario 3, the depassivation of rebar is expedited due to faster carbonation, despite the reduced 
chloride concentration at rebar depth, when compared to Scenario 1 (the baseline). However, 
reduced moisture levels would keep the active corrosion rate at a lower rate. Scenario 3 reduced 
the accumulated section loss. The greatest reduction (greater than 80%) is found in sheltered and 
partially sheltered areas. It also brought about a 30% reduction in section loss for th e rebar under 
or near the sheltering-influence area, such as the bottom layer rebar and adjacent rebars.
