To visualize the distribution of section loss for the three Scenarios over time, a violin plot (Figure 
7.17) was used to show the distribution of section loss on all rebars at a given time, with mean 
values connected by dashed lines. The findings suggest that the severity and frequency of rebar 
section loss have increased in the 50 years, particularly with Scenario 2 (increased CO2 and 
participation) where the rebar section loss is significantly increased.  In Scenario 3, the distribution 
curve shape of rebar section loss remains largely unchanged when compared to Scenario 1. 
However, a slight decrease in the distribution of high section loss values has led to a noticeable 
reduction in the mean value. As such, while Scenario 3 effectively suppresses high rebar section 
loss, it does not have a significant impact on overall corrosion deterioration.  
 
Figure 7.17 Rebar section loss across the arch over 150 years in service. The last 50 years has 
different climate scenarios. Scenario 1: Baseline,  Scenario 2: increased 𝐂𝐎𝟐and wet (20% 
more precipitation), Scenario 3: increased 𝐂𝐎𝟐 and drought (20% less precipitation). Each 
violin represents the distribution density of the section loss  over all rebars at a given time. 
Mean values of section loss are connected by dashed lines. 
The findings indicate the importance of considering climate change in the maintenance and 
management of infrastructure. For example, in Scenario 2, where increased CO2 and precipitation 
exacerbate rebar section loss, it is recommended to prioritize mainten ance and repair activities in 
areas prone to corrosion. This includes the implementation of regular inspections, and timely repair 

\nPDF_PAGE 235\n 
 
195 
of corroded areas. Additionally, it is essential to improve drainage systems by cleaning off debris 
and bird drop pings in the  way. In Scenario 3, where high rebar section loss is suppressed but 
overall corrosion deterioration remains unaffected, it is recommended to allocate budget to further 
large-scale repair and replacement. 
7.7 Conclusions 
A 3-D transport model was developed to simulate the microenvironment changes at the rebar depth 
in a concrete arch bridge exposed to various environmental factors such as chloride, carbonation, 
humidity, and runoff water. It incorporates real-world data from in-situ sensors monitoring oxygen 
content, moisture, chlorides, and carbonation in the concrete cover to create a computationally 
efficient and realistic microclimate depiction around the bridge .  The corrosion model expressed 
the corrosion current density as a function of pH and chloride co ncentration, capturing their 
synergistic effect, was directly from the electrochemical test of the old rebar and formulated pore 
solution with in-situ concrete pore solution composition.  It predicted rebar corrosion rates using 
century-old steel rebar corr osion kinetics and examine d the impact of climate scenarios like 
elevated CO2 levels and precipitation changes on rebar deterioration.  
The outcomes of this study hold significant implications for the understanding and management 
of corrosion in reinforced concrete structures. Some of the main findings are as follows: 
1) The field optical fibre sensor measurements verified the oxygen saturation level and 
eliminated the possibility that there was a limited cathodic current caused by the hindered 
oxygen transport.  
2) The microenvironment, including chloride content, pH, and water saturation may not be 
uniform across the whole reinforced concrete structure due to different exposure conditions.  
3) In the simulated University Bridge arch component, a combination of carb onation and 
chloride ingress accelerates the corrosion rates of rebar.  Ineffective sheltering from the 
rain or more rainwater retention on the arch facilitates this penetration and leads to higher 
corrosion rates. 
\nPDF_PAGE 236\n 
 
196 
4) The study also investigated the effects o f different climate scenarios on rebar section loss 
in arches over a 50-year period. Elevated CO2 level and increased participation result in a 
substantial increase in rebar section loss, exacerbating broader corrosion section loss, 
whereas elevated CO2 level and decreased participation suppresses high rebar section loss 
but does not have a significant impact on overall corrosion deterioration. Accordingly, 
different maintenance and management of infrastructure are recommended. 
Overall, this study's finding s provide crucial insights into the complex interactions between 
climate, microenvironment, and corrosion behavior in reinforced concrete structures. By 
incorporating these findings into practical applications, such as infrastructure maintenance and 
management, it is possible to enhance the resilience and durability of these structures, ensuring 
their long-term performance and safety. 
7.8 Epilogue 
Chapter 7 presents a comprehensive 3 -D transport model to simulate the microenvironment 
evolution at the rebar depth of concrete structure in its service conditions. This model was applied 
to an uncracked RC structure subjected to service conditions with t he source of chloride, 
carbonation, varying air relative humidity, and exposure to unevenly sheltered run -off water. To 
formulate realistic boundary conditions for wetting and drying scenarios, historical weather data 
and field monitoring data were utilized.  
Although for this uncracked arch component, the empirical corrosion kinetics specifically for the 
century-old steel rebar was used, the presented modelling method can be applied to simulate the 
microenvironment evolution at the rebar depth in cracked c oncrete under realistic exposure 
conditions. A comprehensive corrosion model can then be developed in the next chapter by 
connecting the environment-dependent corrosion kinetics with the environment model.

8 Comprehensive Corrosion Model: Presentation and Validation 
8.1 Preface 
In Chapter 7, we explored the application of a comprehensive 3D transport model to simulate the 
microenvironment evolution at the rebar depth in concrete structures under various service 
conditions. Continuing the investigation from the previous chapter, C hapter 8 focuses on the 
formulation of a comprehensive corrosion model that incorporates crack configuration into the 
existing environment model. This model simulates the evolution of moisture and chloride content 
on embedded rebars in cracked concrete.  It employs homogenized corrosion kinetics as functions 
of the microenvironmental conditions on the embedded rebar to solve the potential and current 
field in the concrete domain and rebar surface. 
To validate the model, a second phase experimental program was designed, specifically targeting 
the validation of segmented rebars in cracked concrete specimens subject to wetting and drying 
cycles with chloride salt solutions.  
With the validated model, the next chapter will be devoted to a numerical exploration o f the 
possible mechanisms causing the marked different observations in rebar corrosion in cracked 
concrete. 
8.2 Introduction 
The most significant effect of concrete cracking on rebar corrosion is the formation of a macro -
cell that may significantly increase th e corrosion rate. It has been observed that passive 
reinforcement in the non -cracked part of an RC structure and bare steel can act as effective 
cathodes if there is an electrical contact to the reinforcement at the cracks when these cathodic 
areas have su fficient oxygen supply. This is typically the case for cathodic areas located in the 
splash zone or exposed to air where the corrosion around cracks can be accelerated due to macro-
cell corrosion. However, under submerged conditions, the cathodic oxygen re duction reaction is 
limited by the transport of dissolved oxygen to the rebar surface (Hussain, 2011). It was found that 
the main effect of macro cracks in submerged concrete is a reduction of the initiation time. Once 
the active rebar corrosion was initiated, the corrosion rate started high, but rapidly decreased and 
approached the active corrosion rate typically found in uncracked concrete (Alekseev et al., 1993). 
\nPDF_PAGE 241\n 
 
201 
According to a study (Wilkins & Lawrence, 1980), the cross-sectional static cracks, even with up 
to 1 mm crack width, would not lead to corrosion at a significant level in the case of submerged 
concrete.  
The effect of cracks on the corrosion rate in cracked concrete could also depend on the crack width 
and concrete type. For high -performance concrete under wetting and drying conditions (splash 
zone), it has been observed that although the chloride threshol d level and the initiation time may 
be close to zero for crack widths up to 0.8 mm and a concrete cover of 30 mm, the active corrosion 
rate will become so small that it may be considered as practically harmless (Pettersson et al., 1996). 
In a study (Lu et al., 2017)  under wetting and drying cycles (ratio 1:1) , the effect of transverse 
cracks on chloride-induced steel corrosion was found to depend on both the cover depth and crack 
parameters such as width and frequency/spacing. The corrosion rate increas es more rapidly with 
increasing crack width and frequency and decreasing cover depth. Under various cover depth and 
crack frequency scenarios, it has been observed that there exists a certain critical crack width below 
which the effect of the crack is negl igible. However, the reasons for the existence of that critical 
width and its dependence on exposure conditions and the crack parameters are not clear. 
Two different mechanisms of corrosion, the micro-cell and the macro-cell have been identified in 
cracked concrete specimens (Subramaniam & Bi, 2010). The two mechanisms primarily differ in 
the length scales and the physical distance between the underlying electrochemical reactions. In 
the microcell mechanism, the anodic and cathodic reaction sites are micr oscopic and are located 
adjacent to each other. In macro-cell corrosion, the anode and cathode are spatially separated by a 
finite macroscopic distance resulting in a flow of electric charges over a physical distance. In the 
case of chloride-induced corrosion, the anodic reaction is localized, confined to the area of steel 
where the chloride threshold level for depassivation is exceeded. The cathodic reaction may 
significantly occur in all areas that are not permanently water -saturated and cathodic current 
density decreases as the distance from the crack increases (Raupach, 1996) . That explains the 
experimental observation that crack frequency could be identified as a governing factor in the rate 
of corrosion (Arya & Ofori-Darko, 1996), as it enables more passive areas near those cracks to be 
polarized to its cathodic direction and hence larger total cathodic current. Poursaee et al. (2010)  
studied corrosion of multi-rebar mortar samples subject to various types of de-icing salt solutions, 
where an acti ve rebar near the surface was subject to the solution and the other three were at a 
\nPDF_PAGE 242\n 
 
202 
deeper location. It was found the micro and macro corrosion rates were similar. However, another 
similar test (Hansson et al., 2006) suggested that macro-cell corrosion is the main mechanism of 
rebar corrosion in ordinary corrosion. Although these tests were not in cracked concrete, these 
findings could suggest that the contributions from micro-cell and macro-cell depend on the area of 
active rebar and passive rebar and th eir arrangement. The contribution of micro -cell corrosion 
could be the dominant mechanism when the active rebar area is comparable to the passive rebar 
and the distance between these two is large.  
In conclusion, most of the previous research on corrosion in cracked concrete has been limited to 
measuring either potentials or corrosion rates in either field structures (submerged or splashed 
zone) or preconditioned samples (often subjected to wetting/drying cycles) in the lab but often 
without monitoring of t he distribution around the crack of any of the environmental parameters 
that can affect the corrosion process, such as water saturation, oxygen, and chloride. Under 
different exposure conditions, the properties of cracks such as crack width, crack frequenc y, the 
properties of concrete, and cover depths would have very different effects on the corrosion rate. A 
clear understanding of the relative magnitudes of the microcell and macro -cell mechanisms in a 
corroding system and what creates the conditions for a ny of the two to become the dominant 
mechanism is still currently not available. The role of each mechanism, its contribution to the total 
corrosion rate and its dependence on the local micro-environment (moisture, oxygen, and chloride) 
created by the very existence of cracks are not clear yet. 
In this chapter, a comprehensive corrosion model is built by coupling the corrosion models 
developed in previous chapters with environmental models that simulate the evolution of moisture 
and chloride content on embedded rebars. The corrosion models employ the corrosion kinetics of 
the embedded rebar which are function of the micro-environment conditions at the rebar location 
such as moisture content and chloride content. Therefore, the comprehensive model can simulat e 
rebar corrosion rate from the exposure scenario that involves concrete cover cracking, non-uniform 
water distribution, and chloride contamination. An experimental program was conducted to 
validate this model. 
\nPDF_PAGE 243\n 
 
203 
8.3 Experimental program 
8.3.1 Material and sample preparation 
Concrete beams were made with Type GU cement with a sand/cement ratio of 1.65 and a water -
cement ratio of 0.7 and 0.5. The same batch was used to cast the cylinder samples when measuring 
the steel corrosion parameters (Chapter 4). The configuration of the cracked beam sample is shown 
in Figure 8.1a.  A No.10 rebar was cut into 30 mm-long segments and embedded in mortar. These 
rebar segments were electrically insulated from each other but were connected through wiring that 
extended out of the beam so that they could be electrically connected externally. As shown in 
Figure 8.1b, the rebar segments were machined with internal threads and nylon screws were used 
to connect them. This connection method allowed the segmented rebar to withstand some t ensile 
strength when the crack was created at the mid -span. The enamel coating and a GFRP washer 
spacer ensured the rebar bar segments were electrically isolated. Figure 8.1c shows a photo of the 
rebar. 
 
(a) 
 
 
(b) 
 
     
      
          
    
    
  
            
   
           
                   
                      
         
    
\nPDF_PAGE 244\n 
 
204 
Figure 8.1 Cracked reinforced concrete beam sample and rebar configuration (a) reinforced 
concrete beam dimensions (b) segmented rebar connection design (c) Photo of the segmented 
rebar. Dimension unit: mm. 
The beam samples and cylinder samples were cured at 25 ℃ and relative humidity greater than 90% 
for two months. The beam samples were then pre -cracked to introduce natural cracks at the mid -
span of the beams by a three-point bending test (Figure 8.2). The beam samples were then coated 
with epoxy on all sides except the surface with the open crack. This helps the formation of a 2 -D 
moisture and chloride penetration profile around the crack. The cracks were reopened to 0.3 mm 
and 1.0 mm after the epoxy coating using aluminum shims. A number of cylinders were cast with 
the same mixes used for the beams to monitor the penetration of moisture and chloride ion. The 
cylinders’ side surfaces were also coated with epoxy to facilitate approximate 1-D species transport. 
Figure 8.3 shows the finished beam samples and cylinder samples. 
 
Figure 8.2 Three-point loading to create vertical cracks.

\nPDF_PAGE 245\n

Figure 8.3 Epoxy-coated beam samples and cylinder samples. 
8.3.1.1 Sample exposure condition 
The beam samples and cylinder samples were subject to wetting and drying conditions. The 
samples were initially set as fully saturated by submersion in fresh water for two weeks and then 
dried for six days in the ambient environment of the Structures Laboratory. Starting at the second 
wetting event, samples were submerged in a 5% wt. NaCl salt solution. The first wetting event 
started on Day 7 for 24 hours. After a 13 -day drying period, the second wetting event started on 
Day 20 for 48 hours and following t hat was a 43-day drying period. The weights of the beam 
samples were measured at the end of each wetting session and periodically during each drying 
session. The water loss for each sample was recorded as the weight loss of the beam samples from 
their saturated condition. The water loss during the wetting and drying process is plotted in Figure 
8.4.

water/cement ratio, and crack width is the surface crack opening size. 
8.3.1.2 Current and potential measurement 
The corrosion condition of the segmented rebar was recorded in two modes: disconnec ted mode 
and connected mode. In the disconnected mode, as shown in Figure 8.5 and Figure 8.6, the open 
circuit potential and the polarization resistance of each disconnected rebar segment were measured. 
The potential is open circuit corrosion potential for the rebar segments in its sounding environment, 
and the polarization resistance can give information about the resting corrosion current density. 
In the connected mode, the rebar pieces were connected, and the ampere meter was installed across 
the whole rebar ends. The current flowing between the neighbouring rebar segments was measured 
by turning off the switch between those two neighbouring rebar segments so the current flows 
through the ampere meter. Figure 8.7 shows the measurement of the current flowing between the 
first and second rebar segments and the measured current was denoted as 𝐼1,2. The current between 
the second and third rebar segments was denoted as 𝐼2,3 and so forth.

8.4 Numerical corrosion model 
8.4.1 Geometry setup 
An 3 -D numerical model were implemented in a commercial FEM software, COMSOL 
Multiphysics. The model replicates the sample geometric configuration used in the experimental 
program described earlier. The crack line was implemented as a flat surface, as previous simulation 
studies have indicated that the roughness of the crack line has minimal influence on the water 
transport profile around the crack (Li, 2014) . Half of the beam ( Figure 8.9) was used for the 
simulation to take advantage of the symmetry and save computation time. 
  
Figure 8.9 3-D model geometry implementing half of the beam sample with the segmented 
rebar. 
 
8.4.2 Water flow and transport of species 
The non-uniform microenvironment on the rebar was simulated with the transport of moisture and 
chloride ion in the cracked concrete.  The boundary conditions for the exposed uncoated beam 
concrete surface and crack surface are aligned with the wetting and drying cycles and salt exposure 
used in the corresponding experiment.  

\nPDF_PAGE 250\n 
 
210 
8.4.2.1 Governing equations 
The governing equations used for the moisture and chloride transport are described in detail in 
Chapter 7 and restated here for convenience. 
8.4.2.2 Water flow in concrete 
In many practical conditions, a porous medium like concrete is not fully saturated. The degree of 
saturation depends on the surrounding environmental  moisture conditions. When concrete is not 
fully saturated, a negative capillary pressure develops and facilitates the absorption of water, and 
this pressure is the driving force of water flow within concrete. Under such conditions, the 
permeability becomes dependent on the water content. Darcy’s law has been extended to describe 
water flow for this kind of problem in unsaturated porous media. One of the earliest and most 
widely used models for water flow in unsaturated porous media is known as Richard’s equation,  
 𝐶 𝜕𝐻𝑝
𝜕𝑡 = 𝛻 ∙ [𝐾𝑠𝑘𝑟𝛻(𝐻𝑝 + 𝑍)] (8.4) 
where 𝐻𝑝 [m] is pressure head, 𝑍 [m] is elevation, 𝑡 [s] is time, 𝐾𝑠[m/s] is hydraulic conductivity 
of concrete at full saturation and 𝑘𝑟 is relative permeability, defined as the ratio between the 
hydraulic conductivity at volumetric water content 𝜃, 𝐾(𝜃 ≤ 𝜃𝑠) to 𝐾(𝜃 = 𝜃𝑠), where 𝜃𝑠[-] is 
saturated water content or the total connected porosity, 𝐶 [1/m] is defined as a change in storage 
due to change in pressure head, 
 𝐶 = 𝜕𝜃𝜕𝐻𝑝 (8.5) 
A constitutive relationship or model that has been shown to work with concrete is  the van 
Genuchten- Mualem model (Kumar, 2010; Li, 2014) , given by Equations (8.6 – 8.9) (Mualem, 
1976; van Genuchten, 1980):

8.4.2.4 Gas transport in concrete 
Because the bulk movement of gas in concrete is rare in practical scenarios, the advection and 
dispersion effects were neglected. The diffusion of oxygen or carbon diox ide were modelled as  
driven by the gas density/partial pressure in the gas phase.  
 
𝜕[𝜖𝑔〈𝜌O2,CO2〉𝑔]
𝜕𝑡 = ∇ ∙ [𝐷𝑒𝑓𝑓O2,CO2∇〈𝜌O2,CO2〉𝑔] + sinkO2,CO2 (8.16) 
\nPDF_PAGE 253\n

or CO2 in the gas phase, 𝐷𝑒𝑓𝑓O2,CO2 is effective diffusivity of O2 or CO2 in the gas phase, the sink 
term, sink𝑂2,𝐶𝑂2 represents the consumption rate for carbon dioxide or oxygen. The consumption 
rate of carbon dioxide is determined by the saturation level and pH in the pore solution and the 
consumption rate of oxygen is determined by the oxygen-reduction cathodic reaction of the rebar 
corrosion. 
8.4.2.5 Boundary conditions 
The two drying and wetting cycles for the uncoated concrete surfaces have the same setting as the 
experiment after the initial saturation. It started with a six-day drying session and a one-day wetting 
session and followed by a two -day wetting session and a 43 -day drying session. The wetting 
condition corresponds to water submergence, and the drying condition corresponds to a relative 
humidity of 25% at 25 ℃ . The relationship between hydraulic pressure head and RH and 
temperature can be derived from the Kelvin equation (8.17)  and Young-Laplace equation (8.18):

8.4.2.6 Calibration of transport parameters 
As described in Section 8.3.1.1, the mortar cylinder samples were prepared and underwent the 
same wetting and drying cycles as the beam samples. The cylinder samples were coated with epoxy 
except for the two ends, which approximates a 1-D transport problem. The cylinder weights were 
measured to record water content change over the wetting and drying cycles. A 1 -D model was 
used to simulate water and chloride ions transport of these cylinders. The measured water content 
changes and chloride content profiles were used to calibrate the transport parameters in the model. 
Figure 8.11 shows the averaged volumetric water content for cylinder samples an d simulated 
results from the calibrated models. The calibrated moisture transport parameters are shown in

8.4.3.2 Concentration polarization 
Concentration polarization is caused when the electrochemical reaction is significantly faster than 
the mass transport of these species. In reinforced concrete corrosion, the limited oxygen diffusion 
is often considered for the concentration polarization effect. The oxygen has a lower concentration 
𝑐𝑠 adjacent to the rebar surface. The oxygen reduction rate becomes slower, and the equilibrium 
potential becomes lower. Therefore, more overpotential (concentration overpotential) is needed to 
achieve the same  cathodic current normally driven at a bulk concentration 𝑐0 . A first-order 
dependence of concentration on the activation-controlled kinetics was assumed (Frankel, 2016): 
 𝑖𝑐𝑂2 = 𝑖𝑐𝑜𝑟𝑟 ∙ 𝑐𝑠𝑐0 [−10(𝐸−𝐸𝑐𝑜𝑟𝑟)/𝛽𝑐 ] (8.25) 

\nPDF_PAGE 260\n 
 
220 
It is worth noting that this equation corresponds to a concentration polarization overpotential 
component ηconc = βc log10

cs
. In the numerical model, c0 is solved by coupling the electrode 
kinetics and the mass transport of oxygen.

𝑖𝑛𝑒𝑡 = 𝑖𝑎𝐹𝑒 + 𝑖𝑐𝑂2 = 𝑖𝑐𝑜𝑟𝑟 ∙ 10(𝐸−𝐸𝑐𝑜𝑟𝑟)/𝛽𝑎 + 𝑖𝑐𝑜𝑟𝑟 ∙ 𝑐𝑠𝑐0 [−10(𝐸−𝐸𝑐𝑜𝑟𝑟)/𝛽𝑐 ]

and electrolyte domain.

8.5.1 Disconnected mode: open-circuit corrosion potential 
The open -circuit potential reflects the corrosion state of the rebar segment in the corrosive 
environment. The open circuit potential experiment measurements are compared with the 
simulated results. Four model setups were used to acquire the open circuit potential of each rebar 
segment from the numerical model. In each setup, the corrosion kinetics were applied to an 
individual rebar segment while the rest of the rebar surfaces remain ins ulated. The comparison 
between the simulated results and the test results are presented in Figure 8.17. 
 
 
Figure 8.17 Validation of open circuit potential in the disconnected mode. 
Rebar 4 segment near the crack responds sensitively to the drying and wetting cycle due to the 
rapid change of chloride and moisture near the crack. For beams with w/c of 0.7, rebar1-3 are not 
immediately affected by the first 1 -day wetting session but are quickly affected by the second 2 -
day wetting session when the chloride concentration reach ed the critical value at the rebar level. 
The crack size does not sig nificantly affect the potential evolution around the crack. However, 

\nPDF_PAGE 263\n 
 
223 
denser concrete slows down the penetration of chloride so that the rebar 1 -3 remains not affected 
for w/c of 0.5 concrete samples.

8.6 Conclusion 
A comprehensive corrosion model has been developed. It simulates the evolution of moisture and 
chloride content on embedded rebars in cracked concrete and uses homogenized corrosion kinetics 
as functions of the microenvironmental conditions on the embedded rebar to solve the potential 
and current field in the concrete domain and rebar surface. 
The corrosion model was validated by comparing simulated results with experimental results at 
various moisture and chloride contents for segmented rebar in cracked beams. The segmented 
beam sample was tested in disconnected mode and connected mode in wetting and drying cycles. 
The measured results, corrosion potential, corrosion current density in disconne cted mode, and 
rebar potential, total current in connected mode, were compared to a 3 -D model representing the 
same experimental setup.   

\nPDF_PAGE 266\n 
 
226 
The water-cement ratio had the greatest influence on the corrosion condition of the rebar in the 
cracked beams. The tested crack sizes (0.3 mm and 1.0 mm) had similar effects on increasing the 
non-uniform corrosion rate near the cracks. The simulated corrosion results were validated by 
experimental studies. The comprehensive corrosion model can predict the rebar corrosion  in the 
cracked beams subject to wetting and drying cycles with the contamination of chloride. 
8.7 Epilogue 
Chapter 8 focused on the formulation of a comprehensive corrosion model that incorporates crack 
configuration. This model simulates moisture and chloride content evolution on embedded rebars 
in cracked concrete, using homogenized corrosion kinetics. The model was validated with the 
corrosion test results from segmented rebars in cracked concrete subjected to wetting and drying 
cycles with chloride salt so lutions. This comprehensive corrosion model will contribute to our 
understanding and practical applications in assessing and designing reinforced concrete structures 
in a corrosive environment  such as marine environment, potash mine, bridge deck with the 
application of de-icing salts. In the next chapter, the validated model will be utilized to explore the 
mechanisms behind the observed variations of rebar corrosion in cracked concrete.  
 
 
 
 
 
\nPDF_PAGE 267\n 
 
227 
9 Numerical Investigation of Effects of Crack on Rebar Corrosion in Concrete 
9.1 Preface 
In this chapter, the validated model with a 2 -D implementation is utilized to explore the intricate 
relationship between cracks, microenvironmental factors, and rebar corrosion. The model allows 
for a comprehensive examination of key parameters such as moisture, chloride accumulation, 
oxygen availability, electrode potential, and corrosion current density of the rebar. The numerical 
exploration provides a mechanistic explanation of the observed rebar co rrosion behaviour in 
cracked concrete, with particular emphasis on corrosion reduction over time and the effect of crack 
size. This chapter provides a deeper understanding of the relationship between cracks, 
microenvironmental factors, and rebar corrosion,  which can provide a more accurate assessment 
of corrosion and select optimized corrosion mitigation strategies in practical applications. 
9.2 Summary of typical and peculiar observed behaviours (Lab and/or field) 
The variation in the effects of crack on the rebar in cracked concrete was reviewed in Chapter 8  
(Section 8.2) and is summarized as follows. Typically, macro-cell corrosion will accelerate the 
active corrosion rate at the crack where the protective film is damaged due to chloride ions. The 
active corrosion may start with a high rate, but it will decrease over time, resulting in a small and 
harmless rate for submerged concrete structures. However, in the partial ly saturated case, the 
corrosion rate also decreases with time and there is a peculiar behaviour where there exists a critical 
width below which corrosion is considered harmless. In the subsequent sections, we will examine 
how the cracks affect the microen vironment and corrosion of rebar in cracked concrete in 
submerged conditions and wetting and drying conditions so that it provides a mechanistic 
explanation of the observed rebar corrosion behaviour in cracked concrete.  
9.3 Numerical investigation of effects of crack on corrosion  
For computational efficiency, a 2 -D model implementation was used to explore the effect of 
cracking on the microenvironment of concrete in the cracked concrete. As shown in Figure 9.1, 
the geometric configuration was similar to the 3-D model used in the validation tests, but with a 
longer length of the concrete beam. The rebar was represented as a line in the concrete doma in.  
The governing equations for transport of water, chloride ion, and oxygen in the concrete domain 
\nPDF_PAGE 268\n 
 
228 
can be found in Section 8.4.2. The governing equations for the electrochemical process can be 
found in Section 8.4.3. 
 
Figure 9.1 Geometric configuration of the 2-D corrosion model of the cracked beam. 
The numerical investigation  will explore, under two types of exposure conditions, how the 
evolution of the microenvironment affects the corrosion of steel reinforcement in cracked concrete. 
The focus will be placed  on the key factors such as moisture, chloride accumulation, oxygen 
availability and the resultant electrode potential, and corrosion current density of the rebar . The 
investigation will focus on two types of cracks: a crack with a crack width of 1 mm on the surface, 
and a very fine crack size of 0.1 mm on the surface. The crack widths decrease linearly along the 
depth of the crack with the crack width of zero at the crack tip.  The hydraulic conductivity along 
the crack line i s depended on the crack width, defined by the cubic law , Equation (8.11). The 
chloride diffusivity along the crack line equals the chloride diffusivity in the aqueous  solution 
during the wetting session and matches that in concrete during the drying session. The oxygen 
diffusivity along the crack equals the oxygen diffusivity in air during the drying session and is 
equivalent to the oxygen diffusivity in concrete durin g the wetting session . The boundary 
conditions for wetting/submerged condition and drying condition are summarized in Figure 9.2. 

\nPDF_PAGE 269\n 
 
229 
 
Figure 9.2 Model boundary condition settings for the investigated exposure conditions: 
submerged condition and wetting and drying cycles. 
 
9.3.1 Submerged condition 
9.3.1.1 Submerged condition: 1 mm crack 
As shown in Figure 9.3, under the submerged condition, concrete remains saturated. At the early 
stage (Day 30), chloride ions diffuse to the rebar surface through the crack, which causes localized 
depassivation of the rebar in the cracked zone. The corrosion kinetics on the reb ar are mainly 
influenced by the chloride content. As shown in Figure 9.4, the equilibrium potential on the rebar 
is affected by the chloride content and water content  (Figure 8.13), and hence, has very different 
negative value at the crack due to high chloride content. The actual electrode potential on the rebar 
is determined by the electrochemical reaction (Section 8.4.3). The rebar has an electrode potential 
(-0.42 – -0.56 V) with the most negative potential at the crack. This potential va riation is small 
due to the low resistivity of the water -saturated concrete. The rebar near the crack has a positive 
overpotential (electrode potential > equilibrium potential) and the net electrolyte current flows 
from the cracked area to the uncracked area of the rebar, which confirms that most anodic reaction 
(iron dissolution) occurs at the crack. The most cathodic reaction occurs on the rebar not at the 

\nPDF_PAGE 270\n 
 
230 
crack but in the vicinity of the crack with lower chloride content, which is indicated by the reduced 
oxygen saturation level in that area. 
 
Figure 9.3 Water saturation, chloride content, electrode potential and oxygen saturation in 
the cracked concrete under the submerged condition at the early stage (day 30) and 
developed stage (day 180). 
With the continuing chloride ingress and corrosion, chloride content will increase along the whole 
rebar, as shown in Figure 9.3 at Day 180 (called here the developed stage). The difference in the 
degree of depassivation and equil ibrium potential at the crack and away from the rebar becomes 
less significant, but still, the electrolyte current flows from the cracked area to the uncracked area 
of the bar meaning the rebar at the crack is more anodic. The oxygen is almost depleted on the 
rebar surface, which limits the cathodic reaction (oxygen reduction) and leads to concentration 
polarization with a very negative potential (-1.15 – -1.17 V). The electrode potential on the rebar 
becomes more uniform. 

\nPDF_PAGE 271\n 
 
231 
 
Figure 9.4 Equilibrium potential of the rebar determined by microenvironment and 
electrode potential of the rebar determined by the electrochemical reaction; the 
overpotential = electrode potential – equilibrium potential. 
 
9.3.1.1.1 Corrosion current 
From the early stage to the developed stage, the intensity, and the distribution of corrosion current 
density (anodic current density) on the rebar change over time. As shown in Figure 9.5, the 
corrosion current density at the crack decreased significantly over time. Corrosion becomes less 
localized, and the current density spread out to a larger corrosion area with a value comparable to 
the corrosion of a passivated rebar. As shown in Figure 9.6, the line -averaged rebar corrosion 
current density in the cracked zone increase s to a peak value and then decrease s over time. The 
cracked zone is defined as 1 0 cm across the crack, the typical sampling size of the rebar by the 
experimental measurement (linear polarization resistance or LPR). The total anodic current density 
on the rebar in the cracked zone has two contributions: the micro -cell component which h as the 
corresponding cathodic current from the rebar in the cracked zone and the macro -cell component 
which has the corresponding cathodic current from the rebar outside of the cracked zone. As the 
chloride penetration and oxygen consumption proceed, the m acro-cell component decreases to a 
similar weight as the micro-cell component, which is in agreement with the spread -out corrosion 

\nPDF_PAGE 272\n 
 
232 
current distribution along the whole rebar.  The averaged corrosion current in the cracking zone 
decreases with time due to limited oxygen supply despite an increase in chloride content. 
 
Figure 9.5 Corrosion current density distribution along the rebar at different times.  
 
Figure 9.6 Averaged corrosion current in the cracking zone at different times for the 
submerged condition with 1 mm crack. 
 

\nPDF_PAGE 273\n 
 
233 
9.3.1.2 Submerged condition: 0.1 mm crack 
As shown in Figure 9.7, for a much finer crack (0.1 mm), the diffusion of chloride ions through 
the crack is reduced. The penetration rate of the chloride ions through the crack relative to their 
penetration through uncracked concrete is significantly decreased in this case.  
 
Figure 9.7 Water saturation, chloride content, electrode potential and oxygen saturation in 
the cracked concrete under the submerged condition at the early stage (Day 30) and 
developed stage (Day 180). Case:  0.1 mm crack. 
As a result, compared to the 1 -mm crack case, at the early stage (Day 30), the localized high 
corrosion current density of the 0.1-mm case is reduced because the chloride conte nt at the crack 
is only slightly higher than that on the rest of the rebar and the rebar at the crack is less anodically 
polarized (Figure 9.8).  
 

\nPDF_PAGE 274\n 
 
234 
 
Figure 9.8 Equilibrium potential of the rebar determined by microenvironment and 
electrode potential of the rebar determined by the elect rochemical reaction; the 
overpotential = electrode potential – equilibrium potential. Case: 0.1 mm crack. 
For the same reason, the peak corrosion current density (0.5 𝐴/𝑚2 ) is also reduced by 
approximately 50%. The overall reduced corrosion also means a reduced cathodic reaction. 
Therefore, the depletion of oxygen is also delayed, which leads to a slower reduction of corrosion 
current density over time at the crack, as shown in Figure 9.9 but the corrosion current density 
finally levels off and is reduced to a similar value as the one seen in the 1 mm crack case. In 
addition, Figure 9.10 shows that for this finer crack case, the micro -cell component has a much 
higher weight of contribution to the total corrosion current density in the cracked zone. The micro-
cell corrosion starts becoming dominant after the corrosion current density peaks at Day 60.  
 

\nPDF_PAGE 275\n 
 
235 
 
Figure 9.9 Corrosion current density distribution along the rebar at different times.  Case: 
0.1 mm crack.  
 
Figure 9.10 Averaged corrosion current in the cracking zone decreases with time due to 
limited oxygen and more exposure to chloride. Case: 0.1 mm crack. 
In summary, the corrosion model captures the field observation of the dependence of crack size 
and corrosion rate reduction over time. For the submerged condition, cracks affect the corrosion 
by affecting the chloride diffusion rate through the crack. Crac ked concrete with a smaller crack 
size has reduced peak corrosion current and slower reduction of corrosion rate over time, an overall 
more uniform corrosion with more micro -cell corrosion. Eventually, the corrosion will reduce to 

\nPDF_PAGE 276\n 
 
236 
a similar value when the whole rebar is contaminated with chloride and the corrosion rate is 
controlled by the limiting diffusion of oxygen. 
9.3.2 Wetting and drying condition 
When cracked reinforced concrete is exposed to the wetting and drying scenario with a chloride 
source during the wetting cycles, the corrosion kinetics of the rebar is influenced by both water 
content and chloride content. The change in water content will significantly affect chloride 
transport and oxygen diffusion. The chloride can penetrate concrete from the crac k via advection 
in the wetting cycle after a drying cycle. The oxygen depletion could be less of a concern for 
mostly dry conditions (caused by higher oxygen diffusivity in drier concrete). We will look at 
scenarios with two crack sizes while tracking the availability of oxygen.  
9.3.2.1 Wetting cycles: 1 mm crack 
The weekly wetting and drying cycles were set up as one day of wetting and six days of drying. 
The solution for Day 28.5 and Day 175.5 (half-day into the wetting cycle) are selected to represent 
the early stage and developed stage of corrosion.  
As shown in  Figure 9.11, in the early stage, rapid chloride penetration occurs at the crack via 
advection in the wetting cycle. The rebar at the crack is anodically polarized, exerting net anodic 
current. The electrolyte current flows out from the rebar at the crack. Similar to the submersion 
case, the most cathodic reaction occurs on the rebar not at but near the crack. The oxy gen has a 
higher saturation level on the rebar than the submersion case with a 1 mm crack. This is because 
oxygen is replenished partially during the drying cycle where the dried crack and dried concrete 
near the crack have a much higher oxygen diffusivity. In the developed stage, the development of 
the chloride profile shows slower continuing chloride penetration due to no flux in the drying 
period and reduced diffusivity of chloride in drier concrete. In the drying cycle, the chloride 
continues to diffuse into deeper concrete while there is no supply on the concrete surface. 
\nPDF_PAGE 277\n 
 
237 
 
Figure 9.11 Water saturation, chloride content, electrode potential and oxygen saturation in 
the cracked concrete under the wetting cycles at the early stage (day 28.5) and developed 
stage (day 175.5). 
As shown in Figure 9.12, the equilibrium potential difference between the rebar at the crack and 
rebar away from the crack is reduced, which results in a reduced anodic polarization of the rebar 
at the crack, which led to a reduced anodic corrosion current density at the rebar. The accumulated 
oxygen consumption on the rebar has caused oxygen to deplete on the rebar away from the crack. 
The oxygen-limited cathodic reaction also contributes to the reduction of the anodic reaction at the 
crack. Therefore, the corrosion current at the crack decreases over time ( Figure 9.13). It is worth 
mentioning that the oxygen at the crack still has a higher satu ration level because the rebar at the 
crack is anodically polarized, and the oxygen reduction reaction rate is suppressed to a low level. 
The additional oxygen supply through the crack is not sufficient to replenish the oxygen consumed 
away from the crack. 

\nPDF_PAGE 278\n 
 
238 
 
Figure 9.12 Equilibrium potential of the rebar determined by microenvironment and 
electrode potential of the rebar determined by the electrochemical reaction; the 
overpotential = electrode potential – equilibrium potential. Case: 1 mm crack, wetting cycles. 
 
 
Figure 9.13 Corrosion current density distribution along the rebar at different times.  Case: 
1 mm crack, wetting cycles.  
 

\nPDF_PAGE 279\n

9.3.2.2 Drying cycles: 1 mm crack 
The solution for Day 35 and Day 182 (the end of the drying cycle)  are selected to represent the 
early stage and developed stage of corrosion. As shown in Figure 9.14, at the end of each drying 
cycles the water content near the crack is low, and the chloride content profile near the crack is 
close to 2D diffusion. The chloride content on the concrete surface and crack surface is slightly 
less than the case in the wetting cycles due to no flux boundary condition on these surfaces during 
the drying cycles. Rebar at the crack becomes less anodic when exposed to low water content and 
high chloride content. The rebar in the cracked zone could have more positive equilibrium potential 
than the rest of the rebar so that the net effect of rebar at the crack is providing cathodic current.  
 
Figure 9.14 Water saturation, chloride content, electrode potential and oxygen saturation in 
the cracked concrete under the drying cycles at the early stage (day 32) and developed stage 
(day 182). 
 

\nPDF_PAGE 280\n 
 
240 
This is the case in the developed stage at Day 182 ( Figure 9.15), where chloride content on the 
rebar away from the crack increases to an extent, the rebar away from the crack can be more anodic 
than the rebar at the crack. The most anodic area is found around 15 cm away from the rebar, and 
the electrolyte current flo ws from that spot to the rebar at the crack and the rebar far away from 
the crack. 
Because of the low water content near the crack in drying cycles, the corrosion current density is 
low at the crack and the maximum corrosion current density is found immediately near the crack 
within 5 cm ( Figure 9.16). The corrosion current density distribution spread out to a larger area 
away from the crack with a significant reduction in the intensity of the corrosion current density. 
 
Figure 9.15 Equilibrium potential of the rebar determined by microenvironment and

9.3.2.3 Corrosion current change over the wetting and drying cycles: 1 mm crack 
As shown in Figure 9.17, the line-averaged corrosion current density in the cracked zone fluctuates 
intensively with the wetting and drying events. For consecutive wetting and drying cycles, it peaks 
during the wetting cycle and decreases in the drying cycle. Over a number of wetting and drying 
cycles, the corrosion current density in the cracked zone peaks at the early stage and then decreases 
over time to a smaller steady rate. The anodic current density is domina ted by the macro -cell 
mechanism in the early stage and in the developed stage, the micro -cell anodic current surpasses 
the macro-cell counterpart and becomes the controlling mechanism. It is worth noticing that in the 
drying cycles in the developed stage, the micro-cell cell is dominant because the rebar exposed to 
dried chloride-contaminated concrete at the crack effectively acts as a cathode. 
The maximum corrosion current density of the whole rebar is shown in Figure 9.18. The maximum 
corrosion rate is located at the crack during the wetting cycles, in the cracking zone in the early 
stages, and in the uncracked zone in the developed stage. The peak values in the wetting cycles at 
the crack keep decreasing from the beginning.
