9.3.2.6 Corrosion current change over the wetting and drying cycles: 0.1 mm crack 
The line-averaged corrosion current density in the cracked zone  through the wetting and drying 
events is plotted in Figure 9.25. The maximum corrosion current density of the whole rebar is 
shown in Figure 9.26. 
 
Figure 9.25 Averaged corrosion current in the cracking zone decreases in the drying cycle 
and decreases overtime due to limited oxygen and more exposure to chloride. Case: 0.1 mm 
crack, wetting and drying cycles. 
The case of a 0.1 mm crack exhibits an almost identical solution  to the 1 mm crack case . The 
reason behind this is that unlike in submerged conditions where a smaller crack size reduces the 
progress of chloride diffusion through the crack, in wetting and dryin g cycles, the crack opening 
size does not have a significant effect on the chloride profile around the crack, even with the same 
crack length. The rapid penetration of chloride is primarily attributed to the wicking effect of the 
dried concrete surrounding the crack. 
 

\nPDF_PAGE 288\n 
 
248 
 
Figure 9.26 Maximum corrosion current on the rebar fluctuates in the wetting and drying 
cycles and decreases with time due to limited oxygen and more exposure to chloride . Case: 
0.1 mm crack, wetting and drying cycles. 
In summary, the model suggests that the corrosion kinetics of the rebar in the cracking zone are 
affected by chloride and moisture, with the possibility of becoming a cathode in drying cycles. The 
average corrosion rate of the reb ar in the cracking zone peaks during the wetting cycle and 
decreases in the following drying cycle, and peak values decrease over the repeated wetting and 
drying cycles. The final reduced corrosion rate is controlled by the chloride-contaminated area on 
the rebar and the reduced oxygen concentration on the rebar. The micro-cell corrosion eventually 
becomes the dominant contributor to the rebar corrosion in the cracking zone. 
For the wetting and drying scenario, the chloride profile is not significantly affe cted by the crack 
opening size in the wetting and drying cycles which is different from the submerged case. As a 
result, the model at this stage,  does not capture the field observation where smaller cracks are less 
harmful.  
9.3.3 Crack healing and critical crack width 
A substantial amount of laboratory and field experiments have shown that the width of surface 
cracks in reinforced concrete plays a crucial role in determining the corrosion rate of the steel 
reinforcement. Standard codes in many countries have adopted a t hreshold crack width to reflect 

\nPDF_PAGE 289\n 
 
249 
experimental observations that cracks below a certain width do not significantly affect the 
corrosion rate of steel reinforcement in concrete. This threshold crack width typically ranges from 
0.1 mm to 0.2 mm. Although a num ber of possible mechanisms (effect of crack width on the 
ingress of aggressive agents, self -healing, etc.) have been suggested , mostly by speculation , as 
possible reasons for the difference in corrosion behaviour between thin cracks and thick cracks, 
there is no clear understanding about which parameters are actually affecting the difference in 
corrosion behaviour between thin cracks and wide cracks. Hence, there is a need to shed more light 
on the intricate relationship between crack characteristics and co rrosion behaviour, a compelling 
yet complex aspect of reinforced concrete durability that remains poorly understood. 
The current model assumes that the cracks remain open indefinitely without any healing 
mechanism, which may not be a realistic representati on of the long -term behaviour of concrete 
structures. In reality, concrete has the ability to heal thin cracks over time through a process known 
as autogenous healing. This process occurs when the moisture in the environment reacts with the 
unhydrated cement particles in the concrete, leading to the formation of calcium -silicate hydrate 
(C-S-H) that can fill in small cracks and pores. In the next section, the crack healing mechanism 
will be added to the current model to investigate its effect on corrosion in cracked concrete. 
Therefore, to more accurately model the long -term effects of cracks in concrete structures, the 
model should incorporate the concept of crack healing. The crack can be assumed to heal only up 
to a maximum crack opening. Since the crack opening changes with depth, fractures with wide 
surface crack openings would seal the crack only up the height corresponding to this max “healable” 
opening. This concept allows a unified treatment of wide cracks and thin ones. After healing the 
cracks woul d remain at their new reduced depth and have, essentially the effect of a reduced 
concrete cover at their locations. 
The maximum healable crack size in concrete could be up to 0.2 mm in width in freshwater 
environments under atmospheric conditions, where c alcite precipitation is identified as the main 
cause of autogenous self -healing. However, the efficiency of healing may vary depending on 
several factors such as crack width, roughness, tortuosity, the presence of liquid water, water 
pressure, water chemis try, temperature, aggregate size, gradation curve, water -cement ratio, 
cement content, and exposure conditions (Lahmann et al., 2022).  
\nPDF_PAGE 290\n 
 
250 
To investigate the crack healing effect on corrosion in the existing corrosion model, the healable 
crack width was set as 0.08 mm and it heals linearly with time up till Day 60. The healing effect 
was expressed as the reduction of the transport properties of water, chloride ions and oxygen gas. 
When the crack is completely healed, its transport parameters on the crack line become negligible. 
Figure 9.27 shows a surface plot of the reduction function with the reduction to 10−15 . 
 
Figure 9.27 Linear reduction of transport properties such as hydraulic conductivity and 
chloride and oxygen diffusivity as a function of time when crack width with a healable crack 
width of 0.08 mm. 
 
For the model with a 0.1 mm crack surface opening, the crack width decreases linearly to zero to 
the tip of the crack. As shown in Figure 9.28 and Figure 9.29, the hydraulic conductivity and 
chloride and oxygen diffusivities reduce over time for the crack size less than 0.08 mm, so 
effectively the rebar regained some concrete cover at the crack (the crack width at the rebar depth 
is less than 0.08 mm, the healable crack width).  

\nPDF_PAGE 291\n 
 
251 
 
Figure 9.28 The hydraulic conductivity along the crack line changes due to crack healing. 
 
Figure 9.29 The diffusivity of chloride ions and oxygen gas along the crack line changes due 
to crack healing. 
This crack-healing implementation accelerates the corrosion rate reduction over the wetting and 
drying cycles ( Figure 9.31). The effect is brought by the reduced chloride content gra dient, and 
the reduced oxygen saturation level in the cracking zone. Because the healing is controlled by the 
crack width, finer cracks will have more healing than bigger cracks resulting in more recovered 
concrete cover for the rebar at the crack. For the  investigated simulation, the crack has a surface 

\nPDF_PAGE 292\n 
 
252 
opening of 0.1 mm. The crack completely heals at Day 60 for a crack width less than the maximum 
healable width (0.8 mm). In addition, a less permeable high -performance concrete (HPC) was 
implemented. It has reduced hydraulic conductivity, 𝐾𝑠 and chloride diffusivity, 𝐷𝑠 of the control 
concrete by a factor of 0.2. Low oxygen diffusivity, 𝐷𝑒𝑓𝑓𝑂2  refers to the diffusivity that caused 
oxygen to be depleted eventually on the rebar when there is no crack healing effect and high 𝐷𝑒𝑓𝑓𝑂2  
is scaled up by ten.  
For less permeable concrete, the effect of the crack healing on reducing corrosion would be more 
pronounced so the finer cracks become less harmful. As shown in Figure 9.30, the initial high 
concentration of chloride at the crack dissipates over time with a diluted chloride content at the 
crack and this reduction is more effective for the less permeable HPC with a smaller accumulation 
of chloride content at the crack. The rebar at the crack will become less anodic or even enter the 
passive zone with the restoration of the passive film. This prediction aligns with the field 
observation that for less permeable high-performance concrete, the allowable crack width is larger 
concerning corrosion damage. 
 
Figure 9.30 The evolution of chloride content profile (wt.% Cl by weight of cement) around 
the crack (0.1 mm surface opening) after complete crack healing (Day 60) with a width up to 
0.08 mm and crack width greater than 0.08 mm remains unhealed.  Left: high performance 
concrete (HPC), Right: control concrete. 
The averaged corrosion current density in the cracking zone is p lotted in Figure 9.31. After the 
complete crack healing at Day 60, there is a major corrosion reduction in the cracking zone for 

\nPDF_PAGE 293\n 
 
253 
both types of concrete. The reduced corrosion current density is similar to that in the drying cycle 
due to healed crack shielding the sudden water suction and the companion chloride ion advection 
in the wetting cycles. The corrosion current density continues to steadily decrease and levels off 
to a stable low corrosion current density.  
For the control concrete, chloride content increased significantly at the crack and overall on the 
entire rebar. The rebar at the crack has a similar exposure compared to the rest of the rebar. The 
rebar corrosion in the cracking zone has a major contribution in the form of micro-cell corrosion.   
The stabilized low corrosion rate depends on the availability of oxygen. As shown in Figure 9.31, 
low oxygen diffusivity has led to a much lower corrosion current density corresponding to oxygen 
depletion on the rebar surface.   
 
Figure 9.31 The effects of c rack healing on the reduction of corrosion rate in the cracking 
zone. The surface crack width is 0.1 mm; the salt solution in the wetting condition is 3.0 wt.% 
NaCl. HPC (high  performance concrete) has lower hydraulic conductivity and chloride 
diffusivity by a reduction factor of 0.2 to the control concrete. Low oxygen diffusivity 𝑫𝒆𝒇𝒇𝑶𝟐  
refers to the diffusivity that could have caused oxygen to be depleted eventually on the rebar 
if there is no crack healing effect.  
For the HPC, this stable corrosion rate is not significantly affected by oxygen diffusivity. The 
corrosion rate is so small that the corresponding oxygen reduction reaction will not cause a 
significant decrease in oxygen content on the rebar. The corrosion current is low due to the 
dissipated high chloride from the cracking zone. 

\nPDF_PAGE 294\n 
 
254 
9.3.4 Critical crack width 
The “critical crack width” or “threshold crack width” is described as a maximum allowable surface 
crack opening, below which the crack is considered harmless. The numerical investigation has 
provided a deeper mechanistic understanding on the intricate relationship between crack healing, 
exposure conditions, and corrosion behaviour . Building upon the insights gained from this 
numerical study, this critical crack width for reinforced concrete in a corrosive environment can 
be sought in the following way: 
First, the corresponding crack width at the rebar depth should be less than the maximum healable 
crack width so that the crack healing process can recover some of the concrete cover at the crack. 
This value can be determined by the crack geometry and the thickn ess of the concrete cover, and 
it is not conservative. 
Second, with the recovered concrete cover over the crack, it should bring a significant reduction 
in the corrosion current density, potentially to a negligible value. However, this depends on the 
existing corrosive microenvironment around the crack at the time of significant crack healing, 
continuous exposure conditions in the service condition, concrete properties, and corrosion 
kinetics of the embedded metal. The evaluation of this complex corros ion activity on the rebar is 
necessary to determine this value, which can be done using the proposed comprehensive corrosion 
model. 
9.4 Conclusion 
The comprehensive corrosion model that was presented and validated in chapter 8 was used in this 
chapter to assess the impact of cracks on corrosion.  A 2-D model was used to examine how the 
cracks affect the microenvironment and corrosion of rebar in cracked concrete in submerged 
conditions and wetting and drying conditions. It examines the key microenvironment factors such 
as moisture, chloride accumulation, oxygen availability and the resultant electrode potential, and 
corrosion current density of the rebar and provided a mechanistic explanation of the observed rebar 
corrosion behaviour in cracked concrete: corrosion reduction over time and the crack size effect. 
\nPDF_PAGE 295\n 
 
255 
9.4.1 Submerged condition 
In the submerged condition, cracks affect corrosion by affecting the chloride diffusion rate through 
the crack. A thinner crack results in a reduced diffusion influx of chloride ions through the crack. 
Concrete that is cracked with a smaller crack size has a reduced peak corrosion current and slower 
reduction of the corrosion rate over time, resulting in overall more uniform corrosion with more 
micro-cell corrosion. Eventually, the corrosion will reduce to a similar value when the entire rebar 
is contaminated with chloride, and the corrosion rate is controlled by the limiting diffusion of 
oxygen. 
9.4.2 Wetting and drying condition 
9.4.2.1 Corrosion reduction over a drying cycle and wetting and drying cycles 
For wetting and drying conditions, the model suggests that the corrosion kinetics of the rebar in 
the cracking zone are affected by chloride and moisture, with the possibility of becoming a cathode 
in drying cycles. The average corrosion rate of the rebar in the cracking zone peaks during the 
wetting cycle and decreases in the following drying cycle, and peak values decrea se over the 
repeated wetting and drying cycles. The final reduced corrosion rate is determined by the chloride-
contaminated area on the rebar and the reduced oxygen concentration on the rebar. Micro-cell 
corrosion eventually becomes the dominant contributor to the rebar corrosion in the cracking zone. 
9.4.2.2 Crack size and crack healing 
For the wetting and drying scenario, unlike the submerged case, the model shows that the crack 
opening size does not significantly affect the chloride influx through the crack. To capture the 
effect of crack size on corrosion, the model needed to incorporate the crack healing mechanism, 
which is a more realistic representation of the long-term behaviour of concrete structures. 
This crack healing implementation allowed the crack to heal when the crack opening was less than 
the critical “healable size”, and the healable portion of the crack had decreasd transport properties 
over time. The crack healing can slow  down future chloride transport and potentially allow high 
chloride content to dilute near the crack and limit oxygen diffusion through the crack. The less 
permeable concrete has a more significant chloride content reduction due to crack healing. The 
\nPDF_PAGE 296\n 
 
256 
crack healing implementation accelerated the corrosion rate reduction over the wetting and drying 
cycles. For a given critical healable crack size, a finer crack has a more healable crack length than 
a wider crack resulting in more recovered concrete cover for the rebar at the crack. 
For the same crack geometry, the effect of crack healing on the reduction of corrosion also depends 
on other factors such as the permeability of concrete. For the control concrete, t he stabilized low 
corrosion rate is dependent on the availability of oxygen. However, for HPC, the stable corrosion 
rate is not significantly affected by oxygen diffusivity , but mainly due to the dissipation of high 
chloride concentration from the cracking zone. 
9.4.2.3 Implication on the “critical crack width” – the maximum allowable surface crack 
opening, below which the crack is considered harmless in a corrosive environment.  
The critical crack width should be small enough such that the corresponding crack width at the 
rebar depth is greater than the maximum healable crack width. Therefore, the crack healing process 
can recover some of the concrete cover at the crack. This value is determined by the crack geometry 
and the thickness of the concrete cover.  
The critical crack width should be even smaller so that with the recovered concrete cover over the 
crack, it should bring a signif icant reduction in the corrosion current density, potentially to a 
negligible value. The resultant corrosion rate depends on many factors such as the existing 
corrosive microenvironment around the crack at the time of significant crack healing, continuous 
exposure conditions in the service condition, concrete properties, and corrosion kinetics of the 
embedded metal. This is the reason the “critical crack width” value has a large variation for 
different cover thicknesses, different concrete types, and differ ent exposure conditions. The 
evaluation of this complex corrosion activity on the rebar is necessary to determine this value, 
which can be done using the proposed comprehensive corrosion model. 
9.5 Epilogue 
The comprehensive corrosion model provides a mechanis tic understanding of the complex 
behaviour of rebar corrosion in cracked reinforced concrete structures. By considering factors such 
as crack width, healing, and microenvironment -dependent corrosion kinetics, the model enables 
the assessment of corrosion r ate in cracked and uncracked RC structures in realistic service 
\nPDF_PAGE 297\n 
 
257 
environments. In addition, the determination of the critical crack width for specific reinforced 
concrete configurations and their service environments becomes possible. 
 
\nPDF_PAGE 298\n 
258 
10 Conclusions and Recommendations  
10.1 Summary of work 
This research work aim ed to develop a comprehensive mechanistic corrosion model for rebar 
corrosion in non -uniform microenvironments caused by cracking and exposure conditions in 
reinforced concrete structures. The developed model is a versatile tool to perform in -depth 
corrosion assessment for RC structure s and service life prediction.  It allows preventive action to 
take place and avoids unnecessary major expensive repairs, and hence, a reduced life cycle cost. 
It would also help policymakers to regulate the evaluation criteria and cho ose optimum 
rehabilitation strategies for RC structures exceeding their design service life that do not necessarily 
need to be replaced. 
Both experimental work and numerical modelling work have been conducted to overcome the 
challenges that current numerical models face in describing the corrosion cell of rebars in concrete 
in the presence of spatial variations of the microenvironment due to cracking and service exposure 
conditions. This research work was organized into nine chapters that establish ed a logical 
progression in addressing specific objectives and connecting the research topics. 
A thorough literature review was conducted on three topics of corrosion of cracked reinforced 
concrete: 1) the common and contradictive experimental and field observation s of non-uniform 
corrosion in cracked concrete, 2) f undamentals on corrosion modelling and various numerical 
treatments of the corrosion cell,  and 3) fundamentals on mass transfer models describing the 
evolution of corrosive microenvironment in concrete affected by various service exposure 
conditions. 
The phase one experimental study focused on characterizing the corrosion parameters of rebar in 
simulated pore solutions and mortar using an improved test method and investigating the 
synergistic effects of chloride, carbonation, and water on rebar corrosion. The phase two 
experimental program performed a corrosion test of the segmented rebars in cracked concrete 
under wetting and drying cycles with chloride salt solutions. The measured potential and current 
distributions on these rebar segments were used to validate the developed corrosion model. 
\nPDF_PAGE 299\n 
259 
In the modelling part, a circuit model was developed to simulate the polarization behaviour of 
rebar with non -uniform conditions. This model was used to explore the relationship between 
overall corrosion parameters and non-uniform corrosion parameters at multilevel scales. At the 
pore level, a model was developed to simulate the non -uniform allocation of active and passive 
rebar-solution interface in a representative elementary volume based on non -uniform moisture 
distribution and free chloride ion distribution at a pore level.  
The environment model was developed to simulate the microenvironment evolution in concrete 
by simulating the transport of water, chloride ions, oxygen, and carbon dioxide gases in concrete. 
This model was applied to an old concrete arch bridge under various service conditions, including 
chloride sources, carbonation, moisture fluctuation, and exposure to different environmental 
factors. The predictions of the model align ed well with the current con dition of the bridge, and 
simulations for future climate scenarios provided insights into the implications of climate change 
on rebar section loss. 
A comprehensive corrosion model was then developed by integrating crack configuration into the 
previously developed environment model to simulate the moisture and chloride content evolution 
on embedded rebars in cracked concrete. It employ ed homogenized corrosion kinetics dependent 
on the microenvironmental conditions to solve the potential and current fields on both the concrete 
domain and rebar surface. With the validated model, a numerical investigation was performed to 
explore the mechanisms of how the cracks affect the microenvironment and the corrosion 
behaviour in the cracked concrete, which provided a mechanistic explanation of the observed rebar 
corrosion behaviour in cracked concrete, including corrosion reduction over time and the influence 
of crack size and crack healing. 
10.2 Conclusions 
The project addressed the synergistic effects of chloride, moisture, and carbonation on corrosion 
parameters, explored the relationship between overall and non-uniform corrosion parameters, and 
developed a mechanistic corrosion model that considers the impact of cracking and exposure 
conditions. The findings contribute to a better understanding of rebar corrosion in complex 
\nPDF_PAGE 300\n 
260 
microenvironments, offering insights into the design and maintenance of reinforced concrete 
structures. The detailed findings regarding the specific sub-objectives are as follows: 
10.2.1 The synergistic effect of chloride, moisture, and carbonation on the corrosion 
parameters of rebar in concrete. 
10.2.1.1 Test methods 
Accurate quantification of corrosion parameters is a vital prerequisite to corrosion modelling. An 
improved three-electrode potentiodynamic test procedure was developed to measure the corrosion 
parameters of as-received rebar in simulated pore solutions and mortar. It was found that the use 
of a Faraday shield and floating ground significantly reduced the large electrical potential 
oscillations caused by real -time IR compensation. The air -dried rebar-embedded mortar samples 
should be tested in a non -submerged setting to avoid the open circuit potential drifting. It was 
necessary to perform separate anodic and cat hodic scans to minimize the alteration of the rebar 
surface. 
10.2.1.2 Corrosion parameters in simulated pore solution (SPS) and carbonated simulated 
pore solution (CSPS) with various chloride concentrations, the significance of 
depassivation time 
The continuous pro cesses of depassivation and rust build -up were found to have an impact on 
corrosion parameters during the observed periods (short term: 36 hours, 72 hours, and long term: 
20 days). Over the 20 days of conditioning of rebar in SPS and CSPS with various chlo ride 
concentrations, depassivation slightly decreased the corrosion potential ( 𝐸𝑐𝑜𝑟𝑟) for both SPS and 
CSPS, while significantly increasing the corrosion current density ( 𝑖𝑐𝑜𝑟𝑟) for rebar in CSPS. A 
chloride concentration was identified (2% wt.% for SPS and 1% wt.% for CSPS), beyond which 
higher chloride concentrations had no significant effect on 𝐸𝑐𝑜𝑟𝑟 and 𝑖𝑐𝑜𝑟𝑟 over the long term (20 
days). The results of the repeated tests showed decreased random uncertainties for long -term 
conditioning compared to short-term ones. 
The anodic Tafel slope in SPS decreased with the chloride concentration at 72 hours and reached 
a similar value (0.2 –0.3 V/dec) for all chloride concentrations at 20 days. In CSPS, the anodic 
\nPDF_PAGE 301\n 
261 
Tafel slope reached similar valu es for all chloride concentrations and drifted to a higher value 
(0.4–0.5 V/dec) at 20 days with the rust build-up. 
The cathodic Tafel slope |𝛽𝑐| tends to increase with chloride concentration in both SPS and CSPS 
but with a large variance for short -term conditioning. More stable and less scattered values were 
found at 20 days when the disruption of passive oxide film stabilized over time. In CSPS, |𝛽𝑐| 
increased with the chloride concentration (0.13 –0.55 V/dec). In SPS, it only increase d with 
chloride concentration up to 2% (wt.%) from 0.23 V/dec to 0.38 V/dec and then levelled off at 
higher chloride concentrations. 
10.2.1.3 Corrosion parameters for rebar in uncarbonated and carbonation mortars with 
various chloride contents 
For both non-carbonated and carbonated mortars, the corrosion potential  𝐸𝑐𝑜𝑟𝑟 decreasd to more 
negative values with the increase of chloride content. However, unlike SPS, in non-carbonated 
mortar samples, the corrosion potential (𝐸𝑐𝑜𝑟𝑟) decreased as the chloride concentration increased, 
without exhibiting a specific threshold chloride content in partially saturated conditions with 
chloride content up to 2.24% by weight of cement. However, under saturated conditions, a 
"threshold" chloride content (0.21% – 0.43% by weight of cement ) was observed, similar to the 
behaviour observed in SPS.  
In carbonated mortar samples, on the contrary, this chloride content threshold was observed under 
partially saturated conditions, but 𝐸𝑐𝑜𝑟𝑟 remained similar (around -550 mV – -600 mV) across all 
chloride levels under saturated conditions. Additionally, 𝐸𝑐𝑜𝑟𝑟 values in carbonated mortars were 
more noble compared to non-carbonated mortars, resembling the behaviour observed in SPS and 
CSPS. 
In drier conditions with lower chloride contents, the corrosion current densities 𝑖𝑐𝑜𝑟𝑟 in carbonated 
samples were considerably higher compared to non-carbonated samples. However, this difference 
was reduced with the increase in chloride content and relative humidity (RH). 
In non -carbonated mortar, 𝑖𝑐𝑜𝑟𝑟  increased with higher chloride content across all humidity 
conditions, which aligned with the inverse trend of 𝐸𝑐𝑜𝑟𝑟. However, in carbonated mortar under 
\nPDF_PAGE 302\n 
262 
drier conditions (RH 65-85%), the two-stage leveling off trend observed in 𝐸𝑐𝑜𝑟𝑟 did not manifest 
in 𝑖𝑐𝑜𝑟𝑟. Instead, 𝑖𝑐𝑜𝑟𝑟 continued to rise with larger chloride content (0.43% -2.24% by weight of 
cement), while 𝐸𝑐𝑜𝑟𝑟 plateaued. This suggests that factors beyond chloride depassivation, such as 
moisture content and resistivity, contributed to the corrosion current density with a lesser impact 
on 𝐸𝑐𝑜𝑟𝑟.  
In both non-carbonated and carbonated mortar, the anodic Tafel slopes (𝛽𝑎) did not exhibit a clear 
dependency on chloride content and conditioning relative humidity (RH), ranging from 0.3 –1.0 
V/dec. However, exceptionally high 𝛽𝑎 values were observed in non -carbonated mortar under 
submerged conditions with higher chloride content. 
In partially saturated conditions, the cathodic Tafel slope (|𝛽𝑐|) in non-carbonated mortar increased 
with chloride content and stabilized at higher chloride levels. Carbonated mortars had significantly 
larger |𝛽𝑐|  values (0.30 –0.55 V/dec) compared to no n-carbonated mortars (0.12 –0.30 V/dec), 
although no definitive trend was observed in carbonated mortar. In saturated conditions for both 
types of mortars, |𝛽𝑐| reached a peak value when the chloride content reached 0.43% by weight of 
cement and then decreased with higher chloride content. 
10.2.1.4 The interactive effect of moisture, chloride, and carbonation on rebar corrosion 
Carbonation and chloride content affect water content and resistivity in mortars. In carbonated 
mortars, higher chloride content led to increased water retention at a given RH and decreased 
resistivity at a given saturation level. However, the effect of chloride content on water retention 
and resistivity was insignificant in non-carbonated mortars. 
Water facilitated the depassivation of rebar and therefore affected 𝐸𝑐𝑜𝑟𝑟 in chloride-contaminated 
mortar in different ways for non -carbonated and carbonated mortar. In non -carbonated mortar, 
𝐸𝑐𝑜𝑟𝑟 decreased with water saturation when chloride content exceeded a critical value but was less 
affected at lower chloride levels. In carbonated mortars, the effect of saturation on 𝐸𝑐𝑜𝑟𝑟 was 
strongest without chloride and diminished with increasing chloride content. 
\nPDF_PAGE 303\n 
263 
Water saturation level affects corrosion rates due to three causes: 1) an area effect by the influenced 
rebar-pore solution area, 2) an ohmic control effect by the influenced bulk resistivity, and  3) an 
anode-to-cathode ratio effect.  
In non-carbonated mortar, when there is no chloride, the main contributor to rebar corrosion rate 
is the area effect. When there is chloride present, in addition to the area effect, there also exists an 
ohmic control effect and anode -to-cathode ratio. The chloride -induced rebar depassivation leads 
to non-uniform corrosion with a varying anode -to-cathode ratio dependent on water saturation. 
This non-uniform corrosion cell is at a scale that is under ohmic controlled by a varying bulk 
resistivity which is also dependent on water saturation. 
In carbonated mortar, the rebar corrosion behaviour is affected by both the anode-to-cathode ratio 
and the surface area. The rust scale on the rebar affects the anode-to-cathode ratio and is influenced 
by the moisture level. When the rust  is dry, it is in a passivation state, and when it is wet, it is in 
an active state, which results in the dependence of the corrosion potential on the water saturation 
level in carbonated mortar. This dependence is more pronounced when the chloride content is low, 
as the conductivity of the rust is highly dependent on water content, and less significant when the 
chloride content is high. The non -uniform corrosion cell occurs on a scale that is not sensitive to 
bulk resistivity, hence less ohmic controlled. 
10.2.2 Homogenized rebar corrosion parameters in concrete as a function of the variable 
microenvironment 
A circuit model was created to simulate the potentiodynamic scan of non -uniform reinforced 
concrete, representing the overall polarization behaviour. 
It was found that the area -averaged localized potential (homogenized potential) of the rebar was 
equivalent to the IR-compensated potential measured from the concrete cover. However, the non-
uniform local potential on the active and passivated rebar units (rebar -concrete interface at 
mesoscopic-macroscopic level) and electrical resistance between them may lead to a distorted scan 
curve. When fitted to the Butler -Volmer equation, it exhibit ed larger Tafel constants and an 
overestimation of corrosion current density. This error was more pronounced when the active and 
passivated rebar unit areas were comparable and the resistance between them was large. Therefore, 
\nPDF_PAGE 304\n 
264 
corrosion parameters should be measured on a rebar -concrete interface with a uniform condition 
at mesoscopic–macroscopic scale.  
The parametric study performed using  this model showed that the homogenized corrosion 
parameters were functions of concrete resistance and the area ratio of the active or passive rebar -
concrete surface area. Therefore, the homogenize d corrosion parameters should be a function of 
the microenvironments around the rebar. 
The findings suggested that the homogenized corrosion parameters should be measured in the 
mortar samples used in the experimental studies that are uniform at a mesoscop ic-macroscopic 
level, and therefore samples were treated with various levels of chloride contents, carbonation 
states, and water saturation. The homogenized corrosion parameters as a function of the 
microenvironment should be empirically derived from the test data from experiments similar t o 
those carried out in this project. 
A numerical homogenized model was also developed to establish a mechanistic link between the 
corrosion parameters observed in bulk solution tests and those in mortar. The model quantitatively 
determined the non-uniform distributions of corrosion-affecting factors, such as water content and 
chloride content, at the pore level.  
The pore size distribution was quantified by an improved adsorption-condensation model with the 
water retention curve. The distribution of water a t the pore level was determined from the pore 
size distribution and total saturation level. The chloride concentration distribution at the pore level 
for a given total chloride content was then determined accounting for the bonding effect and 
chloride concentrate effect due to the electrical double layer. The saturation state and chloride 
concentration in pores determine d the allocation of the area of active and passive rebar -solution 
interface in a representative elementary volume ( REV) of 1 𝑚𝑚3. A circuit model was then 
employed to simulate the homogenized corrosion parameters of the rebar -concrete interface in 
REV from corrosion parameters of rebar -solution interface at the pore level. The simulated 
corrosion parameters capture d the observed pattern fro m the empirical functions from the 
experimental data. 
\nPDF_PAGE 305\n 
265 
10.2.3 Simulation of the evolution microenvironment at the rebar depth of a concrete 
structure in service conditions. 
A comprehensive 3-D transport model was developed to simulate the microenvironment evolution 
at the rebar depth of a concrete structure in its service conditions. A series of diffusion-advection-
reaction transport models were used to simulate the mass transport of water, chloride ions, and 
carbon dioxide gas.  
An optimization routine was developed to form a smooth equivalent pressure head boundary 
condition to replace the spiky boundary conditions with discrete rain incidence with acceptable 
accuracy. 
This model was applied to an uncracked RC structure — a one-century-old concrete arch bri dge 
subjected to service conditions , including s ources of chloride, carbonation, varying air relative 
humidity, and exposure to unevenly sheltered run -off water. Historical weather data and field 
monitoring data were used to formulate realistic boundary co nditions during wetting and drying 
scenarios. Laboratory test results from site-extracted concrete and rebar provided corrosion 
kinetics parameters for the old rebar that was different from modern rebar. 
The study also examined the impact of various climate scenarios on rebar section loss in arches 
over a 50 -year projected timeframe. It was observed that elevated CO2  levels and increased 
precipitation led to a significant increase in rebar section loss, ex acerbating overall corrosion 
deterioration. Conversely, elevated CO2 levels with decreased precipitation suppressed high rebar 
section loss but did not have a significant effect on overall corrosion deterioration. Based on these 
findings, different maintenance and management approaches for infrastructure were recommended. 
10.2.4 Validation of comprehensive corrosion model 
A comprehensive corrosion model was developed to solve for the potential and current field in the 
concrete domain and on the rebar surface for cracked reinforced concrete. It used the homogenized 
corrosion kinetics as functions of the microenvironmental conditions on the rebar -concrete 
interface which changed with the evolution of moisture and chloride content on embedded rebars 
in cracked concrete.  
\nPDF_PAGE 306\n 
266 
The segmented beam sample was tested in disconnected mode and connected mode in wetting and 
drying cycles with chloride salt solution.  
In disconnected mode, the corrosion potential and corrosion current density for each segment were 
measured. In connected mode, the rebar electrode potential and total net current leaving or entering 
each rebar were measured. The 3 -D numerical model representing the same experimental setup 
was validated by comparing the experimental results and the simulated results extr acted from the 
model.   
10.2.5 Numerical investigation of effects of crack on rebar corrosion in concrete 
A 2 -D model was utilized to investigate the impact of cracks on the microenvironment and 
corrosion of rebar in cracked concrete under submerged condition and wetting and drying cycles. 
It was used to explain the complex effect of cracks on corrosion, which includes corrosion 
reduction over time and the effect of crack size and crack healing. 
10.2.5.1 Submerged condition 
Cracks affected corrosion by affecting the diffusion rate of chloride ions through the crack. Thinner 
cracks resulted in reduced chloride influx, leading to lower peak corrosion current and slower 
reduction of corrosion rate over time. This led to more uniform micro -cell corrosion. Eventually, 
corrosion reached a similar level when the entire rebar was contaminated with chloride, and the 
corrosion rate was limited by oxygen diffusion. 
10.2.5.2 Wetting and drying condition 
The model suggest ed that the corrosion kinetics of rebar in the cracking zone were affected by 
chloride and moisture, with the possibility of rebar acting as a c athode during drying cycles. The 
average corrosion rate in the cracking zone peak ed during wetting cycles and decrease d during 
subsequent drying cycles. The peak values decreased with repeated wetting and drying cycles. The 
final corrosion rate was determined by the chloride -contaminated area on the rebar and reduced 
oxygen concentration. Micro -cell corrosion eventually bec ame the primary contributor to rebar 
corrosion in the cracking zone. 
\nPDF_PAGE 307\n 
267 
10.2.5.3 Crack size 
In wetting and drying scenarios, the simulation results demonstrated that the crack opening size 
had a diminished impact on chloride influx through the crack, unlike the submerged case. Based 
on the extensive numerical investigation , among all parameters that are known to control the 
corrosion rate, self -healing, which takes place in thin cracks, was the single parameter than can 
explain the stark difference in corrosion behaviour between thin cracks and thick cracks in the 
splash zone (wetting and drying cycles). To capture the effect of crack size on corrosion, the model 
was improved to incorporate crack healing to realistically represent the long -term behaviour of 
concrete structures in the presence of thin cracks . Crack healing allow ed the crack to heal when 
the opening was smaller than a critical "healable size," resulting in decreased transport properties 
over time. Crack healing reduced future chloride transport, diluted high chloride content near the 
crack, and limit ed oxygen diffusion. Less permeable concrete such as HPC experience d greater 
chloride content reduction due to crack healing. Crack healing accelerate d the reduction of 
corrosion rate over wetting and drying cycles. Finer cracks had a greater healable crack length, 
resulting in more recovered concrete cover for the rebar at the crack. 
10.2.5.4 Implication on critical crack width 
The critical crack width should be small enough so that the crack width at the rebar depth is smaller 
than the maximum healable crack width. This allows the crack healing process to recover some of 
the concrete cover at the crack. The critical crack width should be further minimized to a standard 
that the corrosion current density  in the cracking area is significantly reduced, potentially to a 
negligible value. The resultant corrosion rate depends on factors su ch as the existing corrosive 
microenvironment, continuous exposure conditions, concrete properties, and corrosion kinetics. 
The developed comprehensive corrosion model can be used to determine the uncertain critical 
crack width for given reinforced concrete configuration and its service environment. 
\nPDF_PAGE 308\n 
268 
10.3 Recommendations for future research 
10.3.1 Effect of temperature on corrosion parameters 
More work should be done to investigate the effect of temperature on corrosion parameters. The 
temperature dependence of the chem ical reaction rate is usually described by the well -known 
Arrhenius equation. For rebar corrosion in concrete, however, there is no direct pure Arrhenius 
trend because the corrosion rate is affected by multiple factors, pH, chloride, moisture content, 
resistivity, temperature, etc. and the change in temperature will also affect other important factors 
such as resistivity. However, there are numerical models  that take into account the effect of 
temperature. For example, in the numerical model feathering disc rete separated anodic and 
cathodic sites (Pour-Ghaz et al., 2009a) , the Arrhenius type of dependency on temperature has 
been reflected on the exchang e current density of the two half -cell reactions  ( Fe2+/Fe and 
H2O/OH−  ), and another major influencing factor is the resistance change due to temperature 
between the separated anode and cathode. Although the effect of resistivity is debatable when the 
distribution of anodic and cathodic sites is unclear, the effect from th e resistance can be tuned by 
the anodic/cathodic area ratio, a user-input in the macro-cell corrosion model, which is effectively 
a calibration factor. It is worth mentioning that since resistivity is related to the water content, 
mathematically, one can c orrelate either one of them to the experimental data regardless of the 
physical mechanism. A similar model was also developed  by Yu et al. (2017), and corrosion rate 
predictions from both models are reasonably comparable to the experimental result with v arying 
temperature and ambient relative humidity (Pour-Ghaz et al., 2009b; Yu et al., 2017).   
Nevertheless, with the developed numerical homogenized model that links the rebar -solution 
interface corrosion behaviour from the pore level to the rebar -concrete in a REV, it is promising 
to simulate the effect of temperature on the rebar corrosion in concrete without the anode and 
cathode area as a user’s input. In the homogenized model, besides the dependency of exchanging 
current density on temperature, temp erature would also affect water distribution (liquid volume 
fracture and continuity), resistivity, and chloride binding isotherm. 
\nPDF_PAGE 309\n 
269 
10.3.2 Enhancing interpretation of half-cell potential mapping results 
A regular typical assessment of an RC structural member involv es tests of cover thickness, 
delamination, carbonation depth, chloride content at various depths, compressive strength of 
concrete cores, half-cell potential mapping, and occasionally corrosion current density etc. Among 
those acquired data, the most acces sible result is the half -cell potential. The interpretation of the 
half-cell mapping results can be improved. For example, in a bridge deck inspection, the half-cell 
test is a routine task, and a single criterion is applied to interpret the data (ASTM C876 - 15. 
G01.14). Since the homogenized corrosion potential depends on the moisture content, the potential 
field formed by the coupling of rebar mesh in the deck also depends on the moisture and geometry 
of rebar and concrete cover. The measured half-cell (potential on the deck surface) is affected by 
moisture and geometry. Therefore, moisture-and-geometry adjusted criteria should be used. 
10.3.3 Dynamic link between corrosion and concrete fracture models 
The traditional limit-state for the durability design for RC structure is the onset of active corrosion. 
It is not uncommon to have existing aged structures in service that already well pass this corrosion-
initiation period. These structures are operating at their active corr osion stage  and often have 
visible corrosion and corrosion -induced cracking and delamination. To improve the service life 
prediction for reinforced concrete structures, it is essential to establish a dynamic link between the 
corrosion model and a concrete fracture model. This linkage ensures that corrosion -induced 
cracking and propagation are considered and the change in the crack property can affect the 
corrosion. 
\nPDF_PAGE 310\n 
270
